THE cool wind that comes up about dawn waked Mrs.
Meigs. She pulled up an extra blanket, and sank back
luxuriously on her high pillow. She wouldn't go to sleep
again, very likely. At her age people weren't supposed to
need much sleep; but she preferred to believe that she
had always been a light sleeper. Whatever you did when
you were old, people always accounted for on the ground
of your age. It never seemed to occur to them that you
still did things because you were you.
She turned her head to glance at the heavy onyx-and-
gilt clock on her high walnut chiffonier. Her husband
had given her that clock on her twenty-fifth wedding
anniversary. In all the years since, it had never been
within half an hour of right. If it were moved in dust-
ing, it stopped entirely; and somehow Anna always did
manage to move it when she dusted. Anna was as good
as any girl you could get nowadays, but that wasn't say-
ing much.
Mrs. Meigs always liked to see the friendly face of the
clock at this early hour, even if she couldn't trust what
it told her. Beside it hung a family group taken when
all her children were small, with Jack a baby sitting on
his father's knee, and the girls like steps leading up totall Millicent, with her knobby knees showing under a
plaid wool frock. There were a dozen pictures of the
grandchildren in the room, from their baby days on to
Cecile in her début gown and Richard in his first dress-
suit.
It was growing lighter now. There'd be sunshine in
the front bedroom. Although the second bedroom opened
off a court, Mrs. Meigs used it herself, because it was
larger. At that it was crowded, with her big double
bed, and her chiffonier, and a walnut wardrobe spacious
enough to have held all Bluebeard's wives. The people
who planned flats expected you to curl up like a dog.
She heard the radiator begin to pound. The steam was
coming on. She had spoken to the janitor three times
about that radiator. The more it was repaired, the more
noise it made.
As it pounded, she must have dozed. The next thing
she knew, it was broad daylight, and the hands of the
clock pointed to eight. Mrs. Meigs got out of bed with
guilty haste. Only the day before she'd been pointing
out to Anna that breakfast at eight meant eight, not a
quarter past. Getting breakfast over early gave you a
good start on the day.
She put on her shabby dressing-gown and well-worn
slippers. The heavy dining-table was pushed up near the
windows, so that she got the morning sun on her back.
Mrs. Meigs propped a book open with the sugar-bowl.
One advantage of living alone was that she was free to
read at the table. Of course it prolonged the meal unduly,
so that breakfast had a tendency to run into lunch, and
lunch into dinner. But Mrs. Meigs had kept her appetite
as well as her eyesight. There were times when she was
not so sure about her hearing. But she couldn't be ex-pected to hear when other people mumbled. They
mumbled a good deal nowadays. They mumbled and they
shambled, and they didn't like to be told about it.
Anna brought in the coffee-pot and connected the
electric toaster, which had been a Christmas present from
Ralph, the son-in-law who was an electrical jobber. Mrs.
Meigs laid a slice of bread on the toaster. "Good morning,
Anna," she said.
Anna grinned from ear to ear. She was a big raw-
boned girl in her early thirties. She always waited for
Mrs. Meigs to say "Good morning." Then etiquette was
over for the day.
"Good morning, Mis' Meigs," she returned. "You're
looking fine this morning."
"Thank you," said Mrs. Meigs. "It's a fine morning."
"Grand," agreed Anna. "It's warm, too, for October.
I was reading in a magazine last night about Indian
summer. And to-day we get it."
"This isn't Indian summer," objected Mrs. Meigs.
"Ain't it?" said Anna indulgently. "Well, the maga-
zine said it was."
"I don't doubt that," said Mrs. Meigs. "You can learn
a surprising amount from the magazines, Anna. But most
of it is wrong.”
"Well, the magazine said-" insisted Anna.
"Indian summer," Mrs. Meigs interrupted firmly,
"comes much later in the year than this. It comes along
in November, after we've had a hard frost. This is simply
a fine fall day."
"It's that all right," admitted Anna. "Makes you glad
you're alive."
Mrs. Meigs looked hard at her. She herself was glad
she was alive. But Anna, doing housework for a living,and reading misleading magazines for pleasure-what
did Anna get out of life?
"What did your story say about Indian summer?" she
asked.
"Well, there was an old bachelor fifty, and a widow
forty-eight, and she thought they was too old for love,
but he said No, this wasn't the winter of their days, it
was just Indian summer."
"Indian summer? At forty-eight?" echoed Mrs. Meigs
incredulously.
Anna grinned. "I suppose to you that don't seem very
old."
"I shall be eighty my next birthday," said Mrs. Meigs
firmly. "By the way, my birthday comes next week."
"Yes'm. I suppose you'll be having a party, the same
as usual."
"I suppose I will," began Mrs. Meigs, with a strange
lack of enthusiasm. A curl of black smoke ascended from
the toaster. "There!" said Mrs. Meigs. "I've burned my
toast."
She discarded the burned slice, and put on a fresh one
to toast. "That toaster toasts awful rash," remarked
Anna.
"I was thinking about my birthday party," said Mrs.
Meigs. "It's an idiotic custom to celebrate birthdays."
"I think it's kind of nice," said Anna. "My last birth-
day, when you gave me the afternoon off, and five dollars
to take a girl friend to the movies and out to supper,
I thought that was pretty nice. Not that the supper we
got downtown was anything extra. But it's a treat to me
to eat a meal that I don't have to cook myself."
"Perhaps you're right," said Mrs. Meigs. "It isn't cele-
brating birthdays that's an idiotic custom. It's gather-ing in all your relatives and calling it a celebration. Now
if I could just ask three people in for a game of bridge"
"Well, why not?" asked Anna. "Your old lady bridg-
ers certainly have a good time when they come here."
"My children expect a family dinner on my birthday.
I don't quite remember how we got into the habit. Still,
it is nice to have them all around my table again."
"It's all right, sure," agreed Anna. "But why don't
you have the old ladies too? Have 'em before you have
the others? They ain't been here in nearly a month."
"We'll do that. Some day when it's fine. Poor Miss
Lamb can't get out when it rains, on account of her
rheumatism."
"You won't find a better day than to-day," said Anna.
"You think we could do it to-day?" asked Mrs. Meigs
eagerly. "You wouldn't mind the extra work?"
"Pshaw! Your old ladies ain't a mite of trouble, Mis'
Meigs. And you don't eat any supper after they've been
here, so it evens up the work."
"I never have room for any supper," said Mrs. Meigs.
"All right, I'll telephone and see if they can come. They
don't have many engagements, poor old things."
Again a curl of smoke ascended from the toaster. Mrs.
Meigs gave a sniff of disgust. "Two of us right here in
the room, and that's the second slice of toast I've
burned up!"
"Those new-fangled toasters burn the bread easier
than they toast it," said Anna.
Mrs. Meigs wouldn't have said that herself. When
you're crowding eighty, you don't dare to talk like an
old-timer. But she was grateful to Anna for having said
it for her.
"Light the gas-oven, and make me two slices of goodtoast, will you?" she demanded. "Stay right there till it's
finished. If I sit here and burn up bread all morning,
there won't be any party this afternoon."
Anna retreated to the kitchen. Mrs. Meigs sipped her
coffee, and felt the sunshine warm her back. It was a
beautiful morning. The coffee was hot and strong and
sweet. It was awfully nice of Anna to suggest having
company this afternoon, because of course it did make
more work for her. She was a good girl, even if she didn't
see dust.
She was a good cook, too. She came back with two
perfect slices of toast, the middles spongy with melted
butter. "Do you want toast for your old ladies this after-
noon?" she asked.
Mrs. Meigs really shouldn't allow her servant to allude
to her guests as "your old ladies." Still, a person couldn't
always be noticing everything. "Toast and coffee," she
agreed. "Strawberry jam. And you might order some
little cakes from Snaveley's."
"I'll have time to make some cakes myself," said Anna,
"if I just give the dusting a lick and a promise."
"A lick and a promise is right," said Mrs. Meigs. "But
there! We'll be having fall housecleaning soon, I suppose."
"Yes'm. Maybe when we do, you'd better sleep in the
spare room, so that I can take two days to your room.
There's so many things in it, it's a big job."
"A big job? You've never done housecleaning in a big
house," said Mrs. Meigs.
"No'm. I always worked in flats."
"The house I lived in before I moved here," said Mrs.
Meigs, biting a crust and then adding more butter, "was
a real house.""One of those big places on Cass Street, wasn't it? It's
been torn down since."
Mrs. Meigs gave a little shiver. "Yes. It was torn down
when I sold it. But that was a house that was a house."
"You'd lived there a long time?" asked Anna, produc-
ing a dust-cloth and standing with it in her hand. Anna
always counted the time as dusting, if she stood with
the dust-cloth in her hand.
"I went there as a bride," said Mrs. Meigs. "All my
children were born there. And my husband died there."
"Land sakes!" said Anna. "You needed a big house,
too, for your big family."
"Each of my children had a separate bedroom," said
Mrs. Meigs. "I've never held with making sisters room
together. At that, we had two spare bedrooms. Down-
stairs we had a front parlor and a back parlor, and a
library, and a little room we called the study, but it was
really for Mr. Meigs's guests to smoke their cigars in. I
never could stand the smell of stale cigar smoke in my
lace curtains."
"Did you have lace curtains there, Mis' Meigs?"
"Lace curtains to the floor, and carpets from wall to
wall. That house had fourteen-foot ceilings, Anna. There
was room to breathe in it; room to bring up children,
and room to entertain."
"You must have hated to leave it."
"Hated to leave? There have been deaths in the family
that I felt less. Of course, it was the sensible thing to do.
The house was too expensive to keep up, and the neigh-
borhood had run down. I was an old woman, all alone
in the world. It's just as well I was alone, too, those first
dreadful weeks I lived here.""You must ha' been lonesome at first."
"I couldn't find a place to put anything. It seemed to
me as if the walls were closing in on me and smothering
me. I used to roam around at night when I was alone
here, and cry all by myself, and think, Oh, I want to go
upstairs! I want the attic where my children used to
play! I want a fruit cellar and a sewing-room! I want to
go home!'"
Her spoon rattled against her cup. There was a salty
taste in her throat.
"But you got used to it here," suggested Anna.
Mrs. Meigs sat erect again. For a minute she had for-
gotten Anna; she had been living in the sense of her past
sorrows. That would never do. To go making scenes be-
fore servants, and saying out loud things that she'd hardly
liked to admit to herself!
"That was six years ago," she said crisply. "I've got
used to this flat long ago. When you're dusting the living-
room, Anna, I wish you'd take down that cobweb in the
front corner of the ceiling. I'm not fussy, I hope; but
cobwebs are a little too much.”
Anna departed. Mrs. Meigs finished her coffee and
toast, and went to the telephone. She called Miss Lamb
first. That was a lengthy business. Poor Miss Lamb lived
in the Old Ladies' Home, and the matron had to take the
message, and go hunt for Miss Lamb. Word finally came
through, that Miss Lamb would come.
Mrs. Cronkhite lived in a boarding-house: cheerless
enough, but she could at least say her soul was her own.
Mrs. Cronkhite hesitated; she always liked to pretend
that she was rushed with invitations. But she couldn't
keep the eagerness out of her voice as she asked, "What
time?"II
"Half past two," said Mrs. Meigs. "That will give us a
nice long afternoon to play."
"Get on your playing clothes," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
They both chuckled. Mrs. Cronkhite took her bridge
seriously. She even tried to enforce penalties.
That left only Mrs. Bishop to call up. Mrs. Bishop lived
just across the hall. It had been partly on her account,
indeed, that Mrs. Meigs moved into this building. Mrs.
Meigs could have stepped across the hall and rung the
door-bell more easily than she could argue with the
operator about whether that telephone number ended
with a one or with a nine. "Nin-n-ne!" the operator
would say, as if she were accusing a person of being
deaf.
But Mrs. Bishop lived with her married daughter. In
fact Mrs. Bishop's name wasn't on the mail-box at all.
It just said "Whitney." The daughter answered the door,
and supervised her mother's visits with friends. But she
would call her mother to the telephone; that is, she would
if you insisted. Mrs. Meigs insisted.
Mrs. Bishop's voice was thin and quavery. It went up
half a note with excitement when she heard the invi-
tation.
"Half past two?" she quavered. "Can't we come at
two? We'll have longer to play."
It was all settled, then. They were so pleased to come,
she might have asked them oftener. She was the only one
of the four who really had a home to ask people to. But
no, perhaps it was better not to ask them too often. Jam
is a treat; but a steady diet of jam wouldn't be good for
old ladies.
Speaking of jam made her remember. Mrs. Meigs went
out to the kitchen, and opened the corner cupboard. Thetop shelf was a poor substitute for a fruit cellar; but it
was set forth richly with shining jars, each with its white
paper top and neat label, written in Mrs. Meigs's rather
sprawling backhand.
It was strawberry jam for which Mrs. Meigs was fa-
mous. She had always made it by her mother's recipe.
Her oldest daughter Millicent had lately begun to use
commercial pectin in her strawberry jam, and had tried
to get her mother to do likewise.
"You don't have to cook it so long, and it keeps more
of the taste of the strawberries," she urged.
"Who wants to keep the taste of the strawberries? I
prefer jam to taste like jam," said Mrs. Meigs. Millicent
often had that effect on her.
She got down a glass of strawberry jam. Then, still
standing on a kitchen chair to reach, she hesitated a mo-
ment. Anna caught her there.
"You'd ought to let me do the climbing, Mis' Meigs,"
she said.
"I'd like your advice," said Mrs. Meigs. "Here's the
strawberry jam to go with the toast. I was wondering,
shall I take down a glass of raspberry, and one of quince,
and one of damson plums, and wrap the three of them
up together for a prize?"
"You got to have a prize!" said Anna.
"But not that one?" Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I guess
you're right. It would seem too much like bragging about
our jam. What would you suggest for a prize, Anna?"
Anna was occupied in helping her mistress safely back
to the floor. When that was accomplished, she gave the
matter her full attention. "I think a big box of candy
is as nice a prize as anything."
"I think it is too," said Mrs. Meigs. "And so awfullyunsuitable for old ladies, who ought to be careful of their
digestions. Very well, Anna, I'll go out and get it be-
fore they come."
Mrs. Meigs went back to the dining-room. From a
lower shelf of the corner cupboard she began to take
down cups and plates that belonged to her second-best
set. On the upper shelves her wedding china stood ranged
in tall piles, twenty-four of everything. Though that was
the set Cecile called "sacred," it wouldn't have been too
good for this party. But once when she had been using
dishes from the best set, Mrs. Bishop had broken a cup,
and had spent two frantic days trying to match it. As if
those dishes could have been matched for the last fifty
years! Mrs. Meigs had forgotten to regret the irreplaceable
cup, she'd been so sorry for Mrs. Bishop. She wasn't going
to have that kind of thing spoiling the party to-day.
When she had counted out the dishes she went on to
the living-room, and turned her hardy chrysanthemum
and rose-geranium plants so that they'd get the sun-
light on the other side, and snipped off a dead leaf. Be-
tween the plants her eye was caught by a small boy rid-
ing his velocipede on the sidewalk across the street.
He saw her smiling down at him, and rode very fast,
to show her how good a rider he was. The clerk from the
drug-store on the corner came out to put down his
awning. He looked up too, and waved a hand.
Mrs. Meigs had plenty to attend to to-day, with a party
and all. The morning was creeping on, and she wasn't
even dressed. But she caught sight of the mail-carrier
up the block, and lingered to see whether he would turn
into their building. Not that it necessarily meant a letter
for her if he did: there were eight flats in the building.
He turned in, but he didn't ring her bell. Mrs. An-drews from down the block came past, wheeling her
baby-carriage. She looked up and smiled, too. Her baby
wasn't doing so well. Didn't relish its food; and you
couldn't wonder at that, when you saw the way they
diluted its bottle and put in "cultures" and lime-water
and burned flour, trying to make out that milk wasn't
milk. Mrs. Andrews looked more cheerful to-day, though.
The baby must be doing better.
The street was very bright in the sunshine. A neigh-
borly street, and nice and close to downtown, so that a
lot of people went by every day. The garage across the
street was noisy, but it was cheerful too. It had been
interesting all summer to watch the license plates of the
out-of-town cars that stopped there. She'd caught one as
far east as Maine, and three from California. This street
was a part of the Yellowstone Trail. It was more than a
thousand miles from the Yellowstone; but if you kept
on going, it was right on the way.
At length Mrs. Meigs turned away from the window.
As she did so, the cobweb she had mentioned to Anna
caught her eye. It was still triumphantly in place.
But when she went back to the kitchen, it was not to
scold about cobwebs. "Anna," she said, "this party this
afternoon! Of course a person can give a party without
any excuse. But I always feel that I like to have one. In
fact, my husband used to tease me about the excuses I
found for giving parties."
"This can be an extra birthday party," suggested Anna.
She had finished the breakfast dishes, and was sifting
flour and sugar into a mixing bowl.
"No, it can't. Celebrating birthdays is a silly custom,
as I said before. It's sillier still if you don't celebrate on
your birthday.""Then have you thought up a better excuse?" asked
Anna with her broadest grin.
"I certainly have. But I'm ashamed of myself that I
didn't use up this excuse long ago. I've lived here nearly
six years, Anna."
"Yes'm. So you was telling me this morning."
"And I've just realized that I like this place."
"You like a flat after all, Mis' Meigs?"
"I like this flat. I like this street. And I like being so
close to Mrs. Bishop, and having a home of my own,
which is something not everybody has at my age."
"Then you're giving a party to celebrate that you've
been here six years?"
"I'm giving a housewarming, Anna. A housewarm-
ing! And if you ask me, it's about time I gave one."II
MRS. MEIGS took her book with her into her bath,
where with the ease born of long practice she propped
it behind the faucets. The far-sightedness of age has its
compensations. Millicent said that reading in the bathtub
was absurd, and bathing directly after breakfast was
unhealthful, and lying too long in hot water made people
catch cold. Millicent was Mrs. Meigs's eldest daughter.
She had been married thirty years, but that did not pre-
vent her having her mother on her mind. Mrs. Meigs was
not especially grateful for being kept on Millicent's
mind. It was the sort of mind that is full of knobs.
The book was a novel belonging to the circulating
library in the corner drug-store. It had an orange cover,
with the title lettered in scarlet, "Bad Women." When
she started to draw it out, the clerk had said in a shocked
tone, "Oh, I don't think you'll care for that, Mrs. Meigs!
It isn't at all the sort of book I'd give my mother!"
After that, of course she had to take it. Last night she
had discovered to her disgust that the heroine, though
advertised on the cover as "bad," was at bottom a noble
girl traduced by others. Despite her cocktails and her
language, she reminded Mrs. Meigs of the heroines in
the cook's story-paper, which as a child Mrs. Meigs used
to filch regularly from the drawer in the kitchen table.
It was what her grandson called "a gyp." But she
might as well read on to the end, and find out just howbadly she had been gypped. Mrs. Meigs turned in more
hot water, and read on.
She was still reading when she heard the door-bell
ring, and Anna go to answer. It was just her luck, to have
Millicent catch her like this! Mrs. Meigs got out of the
tub so hastily that the pages of "Bad Women" were be-
dewed with soapy water.
A voice called cheerfully from the living-room, "Oh,
Caroline! Where are you?"
Mrs. Meigs's expression of guilt changed to a beam-
ing smile. This wasn't Millicent after all. It was Cecile,
Millicent's daughter. Cecile was only nineteen, but Mrs.
Meigs felt more sense of kinship with Cecile than with
her own children. She and Cecile always laughed at the
same things.
"Still dressing!" Mrs. Meigs called through the bath-
room door.
"Well, hurry up!" said Cecile. "I've brought a man
to call on you."
Mrs. Meigs could hear Cecile talking, and a man's voice
answering. She buttoned her neat percale morning dress,
and glanced into the mirror to be sure her hair
was smooth. Cecile was taking a year off between high
school and college; half a dozen boys of her own age
were keeping her from finding the time heavy on her
hands.
Cecile was bending over the dining-room table, ar-
ranging a bowlful of pink roses. She looked like a pink
rose herself, even in her shapeless tweeds and abominable
flat shoes. She was a tall girl, as all girls were nowadays;
and her hug lifted Mrs. Meigs off her feet.
"Pretty frisky this morning, Caroline?" she suggested.
"Look at the roses the boy friend brought you.""Lovely!" said Mrs. Meigs. "Aren't roses hard to get
in October?"
"They are. But nothing less would satisfy my extrava-
gant young friend."
Mrs. Meigs glanced around to reprove the young
friend's extravagance. Her glance froze as it fell. It was
mere flattery to call this man young. His waistcoat
bulged, and his hair was brushed up from the sides in an
attempt to conceal the shining baldness of the crown.
He was very well cared for, and he looked prosperous.
Mrs. Meigs had no objection to a man's being prosperous,
but she hated to see him look it.
"This is my friend Roger Kane," explained Cecile.
"He's driving me out to lunch at the Country Club, after
I have my golf practice. It's such a beautiful day, I
thought you might like to come along."
"No, thank you," said Mrs. Meigs. "I have an engage-
ment of my own this afternoon."
"We'd get you back in time for an afternoon engage-
ment," urged Cecile.
"My bridgers are coming here for a game. You know
how early they show up."
"I know." Cecile laughed. "I think they sit up all the
night before, so as to get a good start. Perhaps we'd
better try taking you another day, later in the week. I
shall be going out for practice every day now. I've quali-
fied for a State post-season match next week. It's a new
match, and all eyes are on it."
"She stands an excellent chance of winning the match,
too," Roger Kane struck in.
"Yes, I do, if just the right people break their legs
before next week," said Cecile."You're too modest," said Roger. He included Mrs.
Meigs in his smile. "Not every girl with such an im-
portant match creeping up on her would be thinking
of taking her grandmother for a drive."
Mrs. Meigs favored him with a cool glance. He liked
himself so well, nobody else needed to like him. Anyhow
she had never cared for bald-headed men; she used to
tell her husband she thought baldness should be grounds
for divorce.
"You needn't explain Cecile to me," she said crisply.
"And you needn't use that ancestral word 'grand-
mother. "
دو
Roger Kane looked discomfited, and Cecile laughed.
"Call her 'Caroline," she advised. "I always do. What
are you going to wear for your party this afternoon,
Caroline?"
"It doesn't make much difference," said Mrs. Meigs.
"All my clothes look alike anyhow. I've always worn
mourning since your grandfather died, and I expect to
keep right on wearing it."
"I hope you will. It's just the thing for you, somehow.
Makes you look like a grandmother in a picture. By the
way, Caroline, why don't you take to lace caps? You
would be too sweet for words in a pretty lace сар."
"But in my day," protested Mrs. Meigs, "only the
really old ladies wore caps."
Cecile's eyes twinkled. But it was Mrs. Meigs who
laughed outright. "There, my dear, I had forgotten
again! It's no use. I keep forgetting. I shall be eighty
next week. But somehow when it is you yourself who
are eighty, it isn't quite the same."
Her words must have struck the girl as pathetic, forCecile at once became offhand. Cecile always became off-
hand when she was touched. "Let me see your dress, any-
how. I want to be sure it's properly fresh."
She finished the roses, and set them back out of harm's
way. Cecile could arrange flowers like a professional. She
could cook, sew, run a typewriter, drive a car. Her danc-
ing was as good as her golf, and her golf was making
her prominent. But there was a queer restlessness about
her, as if she hadn't quite found herself. Mrs. Meigs might
be prejudiced, but she thought Cecile a person who would
be well worth finding.
"You'll have time for a cigarette, Roger," said Cecile.
She led Mrs. Meigs into her bedroom. From the ward-
robe Mrs. Meigs produced her best dull black silk. Cecile
at once condemned the ruching in the neck and sleeves.
Scissors in hand, she established herself on the edge of
the high black-walnut bed.
"Are you going to keep your friend waiting while
you sew in new ruching for me?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"If he doesn't like the way I treat him, he knows what
he can do about it," said Cecile indifferently.
"Where did you meet this Kane person?" asked Mrs.
Meigs.
"At Aunt Edith's. Edith always entertains the impor-
tant people in town, you know."
"The town is running down since my day," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"Roger owns a couple of the new iron foundries and
things. He's better worth my time than the boys who
always have to ask Dad whether they can have the car
to-night. He likes to spend his money, too."
It was kind of him to buy those roses for an old lady.Mrs. Meigs tried to be just. "What does your mother
think of him?"
"He's been sending me flowers every morning. Yester-
day he couldn't go along for my practice, so he had his
chauffeur call for me anyhow. His car is a Hispano-
Suiza. What would Mother think of him, after all that?
She thinks he's grand."
"Well, you'll try your luck with a lot of men, I sup-
pose, before the right one comes along," said Mrs. Meigs.
"And you've still four years of college ahead of you."
"I wonder, have I?" Cecile let her sewing fall in her
lap. A line of worry appeared between her smooth eye-
brows. "There's Dick in the U now, spending money
like water, and Dad's business going from bad to worse."
"Business has its ups and downs, Cecile. But I guess
among us we can scrape together money for your educa-
tion."
Cecile jumped up to throw an arm around her grand-
mother. "Caroline, you're such a darling! You believe in
education. You believe in the right man' coming along.
You believe in doing good to your neighbors”
"I do not," said Mrs. Meigs firmly. "I did once. But
many years ago I found out that your neighbor's idea of
what he wants done to him may not be at all like what
you think he ought to have done to him."
Cecile laughed outright. Mrs. Meigs laughed too.
She had made Cecile's first short dresses, and now
Cecile was sewing in a ruching for her. If you liked
people, you were willing to take favors from them. "I
want you to pin a fresh veil on my bonnet for me," said
Mrs. Meigs. "But not to-day. My birthday, maybe. You
coming to my birthday party?""Surest thing you know," said Cecile. "I'll send Dick a
card to remind him, too."
"Dick won't want to drive in from Madison just for a
family dinner," said Mrs. Meigs.
"One of your dinners is worth driving eighty miles
for," returned Cecile, shaking out the dress. "Here, this
is finished. Do I smell something good this minute?"
"Cakes for my friends this afternoon," said Mrs.
Meigs.
Cecile went to the kitchen; when she rejoined Roger
she was munching a little cake, and had brought along
another for him. "Bad for your waist-line," she said.
"Tastes good, though, doesn't it?"
Stuffing cake into her mouth, and grinning at him
around it, she didn't look more than ten years old. Roger
beamed at her, though she had spoiled his appetite by that
hint about his waist-line.
"He really admires her!" thought Mrs. Meigs. Of
course, no one could help admiring Cecile. Just the same,
Mrs. Meigs felt more kindly toward him.
"I'll go with you some other time, if you care to ask
me," she said. "Give my love to your mother, Cecile.
Thank her for those lecture tickets she sent me the other
day. I'm sorry I couldn't go."
"Do you like lectures, Mrs. Meigs?" asked Roger
Kane.
"No one likes lectures," said Cecile, tilting back her
head to get the crumbs of cake into her mouth. "People
are always having to take tickets. Then when it comes
to using 'em, they try to work the tickets off on some old
lady of their acquaintance. Haven't you ever noticed
the gray hair in lecture audiences?"
"I'm sure I might like lectures," said Mrs. Meigs po-litely. "That is, some lectures. But I never find time
to go."
"No, there isn't that much time," agreed Cecile. "Com-
ing, Roger?"
Mrs. Meigs went to the window to watch them start.
From Roger's car, Cecile waved a hand to her.
Mrs. Meigs turned away with a little smile. Cecile
always managed to find time for her. And how full a
person's time did get! Here was Mrs. Meigs, living alone,
and carefully avoiding devices to entrap her. (How
quickly Cecile had known she never intended to use those
tickets!) With company coming, Mrs. Meigs had a dozen
things to do before two o'clock.
She got Anna to help her move the card-table near the
living-room windows, where the light would be good.
It was a substantial table that Mrs. Meigs had used in the
old house, none of your wabbly folding affairs, with one
leg that always gave way under somebody's coffee-
cup.
On the table she put a cover with elastic underneath
to draw it smooth: a present this from Cecile. In the
middle of the cover she laid a case with two handsome
decks of cards: that was a present from Richard, though
she suspected Cecile had picked it out for him. The score
pad and pencils went on next.
She was bringing in Cecile's roses from the dining-
room-she wanted them where her friends could admire
them while they played when the door-bell rang. Anna
started to answer. "That's prob'ly Mis' Bishop," she said
with a broad grin. "She always comes early."
"Not this early," said Mrs. Meigs. "It's only half past
eleven. If it's an agent, you get rid of him."
"If it's callers, I'll tell 'em you ain't in," said Anna."All right. And I'll go out on the fire-escape, to save
your conscience," said Mrs. Meigs.
Anna came back from the door, looking as if her face
had frozen. "It's Mis' Graham," she announced, and
withdrew to the kitchen.
The caller had followed her in. She was a tall com-
manding woman, a born executive. "Oh, good morning,
Millicent!" said Mrs. Meigs.
Millicent stooped to give a dutiful peck at her mother's
cheek. "I'm sorry I can't ask you to sit down," said Mrs.
Meigs firmly. "But I've company coming this afternoon,
and I've a lot of things to see to."
"Company?" echoed Millicent. She swept past her
mother into the living-room, and surveyed it with a
general's eye. "Oh, cards!"
"Exactly, cards," said Mrs. Meigs. "There's nothing I
love more than a good game of cards."
"And coffee afterward, I suppose?"
"Well, when I ask people to come to my house, I can't
very well dismiss them with a drink of water and my
blessing, can I?"
"So many old people drink a great deal more coffee
than is good for them," said Millicent. "However! I just
dropped in for a minute. I have an important committee
meeting at twelve."
"You mustn't be late for it," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I came to see about your birthday next week," said
Millicent. "Edith tells me you're planning to have the
whole family here for dinner."
"Yes. I told Cecile to ask you."
"She did say something about it. But Edith and I both
think that getting up a big family dinner is too much
for you.""Edith offered to have the dinner at her house," ad-
mitted Mrs. Meigs. "But there's no reason why she should.
I have good help, and you know I always welcome a
chance to use my best china."
"If you insist on having the dinner here-"
"I insist, Millicent."
"Then let each of us bring something toward the
dinner. I could bring a cake, and Edith the salad, and
Natalie scalloped potatoes-"
"Jack's wife lives in a hotel. She could bring a bottle
of olives," interrupted Mrs. Meigs. "No, thank you,
Millicent. So long as I'm the hostess, I intend to provide
everything that a hostess should."
"So long as you're the hostess! Humph!" Millicent
stared at her mother. She was a full eight inches the taller
of the two; and under her search-light glance Mrs. Meigs
shifted uneasily, and wondered if her face could possibly
be dirty.
Whatever Millicent had on her mind, she didn't get it
said. Anna returned from the kitchen, carrying a glass
of water on a small tray.
"I thought you might be thirsty, Mis' Graham," she
announced.
"Why should you think that?" asked Millicent with
dignity.
"Well, in case you get thirsty, here's the water ready
for you." Anna set the tray down on the card-table. As
she turned to leave the room, she encountered Mrs.
Meigs's puzzled glance. One of Anna's eyes closed for a
second.
Mrs. Meigs choked. Millicent had worked that trick
pretty often, being thirsty so that she could get out into
the kitchen and see how Anna kept it. Still, one mustn'tencourage impertinence from a servant. "Do sit down
and make me a little visit, Millicent," said Mrs. Meigs,
loud enough so that the retreating Anna would hear
her.
"I really haven't time," said Millicent. But she sat
down.
Unluckily, she sat on the book from the circulating
library. She pulled it from beneath her, and glanced at it.
"It isn't really half so bad as it looks," said Mrs. Meigs
hastily.
But Millicent was inspecting the pages. "It's splashed
with water," she said sternly. "Mother, you've been read-
ing in the bathtub again."
"Reading in the tub," said Mrs. Meigs, "makes any
book enjoyable. Even that one."
"But when you get to reading, you stay in the tub too
long. Lying in hot water makes people catch cold."
"I don't have half as many colds as you do," retorted
Mrs. Meigs.
"And I suppose you lock the bathroom door when
you take a bath?"
This was a new charge. "I certainly do," said Mrs.
Meigs. "With only one bathroom in the flat, I don't want
Anna walking in on me."
"But you must never lock the bathroom door. Haven't
I told you that before? You might have a stroke while
you're in the tub!"
"I might," said Mrs. Meigs, not in the least alarmed.
"If I did, Anna could call the fire department. They'd
put a ladder up to the window."
"Really, Mother, you're hard to do anything with."
Millicent sighed. Then her eye caught something on thetable, and she returned to the onslaught. "Look where
that girl leaves her duster!"
"She hasn't finished," said Mrs. Meigs. The misplaced
duster was a minor matter. But at that moment she be-
came guiltily aware of the cobweb just over Millicent's
head. If Millicent should happen to glance up! "Really,
Millicent, I know you mean to be helpful. But you
mustn't let me make you late for your committee meet-
ing."
Millicent rose with a sigh. "It interferes with my own
concerns, having to run over here and be sure that you're
all right."
"You shouldn't bother. If anything happened to me,
and Anna forgot to let you know, I'm sure the coroner
would telephone you."
Millicent smiled reluctantly. "Your sense of humor is
wonderful. Just the same, it's too much for you to have
the cares of a household at your age."
"That's all right. I've three married daughters to call
on if I want anything." Mrs. Meigs changed the subject.
"Cecile dropped in here this morning with a new beau.
New rather than young, I should call him."
"You mean Roger Kane? He's most attentive to
Cecile," said Millicent eagerly. "Didn't you think he was
nice?"
"He has a nice car," said Mrs. Meigs.
When Millicent had gone, she spoke to Anna about
the duster. Then she began to change her dress. The
cloud of her pleasant busyness wrapped around her again.
Settling her new ruching before the glass, she paused
for a troubled moment. "The cares of a household are too
much for you at your age," Millicent had said. That wasnonsense, now. But she wasn't getting any younger. If
the day ever came when she couldn't carry on-
"I'm getting morbid," she thought. She took down her
bonnet from a high shelf of the wardrobe, and called to
Anna that she'd eat her lunch on a corner of the kitchen
table, to save time.III
SHE had never been one to run to the grocery in her
house dress. Though her quest of a prize would take her
no farther than the corner drug-store, she put on her
bonnet. Not of course her best bonnet with the white
lawn ties and the white facing, but the all black bonnet
of whose age she was beginning to be a little proud. It
had a new veil every six or eight months, to be sure; but
no one could deny that the bonnet itself was ten years
old.
She had put on old-fashioned "widow's mourning"
when her husband died. She'd been sixty-four at the
time, and naturally she hadn't expected to survive him
long. When life began to pick up interest again, she had
stuck to a way of dressing that saved bother. She'd never
been a great hand to fuss about clothes; there were too
many other things that were more interesting.
Her eyes had taken on the wintry blue of old age; but
on a network of fine wrinkles, her cheeks were still pink.
She had always been small, and now she'd reached the
age of growing smaller; but she held herself carefully
erect. Girls in her day were taught to keep their backs
straight, even if they didn't have the modern fad for
exercise.
She had to manage the door of the apartment building
for herself; but at the drug-store a young man opened
the door for her. He followed her in, and stood waiting
while she explained to the clerk just what she wanted.A big package of chocolates, in a fancy box, and no hard
centers or nuts, please.
"Make him give you a tin box," suggested the young
man who had followed her in. "Then when the candy
is gone, you'll still have the box. That's what I always
get for my mother."
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I do like nice boxes. I never
can bear to throw one away. I've a whole stack of empty
boxes in the bottom of my wardrobe."
"So has my mother," said the young man. "She's back
East, where I come from. You remind me of her. You
must be just about the same age."
"How old is your mother?" asked Mrs. Meigs, looking
over the boxes the clerk was spreading before her. "Or
don't you know?"
"I know all right," said the young man. "She's very
proud of her age. She's sixty-nine."
It was all blarney, perhaps, his pretending to be so
surprised when she told him she was eighty. But after
Millicent's insinuations, it was very soothing. Mrs. Meigs
was still beaming when she climbed the stairs to her
second-floor flat, with a five pound box of chocolates
under her arm.
She found Mrs. Bishop waiting for her, though it
wasn't yet two o'clock. Mrs. Meigs exhibited the box to
her. "This'll give you something to play for," she said.
Mrs. Bishop blinked. "Land sakes! I would like to have
a big box of candy all for my own. But there isn't much
chance of me winning it, with Mrs. Cronkhite playing."
"Mrs. Cronkhite is a good player," admitted Mrs.
Meigs. "Not quite so good as she thinks she is. Still, she's
good."She hid the box of candy in her bedroom, and drew up
a rocking-chair near Mrs. Bishop. After her full morn-
ing, she didn't mind drawing a long breath before the
party began.
"S'pose the others will get here all right?" asked Mrs.
Bishop.
"Some one always walks to the street-car with Miss
Lamb, and that makes her slow getting started. But
they'll get here, don't worry."
But Mrs. Bishop continued to worry. "If at the last
minute one of them couldn't come, could you get any
one to play in her place?"
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I had Cal Fletcher play in Miss
Lamb's place once. Remember what happened?"
"I remember Mrs. Cronkhite insulted his playing. I
was so embarrassed! It really was very kind of him to
agree to come. Not every man likes to sit down to cards
with three women."
"Cal hardly knows one suit from another," said Mrs.
Meigs bluntly. "Of course I always make allowances for
him. But I can't expect everybody to do it."
"He's a very old friend, isn't he?" insinuated Mrs.
Bishop.
"He comes from my home town, back in Ohio. I knew
him before I ever met Mr. Meigs."
"He's a nice man," quavered Mrs. Bishop. "I always
think it's nice to have a man around the house, too."
"He's around my house quite often," admitted Mrs.
Meigs.
Mrs. Bishop looked as if she would like to say some-
thing further. Meeting Mrs. Meigs's eye, she thought
better of it. Not that other people hadn't said what Mrs.Bishop was thinking of. Mrs. Cronkhite had once put it
in its bluntest form, "Why don't you and Mr. Fletcher
get married?"
To which Mrs. Meigs had retorted, "I didn't take Cal
when he was a young man, and I certainly don't want
him now." Just the same she thought a lot of Cal-too
much to expose his bridge playing a second time.
Miss Lamb arrived early to-day, and Mrs. Cronkhite
on the dot. "Isn't this nice?" quavered Miss Lamb. "Now
we'll have a nice long time to play."
They drew their chairs around the table. "Two rubbers
and then we'll pivot," announced Mrs. Meigs. She drew
Mrs. Bishop as her first partner.
"That's a pretty dress, Mrs. Cronkhite," said Mrs.
Bishop.
"I've had it for eight years. It always pays to buy good
material," said Mrs. Cronkhite complacently.
Mrs. Meigs picked up her cards with a twirl, so that
everybody could see she had put on her diamond ring
for the occasion. Then she looked at Mrs. Bishop, and
turned the diamond inside. Mrs. Bishop wore what her
daughter picked out for her; to-day she had on a knitted
shoulder wrap of a hideous Easter-egg purple. Mrs. Bishop
was sallow anyhow; and purple so close to her face made
her look greenish and ghastly.
"The clothes people send to the Home for us are good
material," said Miss Lamb. "Sometimes though they're
quite out of date."
"Out of date?" repeated Mrs. Cronkhite with disdain.
"What if they are, pray? A pretty dress is a pretty dress;
and I for one don't believe in changing with every whim
of fashion.”"Oh, of course not!" said Mrs. Bishop hastily. "I see by
the papers, everybody is playing contract nowadays. I
suppose one of these days we'll have to take it up."
"I don't see why," said Mrs. Cronkhite. "Contract is
an overbidder's game." She looked hard at Miss Lamb.
Miss Lamb recognized the hint. But a hint is power-
less against a ruling passion. She examined her cards, drew
a long breath, swallowed hard, and said, "Well, I have a
little bit of everything this time. So I think I'll bid no-
trump."
Mrs. Meigs passed briskly, Mrs. Cronkhite indignantly,
and Mrs. Bishop apologetically. Mrs. Meigs led, and Mrs.
Cronkhite laid down her hand. The only honor in it was
a queen of the suit Mrs. Meigs had led.
"Oh!" gasped Mrs. Bishop. "With such a hand, you
should have taken your partner out of a no-trump bid."
"I don't believe in taking people out," said Mrs. Cronk-
hite firmly. "If they will bid when they haven't the
cards, let them stay in, I say."
"But the authorities say you should take out," insisted
Mrs. Bishop. "This man who has a bridge column in the
paper every night-"
"And who made him an authority, pray?" demanded
Mrs. Cronkhite. "The authorities are self-elected. And
it's easy enough for them to make the games in the paper
come out the way they like. They have the say about how
all four hands should be played."
Miss Lamb drew a long breath, and played the queen
from her dummy. Mrs. Bishop took it with the king. "Oh,
isn't that too bad!" wailed Miss Lamb. "Mrs. Cronkhite
told me to make up my mind always to finesse the queen
or never to finesse her, and I decided always to finesseher. But it seems that every time I do, I always lose her."
Miss Lamb took five tricks in all. "Set two!" snapped
Mrs. Cronkhite.
"Isn't that too bad!" said Miss Lamb. "I had such a
good hand, too. At least, it wasn't so bad. If the finesses
had gone through, and I could have taken just two more
tricks-"
"You wouldn't have been set," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
"But you were set."
Mrs. Meigs played the next hand for hearts, and lost.
"The score all seems to be going above the line," she said
with a chuckle. "At this rate, it will take us a long time
to get six rubbers played."
"You'd have made your bid that time, if you'd taken
out trumps," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
"Maybe I would. But I always like to keep my trumps
in my hand, and have 'em to look at," said Mrs. Meigs
blandly. "They look so rich."
"You never play them until you're reduced to them,"
said Mrs. Cronkhite.
"Reduced to trumps!" said Mrs. Meigs with a chuckle.
"That's my system. But of course if you feel like it,
you can always lead my trumps against me."
The next round Mrs. Cronkhite secured the bid. Mrs.
Bishop, leading, asked Miss Lamb, "Are the old ladies in
the Home keeping pretty well this fall?"
"Pretty well for us," said Miss Lamb, laying down her
hand with the clubs and spades all mixed up.
"Did you know Mrs. Meigs's daughter Mrs. Graham
is on the Board of the Home now?" asked Mrs. Bishop.
"Mrs. Graham?" echoed Miss Lamb. "Oh, the mother
of that pretty girl who came in the last time we were
playing?""Yes. Cecile doesn't look a bit like her mother," said
Mrs. Bishop. "That is-I mean- Has Cecile been in to
see you lately, Mrs. Meigs?"
"You live close enough so that you could find that out
some other time," suggested Mrs. Cronkhite. "It's your
trick. Why don't you take it in?"
"Oh, I'm sorry!" said Mrs. Bishop. "I knew you must
have the ace, and I supposed of course you'd play it."
Mrs. Cronkhite played the ace on the second trick.
Then she led out her trumps. She scored a little slam.
"That was wonderful, partner!" said Miss Lamb. Mrs.
Cronkhite beamed. The tension relaxed.
Mrs. Cronkhite and Miss Lamb won the first two
rubbers. Then Miss Lamb and Mrs. Meigs changed seats.
Mrs. Meigs grinned across the table at Mrs. Cronkhite.
"You've got a new partner. Now look out," she warned.
She got the bid for two spades. Miss Lamb led a low
club. Mrs. Meigs took it with the king in dummy. Then
she led back a low club.
"Why," gasped Mrs. Bishop, "you're returning your
opponent's lead! The expert in the paper says never to do
that!"
"The expert in the paper is right, for once," said Mrs.
Cronkhite.
Mrs. Meigs took the trick with the queen in her own
hand. Then she blandly led out the ace of clubs. It secured
her the trick. "Three times around! Clubs went around
three times!" said Mrs. Bishop in tones of awe.
"There's more luck than sense in that," said Mrs.
Cronkhite grimly.
Mrs. Meigs established a cross ruff, and enjoyed herself
thoroughly. She won three tricks, but missed game. "If
you'd led out your trumps-" began Mrs. Cronkhite."I wouldn't have made any more tricks," said Mrs.
Meigs flatly.
"You would have. You'd have gone game," insisted
Mrs. Cronkhite, and explained how.
"That's taking your bridge pretty seriously," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"I hope I do take it seriously," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
"I was taught whist as a child. Old-fashioned straight
whist: still the best game there is, though nowadays
nobody will exert the brains it takes to play it. My father
was a fine whist-player, and he taught us all the game.
There were six of us children, and we were punished,
sometimes severely punished, if we made a mistake at the
whist table."
"No wonder you play a good game of cards, if you
were punished as a child for playing badly," said Mrs.
Bishop.
"I remember once," went on Mrs. Cronkhite, "being
sent to bed without any supper three nights in succession,
simply for failing to get out my trumps. The same thing
happened once at least to each of my brothers and
sisters."
"Well," said Mrs. Meigs, "I was an only child, and I
was always spoiled at home. I liked it, and I'm glad I
was."
Mrs. Meigs played the next hand. At the twelfth trick,
she led her ace of trumps. "Well, I declare!" said Mrs.
Cronkhite. "This is worth waiting for. Mrs. Meigs is
reduced to trumps."
"You don't like it if your partner loses," said Mrs.
Meigs. "And you don't like it much if your partner
wins."
She liked being Mrs. Cronkhite's partner, though, whenMrs. Cronkhite played the hand. After the second round,
Mrs. Cronkhite knew where every card lay. Though she
needn't have bragged so much about it, she really had
card sense. Miss Lamb hadn't, and she never would have.
Not only was she a chronic overbidder: in playing she
always skimmed off her top cards. Mrs. Cronkhite never
bothered to force Miss Lamb's aces. Mrs. Meigs grinned
in sympathy when they came tumbling down.
The second two rubbers ended. Mrs. Meigs changed
places with Mrs. Bishop. "I'm not getting much chance
to play the hands this afternoon," complained Miss Lamb.
"I have good hands, too; but some one always takes the
bid away from me."
"Now that I'm your partner, I can raise your bids for
you," said Mrs. Meigs. "It is much nicer if everybody gets
a chance to play part of the hands."
Mrs. Cronkhite smiled grimly. This promised a killing
for her, and so far she had been on the winning side in
every rubber.
Mrs. Bishop dealt and passed. "Is your youngest daugh-
ter keeping better this fall?" she asked Mrs. Meigs. "She
wasn't so well last winter."
"Natalie doesn't look very well," said Mrs. Meigs. "She
says there's nothing much the matter, but I worry about
her a good deal sometimes."
"My daughter Grace saw her down town the other
day, she told me," said Mrs. Bishop. "Natalie is Mrs.
Brooks, isn't she? Grace saw Mrs. Brooks having lunch at
Rispigliosi's with a young man."
"Are you sure it wasn't Mrs. Meigs's granddaughter?"
asked Miss Lamb. "She looks a good deal like that aunt."
"Now maybe it was," said Mrs. Bishop. "I'm sure it
must have been. What would Mrs. Brooks be doing, hav-ing lunch at a restaurant with a young man not her
husband?"
"She'd just be having lunch," said Mrs. Meigs. "Though
in this town, she might know even that would get talked
about."
"Oh, I didn't mean-!" faltered Mrs. Bishop.
"You just wanted something to talk about, didn't
you?" asked Mrs. Cronkhite sympathetically. "Almost
anything is better than keeping your mind on the game."
Mrs. Bishop smiled apologetically. "I know I never get
around to ask about people's relatives any other time.
But somehow I think of them all when I'm at the card-
table. And then I remember how long it is since I asked
about them the last time."
"Just as long as it is since you played the last time.
That's right," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
True to her promise, Mrs. Meigs raised Miss Lamb's
bid. They were set. Three more times in succession the
same thing happened. "Is this bridge, or is it bumble
puppy?" demanded Mrs. Cronkhite.
"What's bumble puppy?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"It's a game people used to play down in Arkansas
with two lumps of sugar and two fans," explained Miss
Lamb. "The object is to keep the flies off your own lump
of sugar and get them to light on the other person's. Of
course nobody who really was anybody played it. And
I'm sure I don't see the connection now."
"Don't you?" asked Mrs. Cronkhite.
Mrs. Bishop dealt hastily, and picked up her cards.
"One no-trump," she announced.
Miss Lamb spread out her cards fan-shape, closed up
the fan, and opened it again. She was hoping reëxamina-
tion would reveal at least a jack, and justify her in aninformative double. Mrs. Cronkhite scorned informative
doubles, holding them "no better than talking across the
board." But Mrs. Meigs said, What was the difference?
People talked across the board anyhow, and they might
as well do it that way.
A thump on the table made Miss Lamb jump. Mrs.
Cronkhite was spluttering, and her face was purple. In
front of her, face up, lay her own cards. "I give it up!"
she choked. "Six no-trump! You didn't have a no-trump
bid, Mrs. Bishop. You couldn't have. I raise you to six,
and I might just as well lay down my cards and let them
set us."
"What are you raising for, if you expect us to set
you?" asked Miss Lamb, puzzled.
But Mrs. Meigs turned a calm face to Mrs. Bishop.
"Miss Lamb is passing, and so am I," she said. "Just go
ahead and play the hand for six no-trump. I'm sure so
far as I'm concerned, you can make it."
"But I had a no-trump bid," protested Mrs. Bishop,
on the verge of tears. "It was a weak no-trumper, but
I had it."
"Of course you did," said Mrs. Meigs. "Mrs. Cronkhite
must have splendid support for you. Go ahead and play
the hand."
Mrs. Cronkhite sat back in her chair, and glared. Mrs.
Bishop fumbled for her handkerchief.
Not even her usual optimism could make Miss Lamb
think that she "had a good hand" this time. But for once
she was glad she hadn't. She led a heart at random.
Mrs. Cronkhite's hand held three aces, two kings, three
queens. The ace and king of hearts were missing. Mrs.
Bishop took the trick with the queen in her own hand.
Mrs. Bishop played through the spades, the clubs, thediamonds. Most of the tricks she took in the dummy; but
she took them. On the last two diamond tricks, Mrs.
Meigs discarded the ace and king of hearts.
"You see, you get a grand slam," she said placidly.
Mrs. Bishop let her breath go; she'd been holding it
through thirteen tricks. But Mrs. Cronkhite turned on
Mrs. Meigs. "You gave us those two tricks on purpose!"
she said furiously.
"Did you expect me to go banging on my ace the first
thing?"
"It was your play."
"No, it wasn't," said Mrs. Meigs. "It may be the way
you used to play whist, and got sent to bed for. But it
isn't the way I play."
"The game isn't worth playing, the way you play,"
raged Mrs. Cronkhite.
"We might just throw out this hand and not count it,"
suggested Miss Lamb.
"Throw the hand out, when your opponents make a
grand slam!" Mrs. Cronkhite laughed savagely.
"There, you see!" said Mrs. Meigs. "It's just as I told
you a little while ago. You don't like it if you lose, and
you don't like it any better if you win."
Mrs. Bishop laughed apologetically. "After all, it's just
a game."
"It isn't a game at all, the way some people play it.
It's a farce," snapped Mrs. Cronkhite.
"You're one game on the rubber. Perhaps you'd rather
not finish it?" suggested Mrs. Meigs with icy politeness.,
"I'd much rather not. I prefer to forfeit it," said Mrs.
Cronkhite, with the dignity of an empress.
Tears came to Mrs. Bishop's eyes. Miss Lamb, though
for once her skirts were clear of blame, withered visibly.But Mrs. Meigs confronted the angry empress with a
dignity equal to her own.
"Very well," she said. "Very well. Then let me add up
the scores of my other guests." She snatched the score
pad, which Mrs. Cronkhite always kept for all of them.
A terrible silence fell. The tap of Mrs. Meigs's pencil
up and down the columns sounded like a hail-storm.
"I don't see how we can ever play again, after such a
scene," whispered Mrs. Bishop to Miss Lamb. "And I did
love our bridge games so."
Blinking away her tears, Mrs. Bishop watched Mrs.
Cronkhite. But either back in Arkansas or in the Old
Ladies' Home, Miss Lamb had learned more than that
about people.
She watched Mrs. Meigs.IV
THE door-bell rang, and Miss Lamb jumped; she was
really awfully nervous to-day. She was the only one
of the group in the living-room who turned to watch
Anna go to answer. Mrs. Bishop's back was toward the
door. Mrs. Meigs was still totting up score. Mrs. Cronk-
hite sat glaring like an offended Buddha.
But take any four women in a room together, and let
a man enter: the atmosphere changes. This man didn't
enter, anyhow: he invaded. He was young, he was tall,
and he was handsome.
"Gram here?" he demanded of Anna. Without wait-
ing for an answer, he rushed into the living-room, and
fell back a step in mock dismay. "At the gaming-table!
I might have known it!"
"Why, Richard!" said Mrs. Meigs.
"Dick to you, madam!" He pulled her out of her chair,
hugged her, and set her back. "Hello, Mrs. Bishop! Keep-
ing Gram out of the paths of righteousness as usual,
aren't you?"
"You remember Mrs. Cronkhite and Miss Lamb?"
prompted Mrs. Meigs. "This is my grandson, Richard,
Miss Lamb. Dick, I thought you were safely in Madison,
studying."
"I was, two hours ago. That is, I was in Madison. I
wasn't studying. Did eighty miles in less than two hours.
Could have done better, only I had to slow up for thetowns. Didn't have money to pay a fine if I should be
ap-pre-hen-ded."
"It's lovely to see you," said Mrs. Meigs. "Quite a sur-
prise, too, in the middle of the week."
"Blame Cecile," said Richard. "There's a dinner dance
going on to-night. She phoned me to come in for it. Got
anything to eat, Gram? I know those dinner dances.
Half past seven is the hour set. Eight by the time people
get there. Quarter past when you sit down. Half past
eight before you get anything to eat. And driving's
hungry work."
"We have some fresh cakes," said Mrs. Meigs. "You
like Anna's little cakes. Won't you sit down and have
coffee with us?"
"Can't," said Richard. "Two girls waiting for me in
my car."
"Two?" echoed Mrs. Bishop.
"Yes'm. Two. Safety in numbers."
"Ask them in," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Can't. They're mad at each other. One of 'em's got
red hair, too.”
Anna brought in a plate of cakes. He helped himself
with both hands, and talked as he ate. "I blew my horn
outside the black-haired girl's house on my way here.
Shall we let her remain nameless? Yes, I think we'll let
her remain nameless. Oh, Dick,' says she. 'You made
your getaway from all the fair sirens in Madison, just to
be with me?' 'And from the traffic cops too, I made my
getaway,' says I. I got a date at seven. I gotta dress, too.
But for the next hour I'm all yours.' So she gets in my
car. Never even waits to put her hat on. At the very
next corner, don't we almost run over the red-haired
girl! I had to slow down, and she recognized me. Oh,Dick!' she says. 'What a pleasure to see you!' 'All right,'
says I. 'Get in!" "
"And then?" asked Mrs. Bishop.
"That's all. They've been clawing at each other ever
since."
"Nowadays girls are a little forward, seems to me,"
said Mrs. Bishop.
"Fresh eggs," agreed Richard. "Good looking, though.
Especially the red-haired one."
"You're Mrs. Graham's son, aren't you?" quavered
Miss Lamb.
"So I've always supposed," answered Richard.
"You look like Mrs. Graham," said Mrs. Cronkhite
firmly.
"Worse luck, I do. Wish I looked like Mrs. Meigs,"
said Richard with a grin. "Got anything else to eat,
Gram? If not, I may as well be going."
"So soon?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, I'll be back! Speaking of which reminds me.
Come here a minute." He drew Mrs. Meigs toward the
door, out of earshot of the others. "Would you mind if
I came here to change my clothes, and remove what
they call in books the stains of travel?"
"You know I'd love it," said Mrs. Meigs. "Is this one
of those things I'm not supposed to mention to your
mother?"
"I'm going to drive back to Madison after the party,"
said Richard. "Got a quiz to-morrow that I can't cut.
But Mom would never believe that zeal for my studies
would lead me to drive until dawn. And you know her
when she gets asking questions."
He did look like Millicent when he said that. When
the laughter died out of his eyes, his young face washard. A little hardness didn't hurt a young man, though.
"Spend the night here, if you like," suggested Mrs.
Meigs.
"Love to, some other time. Quiz is at ten to-morrow
morning."
"You're getting quite serious about your studies,
seems to me."
"Only when I have to be. Don't want my college
career cut short by the authorities. It'll end soon
enough."
"Grand while it lasts, though?"
"Gorgeous!" He hugged her, tweaked her ear, and
shouted "Good-by!" into the other room. Then the
door slammed, and through an open window came the
echoes of his struggle with a balky self-starter.
Mrs. Meigs stepped to the kitchen door. "You can
start the toast now, Anna. And be sure to make plenty
of coffee."
The living-room was still aflutter, as if a strong breeze
had blown through. "Such a handsome young man!"
said Mrs. Cronkhite.
"I'm sorry for those poor girls, though," said Miss
Lamb.
"You needn't be," said Mrs. Bishop. "Nowadays girls
make all the advances, and it's nobody's fault but their
own if two of them fall in love with the same young
man. When I was a girl, we waited to be asked."
"I know," quavered Miss Lamb. "But see how that
came out! Some of us didn't get asked."
Mrs. Meigs entered the room in time to catch those
last words. "You can't tell me that," she said. "You
were too particular. That's all."
"A woman is better off married," said Mrs. Cronk-hite. Her tone was defensive; the late Mr. Cronkhite
had not been an honorary member of the W. C. T. U.
"Of course a woman is better off," said Mrs. Meigs.
"But it's hard on the men. There are three widows in the
room this minute; but widowers are scarcer than hens'
teeth."
"You know Cal Fletcher," said Mrs. Bishop.
"And Dr. Manning," added Miss Lamb.
"I should say she does know Dr. Manning," said Mrs.
Bishop. "I was in here with Mrs. Meigs the other day
when he came and wanted her to go for a walk with
him."
"Did she go?" asked Miss Lamb.
"I did not. He was toting his umbrella, though there
wasn't a sign of rain, so far as I could see. He'd lost the
strap, and to keep the thing furled he'd tied an old
piece of purple ribbon around it." Remembering Dr.
Manning's umbrella, Mrs. Meigs's voice was rich with
indignation that he should imagine his attentions would
be welcome to her.
"His daughter-in-law ought to look after him better
than that," agreed Mrs. Bishop. "Still, when an old
man has no one that really belongs to him-!”
Mrs. Meigs twinkled. "I wouldn't want an old man,
and no young man would want me. So I guess I'm safe
to remain a widow."
Anna came in to spread the embroidered coffee cloth,
a present from Edith, with napkins to match. Mrs.
Meigs poured from a heavy silver coffee-pot. A rich
red gleamed in the heart of the strawberry jam, and
Anna brought the hot buttered toast in relays.
Mrs. Cronkhite mellowed. She always enjoyed her
food. Mrs. Bishop, anxious for forgiveness, tried to begenial too. But Miss Lamb still kept a watchful eye on
Mrs. Meigs.
When more toast had been refused all round, and the
little cakes came in, Mrs. Meigs excused herself for a
minute. She returned with the five-pound box of
chocolates.
"Richard coming in kept us from finishing our game,"
she said. "But Mrs. Cronkhite was on the winning side
in every rubber we did play. So we're going to give her
the prize."
Miss Lamb looked anxiously at Mrs. Cronkhite. After
the way she'd acted, she surely wouldn't be such a pig
as to take that wonderful box of candy. But if she re-
fused it, how awkward that would be!
Mrs. Cronkhite turned purple for a minute. Then she
rose grandly to the situation. "Half one's success in
bridge depends on having a good partner," she said. "If
you can find me an empty box, Mrs. Meigs, I'd like to
divide these bonbons with Mrs. Bishop."
Mrs. Meigs fetched one of the empty boxes from the
bottom of her wardrobe. There were plenty of one-
pound boxes there, but she selected a two-pound.
On her way back with it, she stepped into the kitchen
to tie up a glass of raspberry jam, and a glass of quince,
and a glass of damson plum. This package she presented
to Miss Lamb. It reminded her of the days when she
used to give parties for her children, and made a point
of seeing to it that somehow every guest got a prize.
Still, Miss Lamb ought to take back something to show
to the other old ladies in the Home.
There were fresh exclamations, and another round
of coffee to accompany the cakes. Tongues were loosened
now. They all talked at once, and interrupted eachother, and then interrupted themselves to try to listen.
"Do you remember when we used to play straight
bridge, where the dealer made the trump, or passed the
make to his partner?" asked Mrs. Bishop.
"I remember before that, when we used to play
cinch," said Miss Lamb.
"A silly game, cinch. But good fun, in a way," said
Mrs. Cronkhite tolerantly.
"I don't believe I remember how the game goes,"
said Mrs. Bishop.
"Let's have a round of cinch, and perhaps we can
refresh each other's memories," said Mrs. Cronkhite.
The table was cleared, and they begin to play cinch.
Mrs. Cronkhite did most of the refreshing; but she did
it indulgently. Evidently she had never been sent to
bed for failing to play the pedro on her partner's ace.
The lamps were lighted, and the window closed. All
at once Miss Lamb looked at her watch, which she wore
pinned on her breast. "Oh, dear! I'd no idea it was so
late! They'll be worried at the Home. The matron will
think I've been run over."
"It's time we were going," agreed Mrs. Cronkhite.
"I'll put you on the street-car, Miss Lamb."
There was a bustle of departure. "You don't have to
hurry. Stay and keep me company a while," Mrs. Meigs
whispered to Mrs. Bishop.
Mrs. Cronkhite tucked her prize under her arm, and
took a firm hold of Miss Lamb. "We must play again
soon," she said. "When I think of all the years we've
been playing cards together, just the four of us!"
"And never had a word of trouble!" chimed in Miss
Lamb. "It isn't many card clubs can say the same."
"Not many, I guess," agreed Mrs. Bishop.The two of them who had to take a street-car got
away together, Miss Lamb wavering a little, Mrs. Cronk-
hite slow but solid, like the Rock of Gibraltar taking
a little hike. "It was a lovely party!" said Mrs. Bishop.
"You always do give such lovely parties!"
"I like parties," said Mrs. Meigs. "But the most fun
of a party is having somebody stay afterward to talk
it over with you."
Mrs. Bishop beamed. By common consent, they sat
down in rocking-chairs now. "I think I'll eat another
cake," said Mrs. Meigs. "Won't you have one too? They
don't take off the price for any you send back, you
know."
"Oh, I couldn't eat another thing!"
"Coffee?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"I've had three cups already."
"That'll keep you awake anyhow, so you might as
well have four. Anna!" called Mrs. Meigs. "Bring Mrs.
Bishop a cup of coffee."
Anna appeared with a tray. "D'you want some too,
Mis' Meigs!"
"No, thank you," said Mrs. Meigs. "I've had more
now than is good for me. Why did you bring two
cups?"
"I thought you might want some too."
Anna disappeared. Mrs. Meigs filled a cup for Mrs.
Bishop. Then with careful absent-mindedness she filled
a cup for herself. As she picked it up to drink, she
caught Mrs. Bishop's eye and smiled guiltily.
"I just love coffee," confessed Mrs. Bishop. "But
Grace thinks it isn't good for me."
"Same with Millicent," said Mrs. Meigs. "But what
Millicent doesn't know won't hurt her.""It must be nice to have a home of your own," said
Mrs. Bishop wistfully. "When I had one, I was a pretty
important person. But nowadays-"
"There, there, my dear!" said Mrs. Meigs. "I'm sure
Grace means to be kind."
But as they sipped and rocked, she waited to hear
what daughter Grace had done now. Grace always man-
aged to have just done something, every time Mrs. Meigs
saw Mrs. Bishop.
"This morning-" began Mrs. Bishop. "But maybe
you noticed something when you called me?"
"You were quite a while getting to the telephone.
Is that what you mean?"
"I was crying," admitted Mrs. Bishop. "You see, I
had a lot of silk pieces in an old hamper. I expected to
piece a quilt of them some day, when I got around to it.
The hamper was in our locker room in the basement.
Well, this morning I went down to look my pieces over,
and they were gone."
"Gone? All of them?"
"Every one. I went up and asked Grace about them.
She said she'd thrown them away."
"She'd thrown away your silk pieces?"
"Sold them to the rag man. She said they were dirty.
Some of them may have been. It isn't any too clean in
that locker, and dust does sift into a hamper. But she
never even asked me to look 'em over. She just got rid
of 'em."
"She ought to have a good, smart spanking," said Mrs.
Meigs crisply.
"There was just nothing I could do or say!"
"No. But you owe her a vote of thanks, too. Now
you won't have to make the quilt."SI
Mrs. Bishop laughed a rather weak and quavering
laugh, but she was amused. "You're such a comfort to
me, neighbor," she said. "I don't know what I'd do,
if I couldn't run in here and tell you things."
"Well, I tell you things too," said Mrs. Meigs. "It
works both ways."
In silence they rocked and sipped. A lamp lighted the
deserted card-table, but the corners of the room were
soft with shadow. Mrs. Meigs's mind slipped to the
group picture of her family that hung in her bedroom.
She used to have it out here where people could see it,
but Millicent and Edith had joined forces to get her to
put it in her bedroom. It wasn't as if they were still at
the age when they had beaux; she'd been glad to spare
their feelings then. She'd bring that picture out here
to-morrow, just to show them.
The door-bell made her think Richard was early.
But it was Natalie, Mrs. Carter Brooks on her visiting-
cards. Natalie was the aunt Cecile looked like. She was
the prettiest of Mrs. Meigs's daughters, and the least
lucky. From the time Millicent had got whooping-cough
in school-merely a nice vacation for Millicent, but a
severe sickness for Natalie, who caught it from her-
way back as far as that, Natalie had always got the
short end of things. Her husband was, to Mrs. Meigs's
way of thinking, a very short end.
Natalie was wearing Edith's last year's fur coat,
though the weather wasn't cold enough to justify a fur
coat, and it hadn't been cut down to Natalie's size. Her
eyes were bright, though, and her smile came and went,
and her cheeks were pink under their light dusting
of rouge. Mrs. Meigs saw Mrs. Bishop eye her, thinking,
doubtless, of that silly story her daughter had told aboutNatalie "lunching with a young man." Well, let Mrs.
Bishop take a look. Natalie was worth looking at.
Her call was brief. When she left, though, the charm
was broken. "I must be getting back," said Mrs. Bishop.
"Try not to think too much about Grace," advised
Mrs. Meigs. "And run in here whenever-whenever you
want to come."
"I suppose I'm silly to mind so much," said Mrs.
Bishop. "Still, I am her mother. But nowadays children
all seem to think they know more than their mothers."
"Nowadays!" That word was always coming up in
Mrs. Bishop's talk. As if all the good things in life were
past! But for her perhaps they were, poor soul. Mrs.
Meigs looked around for something tangible that would
express her sympathy: the candy, after all, had been
deeded over by Mrs. Cronkhite.
Her eye lighted on "Bad Women." She snatched it
up. "Here, you haven't read this. Don't you want to
take it along?"
"Oh, I'd like to!" began Mrs. Bishop. "But-"
Mrs. Meigs cut short the inevitable objection; she
knew so well what it was. "You needn't let your
daughter see it. You can always hide books under sofa
pillows."
"I'm almost afraid to try," faltered Mrs. Bishop. "I
feel guilty even about that coffee."
"Here!" said Mrs. Meigs. "Let me smell your breath."
Mrs. Bishop submitted to the inspection so seriously
that Mrs. Meigs's joke turned into a verdict. "You're
all right. Just go to your own room, and have a news-
paper handy to slip the book under, in case you hear
Grace coming.”
Alone again, Mrs. Meigs realized that she wouldn'thave much longer to wait for Richard now. The grand-
children were always pure gain, but she liked to see her
children too, when they came singly as they had come
to-day, not in ranked battalion, as they'd show up next
week for her birthday party. Woman to woman, she
could hold her own even against Millicent. And how
pretty Natalie had looked this afternoon, and how
young! How something else too. Mrs. Meigs recognized
the look, though for the moment she couldn't put a
name to it.
Miss Lamb called up, to let her know she'd got back
safe to the Home, and the matron and everybody had
enjoyed that beautiful jam. Poor thing, to have a few
rounds of cards and a little jam mean so much to
her!
Mrs. Meigs was turning away when the telephone
rang again. It was a man's voice this time: a voice she
knew, though it didn't often call her. "Oh, Carter!"
said Mrs. Meigs dryly. She'd had twenty years to get
used to Carter Brooks, but she'd never got over the
feeling that Natalie should have done better.
"Natalie there?" asked Carter.
"Not now. She was here."
"How long ago did she leave?"
"Not long."
"What time did she get there?"
"She didn't stay long. And she didn't say where she
was going. And she didn't say she was coming back.
Is that all?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Humph, yes!" He hung up. He hadn't even asked
her how she was. He wasn't usually quite so abrupt,
though he never did have any manners. Mrs. Meigs dis-
missed Carter with a shrug.But all at once something clicked. She knew now
what had struck her in Natalie to-day. It was the look
of youth come back, the look-yes, this was it of a
woman in love. And Natalie had been seen lunching
downtown with a young man.
Perhaps she'd gone to dinner with him now. Mrs.
Meigs rather hoped she had. There'd never been a
divorce in the family. But Natalie was entitled to her
share of living. She'd never had it yet.
Mrs. Meigs went to lay out the things for Richard's
bath. She brought out her biggest bath towel; men liked
big bath towels. Richard's lanky six feet entitled him to
one of the biggest. And it seemed only yesterday that
she had held him in her lap to bathe him! He was a
pretty baby. How he'd hate it if she told him so now,
in front, say, of his red-haired girl!
She got out her English lavender soap. She'd been
saving her latest box of it unopened, it smelled so nice
in the linen closet. But the best was none too good for
Richard.
How silly she'd been, to get upset this afternoon be-
cause Mrs. Cronkhite bullied Mrs. Bishop! After what
Mrs. Bishop was used to at home, Mrs. Cronkhite's
bullying must seem like a June breeze. Of course, it was
out of all proportion to make such a scene over a card
game. But that just showed how few deep interests her
friends had in their everyday lives.
She was the only one of them who had any grandchil-
dren. Mrs. Meigs got down her lavender bath salts,
though she couldn't imagine Richard using them. The
hot water in the tub dripped a little. She'd have to speak
to the janitor about it.That was what Millicent called "having the cares of
a household." Well, she was pretty good at attending to
the cares of a household. Mrs. Meigs spread out her
largest bath mat.V
FRIDAY was an uneventful day. Mrs. Bishop came in
for a while during the afternoon. Anna had the after-
noon off, and Mrs. Meigs ate her dinner at a tea-room
in the neighborhood. She had standing invitations both
to Millicent's and to Edith's for Anna's day off, and
she would have had a better dinner at either place. But
she liked the fun of watching the other diners in the
tea-room; and the waitresses were so kind to her, always
saving her a corner table, and shifting the vegetables
from one menu to another, as they weren't supposed to
do.
Saturday morning Cecile telephoned. "Don't have
Anna get you any lunch, Caroline. Save the date for
me." Her voice sounded eager and happy, but she didn't
stop for explanations. She was in a hurry to get in her
golf practice before lunch.
Mrs. Meigs was in her bedroom when the door-bell
rang. She heard Cecile's voice and a man's; she stiffened
a little. Couldn't Cecile move any more without this
Roger Kane person?
But the man who was laughing with Cecile in the
living-room wasn't middle-aged and bald. Indeed his
hair was a flaming red, and he wore it like a crest. Cecile
ran to hug Mrs. Meigs, and kept an arm about her as
she said, "Caroline, I want you to meet my friend Tip
O'Neil. Tip, this is my grandmother, Mrs. Meigs."
Tip grinned, and his grin brought little wrinklesabout his eyes. "You didn't say grandmother?" he asked,
burring his r's a little. "Your sister, possibly. Or a very
young aunt."
"Get along with your blarney!" said Mrs. Meigs. To
Cecile she whispered, "I like this one. When is a woman
old?"
Cecile looked pleased, though she said warningly,
"Never trust Tip when he puts on that County Clare
accent. He learned it off John McCormack's records.
His Irish is two generations back. He comes from Iowa,
though he won't condescend to talk Iowayan."
"I'd have come from Ireland itself, to meet Mrs.
Meigs," retorted Tip. "Maybe you better bring along an
extra shawl or something, Cecile, in case the ground
is damp."
"Where are we going?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"On a picnic," said Cecile.
"A picnic?" Mrs. Meigs considered. "Let me see.
What can I take? There must be something in the
house"
"The arrangements are all made," said Cecile care-
lessly. "It's Tip's party."
They swept Mrs. Meigs down to the street. A Ford
with rusty fenders was parked illegally beside the
hydrant. Tip removed an oil-can and some cotton waste
from the back seat, and then suggested, "Maybe you'd
rather ride with me, Mrs. Meigs? She rides easier in
front."
"You won't be in front," said Cecile. "I'm going to
drive."
"You are not." Tip ducked hastily behind the wheel.
"Afraid to trust me with your bus?" gibed Cecile. "I
drove Roger Kane's car all day yesterday.""His is a Hispano-Suiza. This car is different," said
Tip.
Cecile took Mrs. Meigs in the back seat with her.
"When my ship comes in, darling, I'm going to have
a car of my own," she confided. "I love to drive, but
Dad needs the family car for business purposes, and I
wouldn't care to go far from home in a fifty dollar bus
like my brother's."
"Are we going far from home to-day?" asked Mrs.
Meigs.
"We are," said Tip. "We'll get back safe, too. Though
nobody but a relative of Cecile's would be extravagant
enough to pay fifty dollars for a bus like this."
"I suppose they gave you this one to get rid of it?"
teased Cecile.
"I wouldn't take it as a gift. But when they left it in
front of my place with a ten dollar bill tied to the
steering-wheel, I couldn't hurt their feelings by refus-
ing," said Tip.
He got started, with some grinding of gears. Mrs.
Meigs leaned back in her corner. She loved to ride, and
she was quite uncritical of driving.
Cecile smiled down at her. "I had a beautiful eighteen
holes this morning, Caroline. I'm beginning to be quite
excited about the match next week. You can't believe
what your friends tell you about your game. Of course,
they want you to win. But it looks to me now as if I
really had a chance."
"I hope you do win, if it's any satisfaction to you,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "But in my day, only the boys played
shinny."
Cecile laughed. She laughed easily to-day, and herblue eyes shone like stars. One loose lock, escaping from
the tight brim of her hat, curled in front of her ear.
Tip turned a corner on two wheels. Cecile laughed
