again. "Not nearly so dangerous as it looks," she whis-
pered. "We have the road to ourselves to-day, pretty
nearly."
As they drew toward the outskirts of the town, indeed,
traffic thinned to the vanishing-point. Soon after they
reached open country, Tip turned into a side road. Then
they had not only the road but the world to them-
selves. On the encircling hills, trees showed russet or
crimson. Slanting down from the sun was a visible
ray of mist; but the sky overhead was a deep blue,
and the only clouds were white puff-balls down near
the horizon.
Three miles out the dirt road, Tip turned into the
roadside, and stopped. Between them and a maple grove
lay a rail fence, gray with age. "There!" he said. "How's
this for rural? Antique, too. Fence looks as if it might
have been split by A. Lincoln in person. I'll bet it's the
only one left in the State."
Cecile helped him unload the complicated para-
phernalia of a picnic: a grate with folding legs, a frying-
pan, a coffee-pot smoked by many a wood fire. A dozen
parcels followed.
"It looks as if somebody meant to do some cooking,"
remarked Mrs. Meigs.
"Somebody does," said Tip. "I don't care for the sort
of picnic that tastes as if it had been carried around.
Soggy sandwiches, and more or less baked beans, and
potato salad, but somebody forgot the salt-"
He broke off with a look of consternation, and clappedhis hands on his pockets. "Somebody's name is me! Now
how could I possibly-?"
"How could you not?" asked Cecile. "You need a
guardian."
"You willing to undertake the job?"
"No. But I'm willing to go for the salt." She held
out her hand. Tip stared at her extended hand, but
made no move. "The keys!" she said impatiently. "You
can't expect me to drive back for salt unless I can turn
on the ignition."
"The keys are still in the car. But you don't want to
drive back to town all alone for salt, do you?"
"I'm only going to the nearest farm-house," said
Cecile. "But maybe I'd get there faster if I walked."
She set off down the road, her hands thrust into the
pockets of her tweed jacket. Tip watched her retreating
back with a broadening grin. Then from one of the par-
cels at his feet he produced the salt-cellar. "Think of
my making a mistake like that!" he said cheerfully.
"You knew all the time where that was,” accused Mrs.
Meigs.
"Maybe I did. But I wanted to see, would she go." He
shouted after Cecile, and waved the salt-cellar in the
air.
Cecile acknowledged his wave, but she went on. He
had both Mrs. Meigs and the parcels over the rail fence
when she came back. "I got half a dozen eggs," she said
cheerfully. "Now in case Tip's cooking goes wrong, we
won't starve."
She carried the parcels, and Tip the frying-pan and
grate and coffee-pot. Mrs. Meigs had a cushion from the
car. They set off through the grove. Under their feet thefallen leaves scuffled softly; trodden, they gave off a faint
dry scent that reminded Mrs. Meigs of the rose leaves
she used to keep in porcelain jars when she was a girl.
"They smell brown," she thought.
In a little clearing Tip halted, and began to set up his
grate. "You gather some sticks, Cecile," he directed.
"Dry sticks, not the kind you got last time. Mrs. Meigs,
can you peel potatoes? They're already cooked. All we
have to do is hot 'em."
He produced a thick steak, and cut it in strips. "This
we broil on hot stones. The frying-pan is for the potatoes.
Coffee grounds are in the pot, and the water too. We've
tomatoes to eat in our left hand, and radishes."
He whistled as he worked. In an amazingly short space
of time, he was serving the food on paper plates, and the
coffee in enamel mugs. "Want to boil your eggs in what's
left of the coffee?" he asked Cecile.
"I'll save 'em for next time," said Cecile.
"This is delicious," said Mrs. Meigs. "And everything
was ready so quickly!"
"Tip does all the useless things well," Cecile admitted.
"It's quite an accomplishment, at that. Dick always in-
sists on making flapjacks at a picnic. Did you ever eat any
of Dick's flapjacks, Caroline?"
"No, and nobody else could," said Mrs. Meigs. "But
he likes to make them. It's so simple, he says. Just flour
and water."
There was a bag of doughnuts to top off with. Mrs.
Meigs sat sipping her third cup of coffee. Cecile leaned
back against a tree, looking up at the sunlight through
the branches. Tip threw himself on the ground at her
feet. His gaze up at her was both adoring and mocking."I don't think we'll let Cecile go to college another
year, Mrs. Meigs," he said. "She'd make such a good wife
for some nice man."
"For more than one man, I hope," said Cecile. "Ap-
plications attended to in the order of their receipt. I'll
put yours on file, if you like."
"Nothing to that, sweetheart," said Tip. "After a few
years of me, you wouldn't want another man."
"After a few years of you, there wouldn't be enough
of me left to look for another man," said Cecile. But her
voice was softer than her words, and her eyes were a little
misty now, looking far away through the trees, and not
seeing what they looked at.
With a grin at Mrs. Meigs, Tip moved closer to Cecile,
and turned his head so that his cheek rested against her
hand, where it lay on the ground beside her. Cecile did
not stir, and didn't look down; but she let her hand lie
against his cheek.
Mrs. Meigs watched them. Young people had a strange
way of talking to each other nowadays. What they meant
for gallantry sounded to her like abuse. Yet there was
no mistaking the look in Cecile's eyes. Boys clustered
about her singly or in droves. She was interested briefly,
or not at all. Only this past summer, coming to stay the
night with Mrs. Meigs, she had waxed confidential at
bedtime. "I don't see that I can marry any one, Caroline,
until they legalize polygamy. I'll need a dancing husband
after my golf husband, and I'll be so glad to get to my
Tuesday husband when I'm tired of Monday's."
Tip began to sing, half under his breath. Cecile looked
down at him, and frowned a little. "Sing out loud, or not
at all," she commanded.
Tip grinned, and sang out loud. Cecile lifted the handthat was lying against his cheek, and snatched her hat
off. Her head sank back against the tree trunk, and her
eyes closed. But she listened. Listened not passively, but
greedily.
Tip's singing, though passable, wasn't that good. Mrs.
Meigs felt a little pain at her heart. She wanted just this
for Cecile, of course. But not just yet. Not quite like this.
Not for little Cecile, who had come to climb in her lap
when her doll was broken-only yesterday, it seemed,
or the day before.
Tip passed from "I Hear You Calling Me" to "Mavis."
As he sang,
"And true is the heart of your lover,
Who waits for your coming to-day,"
he fumbled for Cecile's hand again. She let him take it,
but she drew a long breath and opened her eyes.
"Oh, Tip!" she whispered. "If you weren't such an
idiot!"
Tip squeezed her hand, and let it go. "Then say the
word. It's quite a program, learning sense. But you've
only to say the word, and I'll try even that."
"I can just see you!" said Cecile scornfully. "You'll
spend all your life looking for the pot of gold at the
end of the rainbow."
"I'll not!" said Tip indignantly. "Haven't I already
been to college? Coe College in Iowa. You never heard
of it, but I've been there just the same. And haven't I
embarked on a business career? I can even show you a
letter I had from my grandfather, blessing the embark-
ing."
"He was hopeful," said Cecile. "So far you haven't gotaround to start looking for the pot of gold. But you be-
lieve it's there. You know you do."
Tip grinned. "It may, or it may not. Anyhow the
rainbow is awful pretty. Remember the double one we
saw the other day?"
"If rainbows were a profitable business- Oh, give me
a cigarette!" said Cecile impatiently.
She stooped over him as he lighted her cigarette. Her
ruffled hair gleamed in the sunshine; it was pale gold
like a child's, and like a child's it curled on her temples
when she was warm. "All this must bore you, Caroline,"
she said.
"On the contrary, it's an eye-opener for her," said Tip
severely. "Here it is Saturday afternoon, and a day made
to order, and you go talking as if you were a Committee
of Ways and Means."
Cecile clasped her slim sun-browned hands around her
knees, and laughed. "Where did you ever learn there
was such a committee?"
"Leave off talking as if I was a pauper," went on Tip,
winking at Mrs. Meigs. "I've two hundred dollars in the
bank this very minute."
"How'd that happen?"
"I hadn't got round to spend it yet," he admitted.
"And then I've my ranch in Colorado."
"You own Western land?" asked Mrs. Meigs, inter-
ested. Cal Fletcher had inherited three quarter-sections
in Colorado from his wife, and had been paying taxes on
it ever since her death. He'd have sold it long ago, only
no one else wanted it any more than he did.
"A nice parcel of land. It's dry enough so far, I'll
admit." Tip sat up, and faced Mrs. Meigs. A note of en-
thusiasm came into his teasing voice. "But the govern-ment dam is just being finished. Next year we begin to
irrigate. Five years from now, that will be the garden
spot of the world. I'll make the desert bloom, and the
desert will make me rich."
"Well, I won't live to see it," said Mrs. Meigs.
"None of us will," said Cecile. "Tip has visions of
making the desert bloom; and after he's looked at 'em
for ten minutes, he can't tell the difference between his
visions and sober sense."
"Not so fast, not so fast!" interrupted Tip. "The dam
is finishing this very month. I've a man on my place al-
ready to get things going. The finest fellow, Cecile! I
wish you could meet him. An Irishman, stands six feet
three in his stockings."
"A relative who needed to have a job made for him,
I suppose,” said Cecile.
Tip grinned. "Well, yes, he is my cousin. And yes, he
did need a job. He's married, you see, and crazy about his
wife. That's why I'd be willing for you to meet him.
He married a girl who can scarcely write her own name,
but she has a face like the Virgin Mary, and they've a
quite new baby."
"Boy or girl?" asked Mrs. Meigs, who was always in-
terested in babies.
"Boy, I think," said Tip. "No, I guess it's a girl. They
must have told me, but I've forgotten."
"I suppose you raised his pay when the baby was
born," said Cecile.
"I forget," said Tip. "Yes, I suppose I did."
"Quite right if he did," said Mrs. Meigs. "I'm sure any
man works harder if he has a family to support."
"It's a great incentive," said Tip. "Shouldn't you say
so, Cecile?""Anything to stop you arguing," said Cecile.
"A man without a woman," insisted Tip, "is a boat
without a rudder."
"Then I'd advise you," said Cecile, "to look for the
kind of woman who is born to be a rudder."
"They all are," said Tip promptly. "Aren't they, Mrs.
Meigs?"
She always made up her mind quickly whether she
liked people; yet it surprised Mrs. Meigs to find herself
siding now with Tip against Cecile. He was ingratiat-
ing, with his rich voice and his ruddy hair and the smile
that made wrinkles around his eyes. Yet he was unre-
liable, you could see at a glance; and Cecile was quite
right when she called him visionary. Cal Fletcher couldn't
give that Western land of his away.
"Why don't you and Cecile go for a walk through the
woods, before it starts to get chilly?" she asked. "I'll stay
here and watch the things."
"You come too," said Cecile. "Tip will give you an
arm over the rough places."
She loved to walk; and after miles of city sidewalks,
the ground seemed asking to be walked on. But Mrs.
Meigs shook her head. "I'll stay close by, and maybe find
some hickory-nuts," she said.
"Hickory-nuts in a maple grove?" asked Cecile. "Car-
oline, I hope you aren't trying to be tactful."
Tip scrambled to his feet, and put out a hand to help
Cecile. She sprang up without touching his hand, and ran
straight ahead, toward the thickest of the grove. "You
can't catch me!" she called over her shoulder.
Tip ran after her. Mrs. Meigs could see her golden head
flashing between the tree trunks. Then she stepped behindone of them, and vanished. Tip blundered forward, look-
ing for her. He'd gone past when she jumped out, and
shouted, "Fooled you that time! You thought I'd climbed
a tree."
A minute before they'd been struggling with destiny,
slashing at each other, trying to think things out, each
desperately holding his own under the surface of their
raillery. Now they were children, playing hide-and-seek
among the trees. After all, this suited them better. Little
Cecile!
The thin cushion from the car was getting hard. Mrs.
Meigs rose and walked about. Then she sat down again,
and again the cushion got hard. The sun, which had been
almost directly over their heads when they climbed the
rail fence, was level now with the lower branches of the
trees. A chill began to creep up from the ground.
At last she saw them coming back, scuffing through the
thickest of the fallen leaves, their clasped hands swinging
at arm's length between them. Children was all they were.
Yet when they reached her, Cecile's cheeks were flushed
beneath her sunburn, and her eyes were shining again,
shining like twin stars. Tip had kissed her, out here in the
sun and the wind. Mrs. Meigs gave a nod of satisfaction.
He wasn't losing any time.
She was startled, though, when Cecile kissed her. For
greeting and good-by, she expected it. But just to up and
kiss her! That was the kind of thing Cecile would do after
her wedding. Mrs. Meigs could fairly see her, tall and
strange in her white veil, with an armful of lilies, throw
ceremony aside as she came from the church door, and
demand, "Where's Caroline?"
They were making history. Cecile had brought thisyoung man around for her grandmother's approval. But
she would have gone ahead all the same without it. Mrs.
Meigs knew the symptoms.
She was glad to be back in the car. She needed a moment
to readjust herself. Tip and Cecile were stowing away the
picnic apparatus, casually, almost too casually.
"I'll drive home," said Cecile.
Tip yielded, and got in the back seat with Mrs. Meigs.
"I hope you aren't always going to let her have her own
way about everything," said Mrs. Meigs.
"It isn't a question of my own way this time," said
Cecile over her shoulder. "It's a question of everybody's
necks."
Her driving was a great improvement on Tip's. Mrs.
Meigs relaxed thoroughly. "It's been a wonderful day,"
she said.
"And we have the eggs against the next picnic,"
chuckled Tip.
"I'm sure your arrangements didn't need any help,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "With the radishes all looked over and
scrubbed, and the potatoes boiled in their jackets, all ready
to peel and fry."
"You must have got up early, to boil those potatoes,"
said Cecile. "It wasn't ten after twelve when you showed
up at the Country Club to get me."
"Oh, I didn't work this morning, so I had plenty of
time!" said Tip easily.
"I thought you worked half days Saturdays," returned
Cecile, her eyes on the road ahead.
"I did." Tip fumbled in his pockets. "Shall I light a
cigarette for you?"
He lighted it, and she put back a hand. Mrs. Meigs
smiled on the performance. Sort of a kiss at one remove,that was. Since they'd taken to smoking, girls had certain
advantages.
After a puff or two, Cecile said in a cool voice, "Do I
gather that your not working to-day means?"
"You've guessed it," said Tip, quite unabashed. "I quit."
"Again?" asked Cecile in a voice of ice.
"Lord, yes! I've held more different jobs! I don't mind
work so long as what I'm doing interests me. But when it
gets to be a bore—”
"It always bores you in about three weeks," said Cecile.
Tip grinned at Mrs. Meigs. "This time it was only two."
"Losing your job doesn't seem to worry you," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"Why should it?" Tip wanted to know. "I can al-
ways get another job."
"And get fired from that one too," snapped Cecile.
"Look here, Cecile," said Tip in a voice of calm reason.
"You needn't insult me. I never get fired. I quit."
There was a terrible silence. Mrs. Meigs was grateful
for the noise the engine made, though to get over awk-
ward. pauses there was nothing nowadays so good as
talking to the horse, the way her suitors used to.
"Have you and Cecile known each other very long?"
asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Long enough for her to catch on to me. Not long
enough for her to get used to me," said Tip.
Cecile certainly took his affairs to heart. He really was
quite irresponsible. But when they'd had such a beauti-
ful day, it was too bad to quarrel like this, right at the
end.
Cecile turned down Mrs. Meigs's street, drew up in
front of the building, began to search for her hat. "I'm
coming in with you, Caroline," she said."I'll go get rid of the picnic stuff," said Tip. "Saved
this evening for me, didn't you?"
"I did not," said Cecile. She turned on her heel and
walked into the apartment house. Tip stood looking for-
lornly after her.
"Come to see me some day," invited Mrs. Meigs.
"I'd like to come in right now," said Tip. "But I guess
I better not."
"No. Better not," advised Mrs. Meigs.
Young lovers always quarreled and made it up a dozen
times. It was a hopeful sign that Cecile cared enough to
quarrel with Tip. Usually she either tolerated her suitors
or got rid of them so neatly that they had nothing left
to quarrel about. She telephoned her own house now, and
asked whether there were any calls for her. Saturday, and
a popular girl. Of course there would be! Yet Mrs. Meigs's
heart sank when Cecile had received Millicent's report.
She said "Yes," and "Yes," and "Yes." But it was the
third number she chose to call back. Her voice was silken
as she said, "Hello, Roger! This is Cecile. Mother says
you called me. Yes, I just this minute got in.
Dinner, too? Yes, that will be fine. I'll be ready."
...VI
MRS. MEIGS was invited to Edith's for Sunday night
supper. Edith was her second daughter, Mrs. Edgar Lori-
mer. She was keen on the society end, and always did
things beautifully. Even for her mother on Sunday night,
she'd have bits of vegetable in jelly, and creamed lobster
in pie-crust cases, and small fancy cakes that would look
different but taste just alike. Mrs. Meigs took no chances.
She ate a hearty dinner at one o'clock.
She was about to establish herself at the front window,
to see whether any California cars would come past this
late in the season, when she heard the door-bell. A minute
later Anna was reassuring somebody, "No, she ain't got
anybody here. You come right in. I know she'll be glad
to see you."
Mrs. Meigs turned with a smile of pleasure. A little old
man, very neat in his white shirt with its high collar and
old-fashioned stiff cuffs, stood blinking in the bright
light.
"Hello, Cal Fletcher!" said Mrs. Meigs. "Come right in
and give an account of yourself. How are you, any-
how?"
"So's to sit up," returned Cal. "Not getting any
younger. Still, I manage."
He lowered himself to the edge of a chair. The stiffness
of his joints was accompanied by an absurd bashfulness.
Mrs. Meigs had been putting Cal Fletcher at his ease for
the last sixty years. But at the beginning of every visit,she had to start all over. In his eyes she was still the pretty
girl who had been the belle of their home town back in
Ohio; the girl who had refused him, and married a man
from out of town; Carrie Eaton, who had always laughed
at Cal Fletcher.
"Had your dinner?" asked Mrs. Meigs. Cal lived in a
boarding-house-a fortunate arrangement in his case.
It kept him from forgetting to go to meals.
"Yes. Oh, yes! We had our usual Sunday dumplings,"
said Cal.
"Got room for a piece of pie? Anna makes good pie."
"I'd like to. But maybe I better not," answered Cal.
She didn't urge him; his digestion wasn't all it might
be. "Seen the Sunday paper?" she asked.
"I read it this morning. There wasn't much in it," said
Cal plaintively. "Seems like there isn't as much news in
the papers as there used to be."
"There isn't, and there never was," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Lots of times I catch sight of something in some man's
paper in the street-car, and it looks awfully exciting.
But when I get home and look it up in my own paper,
it's awfully flat."
"You read people's papers over their shoulder in the
street-car?" asked Cal, rather scandalized.
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "The other day I was sitting
with a man and reading his paper; and every time before
he turned the page, he looked around to be sure I'd fin-
ished."
"Was it a young man?" asked Cal quickly.
"Not more than thirty, I should say."
"Oh, in that case-!" Cal looked relieved.
To give him time to get over his fidgets, she picked upher knitting and took a few stitches. It looked a little
queer to her, though. Pausing to inspect it, she discovered
that she'd been knitting on to the needle she should have
been knitting off. With an exclamation that wasn't at all
suitable for Sunday, she set to work to undo what she
had just done.
But as far as Cal was concerned, her knitting had
served its purpose. He got up his nerve to ask, "You doing
anything special this afternoon?"
"Not till supper-time."
"Like to come for a walk?"
"I would. It's a beautiful day."
"Yes, it's a wonder how the nice weather keeps up."
Cal took a fresh knot in his courage. "Seeing it's such a
fine afternoon, I thought maybe we might ride out to
Washington Park, and take our walk there."
"That's a splendid idea," said Mrs. Meigs. "Just wait
till I get my bonnet on."
They caught a street-car at the corner. As Cal ex-
tracted two nickels from his worn purse, Mrs. Meigs
happened to notice his hands. Knotted blue veins stood
out on the backs, and his shrunken fingers fumbled as
if he had half lost his sense of touch. Cal was beginning
to show his age. Living in a boarding-house the way he
did, he spent too much time alone.
They had to transfer, and it made a longish ride. But
when they reached the park, Mrs. Meigs was glad they'd
come. The grass was still green here, and the browning
oaks rustled in the breeze. Something nice about the way
an oak keeps its leaves all winter. It isn't a pretty tree,
but it seems to know its own mind better than the elms
and maples.They turned from the cement walk into a gravel path.
A pebble slipped under her foot, and her foot slipped too.
"You'd better take my arm," said Cal.
"No, thank you. When I get to the place where I need
help, I'll ask for it," said Mrs. Meigs.
Under his stubby white mustache Cal's lips parted in a
delighted grin. "Now if that isn't just like you, Carrie!
Remember the time I was walking home with you from
a party, when we were both about nineteen, and took
your elbow where the sidewalk was icy?"
"I suppose I objected."
"You said, 'I can walk by myself.'" Cal imitated the
tone of a girl snubbing a familiarity. "Just then you
stepped on an icy patch. Your feet shot out from under
you, and you fell flat on your back and struck your head.
It stunned you for a minute. I was scared out of my
senses. I thought you were dead."
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I remember now. When I came
to, I found my skirts all nicely smoothed down around
my feet. I spent all the next day wondering whether they
stayed that way when I fell, or whether you smoothed
them down."
The slow dull blush of old age showed under Cal's
skin. "I really don't remember," he said stiffly.
That meant he really did. Mrs. Meigs chuckled
naughtily.
Cal's embarrassment subsided. "I always used to call
for you after prayer-meeting. Remember?"
Mrs. Meigs made a face. "Those prayer-meetings were
the bane of my life. I wasn't a Methodist. My people were
Episcopalians, and not too much even of that. But be-
cause the Methodists couldn't afford to hire an organist,
I used to play for them. And it seemed to me that everyparty I ever wanted to go to came on Wednesday eve-
ning."
"You played the organ very well," said Cal politely.
"Oh, it played itself, almost! It was a funny little
cottage organ, which I had to pump with my feet. I used
almost to forget to make my fingers go, sometimes, I was
so busy watching the choir. There was an old gentle-
man with a goatee who used to sing bass. He'd go way
down cellar for his notes, and you couldn't understand a
single word he sang. He'd been courting the soprano for
thirty or forty years. After she got false teeth she used
to whistle through them, and sometimes on the high notes
her upper ones fell down. But nobody liked to come right
out and tell her that she was too old to sing soprano, and
she was deaf to hints."
Cal chuckled. "You always had the funniest way of
putting things, Carrie."
"I guess I was entitled to all the fun I got out of play-
ing that organ," said Mrs. Meigs. "When I'd been playing
the organ for five years, and me not even a member of the
church, old Miss Agnew got the idea that they ought to
buy me a present, at least."
"I should think they ought," said Cal.
"There was a big cribbage-board down in Saunders's
drug-store, and she knew I liked to play cribbage. So
she decided to take up a subscription among the congre-
gation, and buy the cribbage-board for me."
"It's the best game for two that there is," said Cal.
"But wouldn't the Methodists frown on cribbage? After
all, it's played with cards."
"Part of them frowned on that. Part of them didn't
see why I needed a present anyhow. Part of them thought
two dollars was too much to pay. But Mr. Saunders saidhe'd take fifty cents off the price of the board as his
contribution. It was cracked, anyhow. So Miss Agnew
stuck to it until she had collected the rest of the price,
in dimes and nickels."
"And she bought you a cracked board?" demanded
Cal in scandalized tones.
"I still have it. I can show it to you some day."
"You never told me about it at the time, Carrie."
"At the time, my feelings were hurt," acknowledged
Mrs. Meigs. "But somehow now it seems awfully funny."
"It would have been easier to get up a subscription
for you among the boys," said Cal. "Remember how they
used to gather in the evening in your sitting-room, and
sing? I guess maybe our singing wasn't always so good.
Sometimes your mother used to bring in the lemonade
before we were halfway through the song-book."
"My father used to go out in the hall and wind the
clock. Our hall clock had heavy weights to draw up. He
could make an awful noise with the weights if he thought
the company stayed too long."
"Once," confessed Cal, "I caught your father moving
the hands of the clock ahead half an hour. I was so em-
barrassed I didn't come again for two weeks."
"You always took things too much to heart, Cal. He
didn't mean you, specially."
"We used to sing some pretty songs, Carrie. " "Those
Endearing Young Charms,' and 'Ben Bolt,' and 'Oh, Fair
Dove! Oh, Fond Dove!' "
"Pretty gloomy songs, those were. I liked 'The Bonnets
of Bonnie Dundee' much better."
"Yes, I remember you did. Do you ever play nowa-
days, Carrie?""Never. I wasn't so good, even when I was young. But
all young ladies played the piano in those days."
"Those were wonderful evenings, in spite of your
father and the clock," said Cal wistfully. "I declare, it
seems to me that nowadays the young people don't know
how to have a good time."
"They wouldn't thank you for saying so. But do you
know," said Mrs. Meigs, "sometimes I feel that way about
it myself!"
A turn of the walk brought them in sight of a bench.
"Shall we sit down for a few minutes?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"The sun is nice and warm, and we're out of the wind
here."
"The refreshment pavilion is still open Sunday after-
noons," said Cal. "I thought if we walked on there, you
might like to take something."
She started to refuse; she could still touch her dinner.
The words died on her lips. She suddenly remembered
that Cal Fletcher was poor; he'd only enough income to
pay his board, and have his shoes half-soled and new
laces. He'd probably been saving for a week, to make a
treat for her to-day.
"I don't want anything to eat," she said gently. "But
I would like a glass of ginger-ale, if we could get it. I'm
very fond of ginger-ale, and I never quite like to order
it from the grocery. When they deliver a case, it looks so
awfully like beer coming in."
They found a table at the edge of the pavilion, where
they got the sunlight. "This is like having a box seat to
watch people pass," said Mrs. Meigs. "It's always fun to
watch people."
She watched the people passing. Cal watched her. Turn-ing to pick up her glass, Mrs. Meigs encountered his fixed
gaze. "Is my face dirty?" she asked pointedly.
"No. What made you think it was?"
"You're looking at me as if it was."
"I was just thinking how pretty you look with those
white ties under your chin," said Cal. "Like a little girl
in a sunbonnet."
"Cal Fletcher," said Mrs. Meigs sternly, "that ginger-
ale must have gone to your head!"
He flushed, and carefully averted his eyes. There! She'd
hurt his feelings. She'd kept up with the times so much
better than he had, it was hard for her to realize that in
his eyes she was still a girl; and in his day, a man paid a
girl compliments. Of course, she'd had so much in her
life that marked the passage of time for her: the children
and then the grandchildren. Cal was dreadfully alone
in the world. It was always easier for a woman to make
out by herself than it was for a man, anyhow.
"Cal," she said in her mildest tone, "my birthday
comes this week. It's Tuesday, to be exact."
"I know, you still celebrate birthdays," said Cal.
"If you can call it a celebration! I'm having my whole
family over for dinner."
Cal waited to be asked; she was on the point of asking
him. Then the thought of his poverty recurred to her.
If she asked him to her birthday party, Cal would scrape
together money to buy her a present, and then go with-
out his newspaper and cigars for a week to make up
for it.
"I'm not asking you to my birthday party," she said.
"Unfortunately, I have a talkative family. I don't see
how they came to be so talkative, being my family; but
they are. Sometimes when I've had them all around mefor a few hours, I'm so full of bottled-up conversation
that after they go home I go in my room and shut the
door and say to myself all the things that I didn't get
said to them."
"Not really, Carrie?"
"Well, if I don't, I'd like to. So I'm not asking you to
be drowned in the flood. But I want you to come to
dinner soon, some evening when there'll be just the
two of us."
"Oh, I'd like that! I'd like that much better," said
Cal.
"All right, then. Shall we set the day?"
"You're the one that has a lot of dates, Carrie. I can
come most any time."
"Tuesday company means Wednesday dinner will be
leavings," reckoned Mrs. Meigs. "Suppose you come
Thursday, then."
"All right, Thursday. What time?"
"Six o'clock sharp. Then we can have a nice long
evening."
Cal beamed at her. He was so shy, it would have been
nothing short of cruel to run him up against her whole
tribe. "Hurry and finish your ginger-ale," said Mrs.
Meigs. "I want to walk some more."
They emerged from the pavilion arm in arm, and
started back around the park on the other side. Mrs.
Meigs was admiring a group of copper beeches when she
felt his arm tighten in hers. If she hadn't had hold of
him, he might have scuttled to cover like a rabbit.
Mrs. Meigs looked around to see what had startled
him. Just ahead of them a man had stepped on the grass
and pulled a magnifying-glass from his pocket. He was
examining a bug that had lighted on a bush."Isn't that Dr. Manning?" whispered Cal.
"I suppose it is," said Mrs. Meigs. "It's been such a
nice day, something like that would have to happen."
"Perhaps we can get by before he sees us," whispered
Cal.
They had no such luck. The bug flew away, and Dr.
Manning turned in their direction. His face split in a
gigantic grin of recognition, and he waved his umbrella
at them.
Mrs. Meigs stuck her chin in the air and stepped out as
if she had been sent for. But Dr. Manning walked right
in front of them. "Hello, you two!" he hailed. He was
quite deaf, and talked loud enough so that he heard
himself. "Darby-and-Joaning as usual, hey?"
Cal looked embarrassed. But Mrs. Meigs might never
have heard of that celebrated example of fidelity. "We're
taking a brisk walk," she said firmly. "A very brisk walk.
Watch our dust, if you like."
"Oh, I could keep up with you if I tried!" shouted Dr.
Manning. "But I wouldn't think of trying. Three's a
crowd, eh?"
"Yes, it is sometimes," said Mrs. Meigs.
Dr. Manning guffawed. "I'd have to get up early in
the morning to get ahead of you, little lady!"
Mrs. Meigs looked her scorn, not only of his endearing
phrase, but of his umbrella on a clear day, and the ravel-
ings on his cuffs, and the shine he'd needed for the last
month. She couldn't see why, merely because he was old,
a man need let himself get so sloppy.
As they sailed past him, however, she was oppressively
conscious of his bulk. She had always been a "little lady,"
but she didn't care to be addressed that way. For theproper snubbing of Dr. Manning, she wished she were
seven feet tall.
A few yards down the path, Cal chuckled. "Manning's
getting pretty feeble," he remarked with gusto. "Pretty
feeble!"
Mrs. Meigs looked sharply around at him, but she held
her tongue. Even to her prejudiced eye, Cal was the
feebler of the two. But this would be a poor world, if
everybody couldn't find somebody else to pity.
When they got back to her flat, Cal wouldn't come in.
He had a nervous dread of being in the way. Anna was
out, and Mrs. Meigs had the place to herself. With an
hour clear before Edith would send the car for her, she
suddenly felt sleepy, coming in that way out of the open
air.
She lay down on the couch in the living-room, and
pulled over her a wool "slumber robe" that she had
knitted for her husband to take his Sunday afternoon
naps under.
Bright as the day had been, it was fading already.
Well, that was what you had to expect in October. It
meant nice long evenings, anyhow, when you lighted
the lamps early, and felt how cozy you were.
The flat was warm, and she hadn't thought to open a
window. She didn't get up to do it. How she hated a nap
when it was prescribed for her: Millicent saying, "Mother,
I'm sure this has been too much for you, and you really
must lie down for a while!" But how luxurious it was to
drop off to sleep for a few minutes this way, with the
window panes growing faint and then dark, and the
flat so quiet you could hear the clock ticking two rooms
away!The arrival of Edith's car waked her. She had only to
put on her bonnet and coat. In the car she yawned
luxuriously. The chauffeur carefully tucked up her feet.
She waited until his back was turned before she kicked
them free again.
Edith lived on the best street in town. Her house was
small but expensive. Everything in it always looked
new; and to be sure, most of it was. About the time Mrs.
Meigs was beginning to get used to a piece of furniture,
Edith decided that it was out of fashion, and replaced it.
A maid opened the door, but Edith came into the hall
to greet her mother. Between Millicent and Natalie in
age, she hadn't the looks of either; but her grooming
was miraculous. She looked less like flesh and blood than
like something that had been polished and then var-
nished.
"Will you take off your bonnet here, or do you want
to go upstairs?" she asked.
"I always like to lay my bonnet on the bed at a party,"
said Mrs. Meigs, cocking an ear toward the living-room
door. "I guess this is a party, isn't it?"
"It's just Dr. Stuart," said Edith. "His wife's in New
York, and I thought you wouldn't mind if I asked him
over for supper."
"I don't mind how often I see Dr. Stuart," said Mrs.
Meigs, "so long as I don't see him professionally."
Edith took her up to the guest-room, and turned on a
lamp. The light was reflected from shiny rectangular
surfaces, in colors that shouted louder than deaf Dr.
Manning.
"I've just had the guest-room done over in modern-
istic," Edith explained. "I thought if this was a success,
I'd have the rest of the house done too.""That's a good idea," said Mrs. Meigs. "Try it on your
guests first, and see if they can stand it."
Edith smiled. "Don't you like it?"
"It reminds me of the operating-room when Cecile
had her tonsils out," said Mrs. Meigs. "Only the operating-
room had at least the decency to stay white."
Edith shrugged. "I'm not sure I like it myself. But it's
the way they're doing things this year."
"The same furniture that was comfortable last year
is comfortable this," said Mrs. Meigs. "But I suppose only
an old fogy would think about such a small matter as
comfort."
Dr. Stuart was a man about fifty, who had been Mrs.
Meigs's family physician ever since the death of his
father, "the old doctor." The old doctor had worn a
Vandyke beard, a frock-coat, a silk hat; the young one
took pains to look like any business man. Indeed in
Edith's living-room he looked amazingly like Edgar,
Edith's husband. They might have been cut off the same
piece: standard stuff, but monotonous. It flashed through
Mrs. Meigs's mind, "Tip O'Neil won't look like that,
even when he's fifty."
The supper was what she'd expected. Everything was
encased in jelly, or laid on toast, or disguised under a
sharp, strange-tasting sauce. Just the same Mrs. Meigs
had developed an appetite, being all afternoon in the
open air.
"I suppose you try to get all the supper you can, before
the telephone rings and you're called out on a case," she
said.
"I never answer calls Sunday evening," said Dr. Stuart.
"In fact I've given up night work entirely. It takes too
much out of a man."The old doctor would answer a call any time. It made
you feel better the minute he came into the room. He
carried the worries of his patients as well as their illnesses.
He had died during an epidemic of grippe, on his feet
and making his rounds the day before the end came.
She must have been thinking about the old doctor;
anyhow she was a slow eater. The next thing she knew
everybody else had finished; they were waiting for her.
She looked around with a smile.
"In my youth," she said, "this would have embar-
rassed me. Many is the time I've come away from the
table hungry, because I suddenly realized that I was the
only one still eating."
Dr. Stuart laughed, and helped himself to more aspic
to keep her company. Probably he wanted it anyhow.
It must be awful to be a doctor, and have everybody act
shocked if you ate until you were good and full.
He was bent on enjoying his evening out. When they
got back to the living-room he asked, "Do you still play
bridge, Mrs. Meigs?"
"I never did, if you ask some of my friends," she an-
swered. "But I still play at it."
"We might have a game now," said Edith.
"Your mother doesn't object to playing on Sunday?"
asked Dr. Stuart.
"If I have scruples, I conceal them admirably," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"Then you be my partner," he said.
"I'd love to," said Mrs. Meigs. "But I think on prin-
ciple, husband and wife should never be allowed to play
partners."
Dr. Stuart played with Edith; but he argued as heartily
as if he had been married to her. He doubled a two bid,and she accepted his double. Without waiting for the
hand to be played, he informed her that his double was
informative. Edith told him only a one-double was in-
formative. He explained at great length the merits of an
informative two-double.
Mrs. Meigs listened with interest. She didn't agree with
him at all; she thought you ought to go ahead and bid
your cards. But this would be lovely to spring on her
old ladies the next time she played with them. Wouldn't
Mrs. Cronkhite turn purple at this fresh outrage!
In the middle of the game, she thought suddenly,
"Here I am having such a good time, and Cal probably
had watery prunes, and canned baked beans for supper.
And they've nothing but gas-lights in that boarding-
house of his."
It was partly Cal's fault, though, if he didn't have a
better time. He shrank from meeting new people, and
he was always yawning at nine o'clock. Mrs. Meigs her-
self felt a yawn coming, but she closed her lips resolutely
until the feeling passed.
Then she saw that they were all waiting for her bid.
She hadn't heard the previous bids, but she had two
aces in her hand.
"Double," she said firmly. "And when I say double,
I mean it."
"You would," said Dr. Stuart with a grin. "That or
anything else you say. So I redouble."
Mrs. Meigs twinkled at him. The old doctor had been
a saint, but he always smelled of iodoform and talked in
polysyllables. Anyhow saints missed all the fun.
"Is that an informative redouble?" asked Mrs. Meigs.VII
TUESDAY morning was cloudy, and there was a raw
wind. You couldn't expect the sun to shine forever.
Anyhow Mrs. Meigs was too busy to pay much attention
to the weather. This was the day of her family dinner.
Her ordering had been done the day before, and the
flat cleaned. Anna stuffed the turkey, and began to make
a cake: a double recipe, as befitted a birthday cake. Mrs.
Meigs's first task after breakfast was to take down the
best dishes and wash them. No hand but hers was ever
allowed to touch that sacred set; and it had to be washed
both before and after a party.
She had just begun to count up what she'd need when
the door-bell rang. "I'll answer the bell this morning,"
she called to Anna. "You keep right on with what you're
doing."
Red hair gleamed at her from the threshold, and her
hand was wrung so hard she winced. "Come right in, Tip
O'Neil," she said cordially. "You're an early bird."
"Thought I might manage to get some news of Cecile,"
said Tip. "Every time I've phoned her house since Satur-
day I've been told she wasn't there."
"She probably wasn't. She goes flashing around until
no one can keep track of her."
"But doesn't she drop in here pretty often?"
"Indeed she does," said Mrs. Meigs proudly. "Sunday
I wasn't here myself, and yesterday she played in a golfmatch both morning and afternoon. She won both times;
she telephoned me in the evening. She's playing again
to-day; so you see that is keeping her pretty busy. I ex-
pect her for dinner, though. I'm having all the family
here to celebrate my eightieth birthday."
Tip grinned, and straddled a chair. "A family party!
Must you? On your birthday, too! But I wish I'd known
this was your birthday. I'd have brought you a present.
A quart of likker, or a book you'd have to hide from
Cecile's mother."
He'd met Millicent, then. Family loyalty kept Mrs.
Meigs from confessing to him how many things she'd
had to hide from Cecile's mother. But she did permit
herself to say, "I believe I'd like the presents you picked
out. I've always loved presents. My husband used to
tease me about the way I never refused anything. He
said 'No' was a word I had never learned. But somehow
I don't care much about the presents that people give
old ladies."
"I've heard that in China it's considered the height of
good form to give an old person a coffin," said Tip cheer-
fully. "But of course in an apartment-"
Mrs. Meigs's rocking-chair creaked with the violence
of her rocking. "There are cheaper ways of saying the
same thing. You can say it with a nice warm shoulder
wrap, or a new hot-water bag. Or with bed socks. Bed
socks! Or a new spectacle case, or a book with large print
and lots of pictures. I don't mind the books so much,
though. I can always exchange them for something I
really want to read."
"Ask me to your party," suggested Tip, "and I'll bring
you something that will set the whole tribe by the ears."
"They'll be by the ears enough without your settingthem. Or not by the ears exactly, but they haven't much
in common any more."
"There's always the coal," said Tip cheerfully. "I've
been to family parties myself, back in Iowa where I come
from. It seems to me I was never at one where they didn't
talk about how much coal they burned, or rather how
little."
"I don't mind hearing about the coal," acknowledged
Mrs. Meigs. "It's hearing about everybody's illnesses that
drives me to distraction."
"And having them inquire about your health?" sug-
gested Tip.
"Yes. As if my health were somehow my fault."
"I think," said Tip, "I'll take back what I said about
wanting to be asked. But my strong right arm is at your
service. Don't you want the dining-table lengthened,
or something?"
"Anna always is awkward about putting in the extra
leaves," Mrs. Meigs acknowledged.
Tip sprang to his feet. "Lead me to it!" he commanded.
He lengthened the dining-table in a jiffy. "Any other
reaching or lifting?" he asked.
Mrs. Meigs drew a long breath. Then she favored him
with the supreme proof of confidence. "I think I'll let
you get down the best dishes for me. They're on the top
shelf, there in the corner cupboard. I'll need eleven of
everything."
He had counted eleven dinner plates, and handed them
carefully down to her, when the door-bell rang. "I can
manage here alone, if you want to see who that is," he
said.
It was Cecile, with her arms full of chrysanthemums,
lavender and gold and maroon and cream-color. Mrs.Meigs looked behind her in the hall. "You haven't got that
flower buyer with you, I hope."
"He's down in the car, with the flower box," Cecile
admitted with a twinkle. "No use bringing up the box,
you know. I just came to give you these, and greet you
on your birthday."
"Now that was lovely of you," said Mrs. Meigs. "But
you might have waited until to-night, having that golf
game to play this morning."
"I am excited about the game," Cecile admitted.
"Set your mind on winning, have you?"
"Not only that." Cecile threw back her head. "I feel
as if I couldn't lose. The match to-day is only the semi-
final; but in it I meet this summer's champion. If I win
to-day, that will give me a walkaway in the finals to-
morrow. Of course it's a post-season match. But every-
body's frightfully interested in it."
"Then everybody will be there to see you win," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"I wish you'd include yourself in that," said Cecile.
"Couldn't you manage to get away and watch me play
the first few holes? Roger's chauffeur would bring you
back when you got tired."
"Oh, my dear, I'd love it!" Mrs. Meigs made a mental
calculation. "But no, I'm afraid I can't. Anna's a good
cook, but if I took my eye off her to-day, and then
something went wrong-"
"I'm afraid you're right," said Cecile reluctantly. She
glanced at her wrist-watch. "I've just time to put these
in water for you. The little gold fellows will do for the
center of the table, and the tall ones will make two nice
bunches for the living-room."
She got as far the dining-room door; there she droppedthree flowers. Tip, standing on a chair in front of the
corner cupboard, was grinning down at her. He spoke to
Mrs. Meigs over Cecile's head. "I like this set! It reminds
me of one my grandmother brought over from Ireland."
"I'm saving those dishes as a wedding present for
Cecile," said Mrs. Meigs.
"It would be almost worth marrying Cecile to get
these dishes," said Tip.
Cecile threw her flowers down on the dining-table.
"You're making yourself right at home, Tip," she said
coldly.
Tip stepped down from the chair. "I came here look-
ing for you. Aren't I lucky to have found you!"
"It's hardly worth the effort," said Cecile. "I can't stop
a minute."
"But darling, you might give me a chance to ex-
plain-"
"I might not," interrupted Cecile. "I've listened to
enough of your explanations, one time and another.
You'd talk the clock right off the church-steeple. But
that's all there is to it. Talk!"
Tip approached her. Mrs. Meigs opened the door as
quietly as she could, but Cecile heard her. "Don't go,
Caroline," she said without turning her head.
Mrs. Meigs glanced apologetically at Tip. Indifferent
whether she went or stayed, he walked straight up to
Cecile and laid his hands on her shoulders. Cecile's eyes
fell before his.
"Look at me!" said Tip. All the flippancy had gone out
of him. His voice was hoarse with earnestness.
Cecile pushed his hands away. "Don't try to begin all
over again. I'm sick of it. I'm sick of you."
"Look at me," said Tip steadily, "and say that again."She looked at him, and her color rose. "Say what?"
she stammered.
"Have you forgotten?" whispered Tip.
She had. Looking into his eyes, she had forgotten
everything but him. She lifted a trembling hand, and
laid it against his cheek.
Tip took the hand and kissed it, palm and back and
wrist. "When I woke this morning, I was hungry for the
sight of you," he whispered.
Cecile tried to swallow past the lump in her throat.
Her lips were trembling, and her eyes half shut. Mrs.
Meigs hadn't known she could look like that.
"Every morning when I wake," whispered Tip, "the
same hunger is on me. For the sight and touch of you.
It's like touching a flower. It's like seeing a star."
Cecile smiled faintly, and teased, "You're quoting
poetry." Then her eyelids fluttered and fell.
"What need would I have to quote poetry, when it's
you that are poetry?" Tip seized her in his arms, and
kissed her full on the lips.
This was the moment for Mrs. Meigs to slip out un-
noticed. But as she fumbled for the door-knob, unwilling
to leave, yet ashamed to intrude any longer, Cecile drew
away from him suddenly.
"How does it happen that you aren't at work to-day?
Or at least looking for work?" she demanded.
"How would I have the heart to go looking for work,
when you won't even answer the telephone when I call?"
"Where did you go Saturday after you left me?" in-
sisted Cecile in a voice of steel.
Mrs. Meigs ceased to feel for the door-knob. They
might not want an audience, but at this rate they'd soon
need a peacemaker.Tip's teasing mood had come back. "Now who is it
wants to talk?" he demanded.
"You haven't answered me. Where did you go Satur-
day night?"
"By that you mean, what did I do? Well, what does
any man do when his best girl shows him the door?"
"You got drunk that night," said Cecile, colder and
colder.
But Tip began to warm up. "Yes, I did," he said in-
dignantly. "It isn't the first time in my life that I ever
got drunk. And it won't be the last time, if you go on
tormenting me."
Cecile turned her back on him, and said over her
shoulder, "First you quit your job. Then you get drunk.
Then you decide there's no hurry about working any-
how. It's a great life you're leading."
Tip laid a hand on her arm. "It's no life at all. But
what life can I have without you? I can work when I
want to, and my drinking is nothing. But without you
I'm a ship without a rudder."
"You said that Saturday." Cecile looked at her watch.
"I mustn't be late for my match."
"That's right. You have a match this morning. It'll
save time if I run you out to the Country Club. Unless
maybe you'd be ashamed to be seen at the Country
Club stepping down from such a rusty fender?"
"This would be your chance to stage a really good
accident, I know," said Cecile. "But I've already made
arrangements to be driven out to the Club."
"With that fathead Kane?" asked Tip in a voice of
thunder.
"Don't call names," said Cecile primly."You like to be seen with him. You think he's a
feather in your cap."
"He's a successful man," said Cecile with a shrug.
"But surely that's nothing against him."
"Who said it was?" Tip looked astonished. "Let him
be as successful as he likes. But oh, darling, you don't
care for that fathead, do you?"
"Why shouldn't I?" asked Cecile. "After all, it's just
as easy to fall in love with a rich man as with a poor
one."
"Is it the man a girl falls in love with, or is it his bank
account?"
"It's his bank account she marries," said Cecile
crisply. "I guess I know. There's my poor Dad always
worrying about money, and his expensive family spend-
ing what he hasn't got. I've had enough of that in my
life. Love wears out anyhow. But money lasts. Money
smooths the rough places. Money makes you somebody."
"You mercenary little cat!" said Tip, half amused
and half furious. "Mrs. Meigs, you should have taught
her better."
"I didn't have a free hand with Cecile," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"She'd let the stars go out, while she lights a penny
dip," said Tip scornfully. "I can't waste any more of my
time with a girl like that." He climbed back on the
chair in the corner, and got very busy with the dishes.
Cecile looked as if she would like to say something
more, but she was properly snubbed. She walked out
of the dining-room, quite forgetting the flowers she
had thrown down on the table, and not even picking
up the three she had dropped on the floor.Mrs. Meigs followed her to the hall door. "Is Richard
coming in for the dinner to-night?" she asked.
"I suppose so. I reminded him of it when he was here
last week." Cecile spoke mechanically, looking down at
the floor.
"Darling, a man is different when he's married. It
improves lots of 'em," said Mrs. Meigs.
Cecile twitched her shoulders, and thrust back the
stray lock that was always escaping from her hat. "I've
heard that somewhere before. But it's too big a chance
to take."
"Well, you don't have to decide this morning. You
have other things to think about."
Cecile looked at her blankly. "What other things?
Oh, yes, the match! I've got to win a golf match, and
do myself proud, haven't I?" A single sob escaped her.
"I'd like to make you a birthday present of my victory.
Only I don't see how I'm to keep my mind on the damn
game."
"You must have a little patience with Tip, darling,"
cautioned Mrs. Meigs. "Don't let him upset you so."
"A little patience? How long does he expect me to sit
around and wait for him?" demanded Cecile wrath-
fully.
"Darling, you haven't sat around."
"I should hope not, for the likes of him! Don't
you worry, Caroline. I know what I'm about." Cecile
kissed Mrs. Meigs hastily, and dashed off, slamming the
door.
Mrs. Meigs marched back to the dining-room. "Why
do you stay fooling with those dishes, when your girl
has gone off with another man?"
"To get in strong with you," said Tip, unabashed."Also because I'd rather attend to anybody else's busi-
ness than to my own." He began to collect the scattered
chrysanthemums. "Maybe I'd better put these in the
bathtub for the present? Flowers keep well in a lot of
water."
He hadn't got beyond selecting a small flower that
would be nice for his button-hole when the door-bell
rang. "Maybe that's Cecile come back for me," he whis-
pered with a grin. He rushed to open the door.
Then his face changed comically. He turned to an-
nounce, "No, it isn't Cecile exactly. It's Cecile's
mother."
Mrs. Meigs smoothed out her face hastily, and glanced
about the room for telltale signs that she had been en-
joying herself. How she'd been enjoying herself didn't
matter, with Millicent. It was the principle of the thing
that Millicent objected to.
"Good morning, Mother," said Millicent, with her
little peck at Mrs. Meigs's cheek. "Many happy returns
of the day."
"Not too many," said Mrs. Meigs rebelliously. "You
know Tip O'Neil, don't you?"
"You should remember me, Mrs. Graham," said Tip
with his ready grin. "I've had you on the telephone a
dozen times the last three days."
"Oh, yes, of course, Mr. O'Brien!" said Millicent.
"Now, Mother, I've an important committee meeting
at eleven, but until then I'm quite at your service."
"Sit down, then, and tell me all the news," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"Oh, I didn't come to gossip! I came to help. Let me
see. I think I have just time to make your birthday
cake for you.""My birthday cake is already in the oven," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"Then I'll just run out and see what I can help
about." Millicent started toward the kitchen door.
"Anna doesn't care about having people come into
the kitchen when she's cooking," warned Mrs. Meigs.
"I dare say she doesn't," said Millicent. "I dare say
too she has plenty of reason."
Mrs. Meigs hesitated, trying to think of a broader
hint. Tip came to the rescue. "You mustn't be late for
your committee meeting," he reminded Millicent.
"I'm never late for anything," said Millicent with a
withering glance.
Instead of withering, Tip beamed on her. "How few
of us can say that! I suppose you plan your time
ahead?"
"Exactly! Only yesterday one of the ladies on a com-
mittee with me was saying, 'Mrs. Graham, I don't see
how you manage to accomplish so much outside.' Be-
cause of course I have my own home to manage, and I
always arrange to save some time for my mother."
"That is remarkable," said Tip. "I was thinking, see-
ing I'm just leaving, maybe you'd like to have me drive
you down town."
Millicent thawed further. "Oh, have you a car, Mr.
O'Brien?"
"Mr. O'Neil," Mrs. Meigs corrected. Not that it
would do any good. If Millicent decided that Tip's
name was O'Brien, O'Brien it would remain, so far as
she was concerned.
But Tip ignored the insult of mixing the clans. He
beckoned Millicent to the window. "See with your owneyes if I have a car. No, not that snooty Cadillac. The
old bus leaning against the hydrant."
Millicent took one look, and pronounced judgment.
"After all, a taxi isn't expensive."
Tip was enjoying himself. "But I can go much faster
than a taxi dares to," he argued. "You'd never believe
the old bone-shaker could go around corners on two
wheels the way she does."
"Really, I can't put you to so much trouble," said
Millicent.
"You might take a chance," struck in Mrs. Meigs.
"Tip isn't what I'd call a good driver. But he'll probably
get you down town without a bad accident."
Millicent looked uncertainly from one to the other of
them. She was a born commander, and could put down
insubordination with a firm hand. But when people
began saying things that they surely couldn't mean, she
felt the ground slipping beneath her feet.
The door-bell rang again. Tip, who had made him-
self quite at home, went to answer. He admitted a very
thin woman, with her lips painted until they looked as
if she had been drinking blood, and her eyelashes beaded
with black.
"Another relative!" exclaimed Mrs. Meigs. "The
party isn't until to-night. Don't you people understand
that?"
The newcomer smiled. She wore long dangling ear-
rings that were quite out of place with her tweed suit;
but she had some salt of humor. "I got bored, and came
out for a walk. When I passed here, I thought I'd just
drop in and see if there wasn't something I could do to
help.""There isn't a thing you can do, Estelle. Thank you
just the same. I want you to meet Mr. O'Neil, a good
friend of mine." Mrs. Meigs put that in for Millicent's
benefit. "Tip, this is my son's wife. Mrs. Jack, I prefer
to have her called. If people ever get to calling her
young Mrs. Meigs, that will make me old Mrs. Meigs.
Even on her eightieth birthday, a woman doesn't like to
be called 'old,' just like that!"
"I'm sure I never think of you as old," said Estelle
politely. "Haven't you a single odd job for me? Flowers
to arrange, or silver to clean, or even nuts to crack? I
would love to tie on an apron, and get busy in a kitchen
for an hour. It's a chance I don't often have."
"The silver was cleaned yesterday," said Mrs. Meigs
firmly. "Even the turkey stuffing is made."
"I hope you didn't allow that maid of yours to ruin
the stuffing by putting onion in," said Millicent.
"I didn't allow her to put onion in. I ordered her to
do it. A little onion improves anything," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Except perhaps ice cream."
"I think we're all asked out, Mrs. Jack," said Tip.
"Like to take the rest of your walk in my car? I'm driv-
ing Mrs. Graham down town."
"I didn't agree to that. You'd get me there too early,
and that's almost as bad as being late." Conflicting pas-
sions strove in Millicent's breast. Economy won. "But
seeing you have your car right here, maybe I won't
bother with a taxi."
Tip politely allowed the ladies to pass out ahead of
him. Then he turned, and winked at Mrs. Meigs. She
shaped with her lips the words, "Thanks awfully."
Smiling broadly, she watched from a front windowwhile Tip removed the jack and an oily rag from the
back seat, and stowed Millicent and Estelle away there.
If he didn't quail at the idea of Millicent as a mother-
in-law, red-haired people were all they were cracked up
to be, and more.
That was a good idea of Tip's, to keep the flowers
fresh in the bathtub until she had time to arrange them.
As she picked up the armful, with their strong autum-
nal fragrance, Mrs. Meigs thought with a qualm, maybe
she should have let Estelle help with the flowers.
She didn't like Estelle any too well; but she had to
admit that Estelle seemed to suit Jack. They lived in a
hotel; Estelle passed her days in a haze of cigarette
smoke, and subscribed to all the magazines that tell
what's being shown at the Paris dressmakers' openings,
and who's wintering at Palm Beach. A nice picture Es-
telle would make in a kitchen, with her eyelash black
and those earrings!
But she had looked almost wistful when she made
her offer of help. After all, too bright a lip-stick
wasn't a crime. Maybe at the bottom of her heart Es-
telle was beginning to weary of an easy life, and long
for something a little more worth while. Maybe if Mrs.
Meigs spoke to Jack and gave him a hint-
She drew up short, smiling at her own folly. Surely
she ought to know by this time that the things she her-
self liked didn't mean the same to everybody. But life,
as Cecile had once remarked, was like a very bad golf
course. When you were nearing the end with a good
score, you couldn't help being sorry for the people who
were struggling through the rough half-way along.
She mustn't get to be an interfering old busybody,though. Estelle wasn't getting the most out of her life.
But if she really wanted a home of her own, the first
step toward getting it was to make up her own mind.
Mrs. Meigs went to "look out" the best napkins.VIII
SHE let the flowers go until she had attended to every-
thing else. As she finished arranging them, the clock
struck five. Mrs. Meigs went to her room to change her
dress, and looked longingly at the bed. She would have
liked to lie down for half an hour, but she could imagine
too well the clamor if she were caught lying down.
"Mother! Don't you feel well? Have you been overdoing?
I'm afraid the care of a flat is too much for you. Any-
how you ought not to have company." Mrs. Meigs made
a face at herself in the mirror.
Edith and her husband were the first guests to arrive.
Edith carried a package wrapped in white tissue-paper,
which she was careful to leave in the hall. Then Millicent
came steaming in ahead of her painfully quiet Ralph.
"We decided not to wait for Cecile," she announced.
"She's likely to be late, with her golf and all."
Ralph opened his mouth to say something, and shut
it again. That was the usual fate of Ralph's remarks when
his wife was around: either he didn't get them said, or
he wished he hadn't.
Natalie and Carter came next. Natalie was looking
lovely to-night, in a second-hand dress that became her
better than it ever had Edith, who always expected Nat-
alie to wear out her old clothes for her. Carter was
sulky; but then Carter so often was sulky. He offered
no explanation of his abrupt telephone call the other
ΙΟΙnight. Mrs. Meigs wanted to ask when and where he had
located Natalie; but she bit her tongue, and didn't speak.
Five minutes late, which was ten minutes sooner than
Mrs. Meigs had expected them, came Estelle and Jack.
Estelle was in full evening dress, and clanked with
bracelets.
But the hands of the clock crept along from the hour
to the half-hour, without bringing the grandchildren.
Half an hour was long enough to keep dinner waiting,
even for them. Mrs. Meigs led the way to the dining-
room, though she was acutely conscious of the two va-
cant places.
Anna brought in the raw oysters, carefully embedded
in ice. "Seems to me Cecile is always late," said Edith.
Mrs. Meigs waited for Millicent to explain about
Cecile's golf. But what Millicent said was, "Cecile is punc-
tual enough at home. But Mother spoils her so dreadfully.
Cecile is always coming here to spend the night, and I
suppose that means she stays out as late as she pleases."
"She does that anyhow," said Mrs. Meigs. "So what's
the good of nagging her about it?"
Jack hastily changed the subject. "I didn't see an eve-
ning paper. How did Cecile come out in her match?"
"She telephoned me at the office," began Cecile's father.
He got no further. "She telephoned you and not me?"
demanded Millicent. "Now why on earth should she do
that?"
"Why shouldn't she?" asked Mrs. Meigs. "A girl is at
liberty to telephone her father whenever she pleases. Well,
Ralph, did she say how she came out?"
"She was playing the regular champion," answered
Ralph. "This is a post-season match, you know. Cecile
said the score was tied at the sixteenth hole.""Bully for 'Cile!" said Jack. "She put up a good fight,
then. It must have been an exciting match."
"But who won?" demanded Mrs. Meigs.
"The other woman won," said Ralph. "What could
you expect?"
Mrs. Meigs had expected Cecile to win. Jack and Ralph
thought she was doing well to come so close, and they
knew more about golf than Mrs. Meigs did. But this
morning Cecile had seemed so confident! Confident, that
is, until after her interview with Tip. There was an awful
struggle going on inside of that girl. But why didn't she
go ahead and win her game, if only to show Tip that she
could?
Anna took out the oyster shells, and brought in the
turkey. Just as she was setting it down for Jack to carve,
Cecile slipped in. She was wearing an orchid evening
gown and silver slippers, and her short hair stood out
about her head in a little golden ruff. She stooped to lay
her cheek against her grandmother's; her face was fever-

sembled relatives.
"I suppose you're sorry to be so late," said Edith.
"Thank you for mentioning it for me, Aunt Edith,"
retorted Cecile.
The meal as a meal was successful. Erect at the head
of the table, her wrinkled cheeks flushed with excite-
ment. Mrs. Meigs kept an eye on everybody's plate. She
liked to see people eat; and except Estelle, who was thin
on principle, her guests ate royally. Mrs. Meigs began to
feel easier. Her children were nice people; it gave her
singular pleasure to feel that she had started the careers
of all these big solid middle-aged citizens.
Cecile in the rôle of youngest grandchild dished themolded ice-cream. Mrs. Meigs cut the huge birthday
cake. Coffee was served in cups that held a full half-pint.
Estelle fitted a cigarette into a holder nine inches long.
Millicent glared at it. Mrs. Meigs wondered if Estelle
hadn't brought along that holder just to make Millicent
glare.
She shooed her guests into the living-room while she
went to help Anna with the clearing up. Cecile followed
her. "I blew up at the eighteenth hole this afternoon,
Caroline," she said. "I had that woman beaten, but at the
end, I wilted."
"I'm afraid you were thinking about your upset with
Tip this morning," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Upset is Tip's middle name," said Cecile bitterly.
"But blaming Tip is too easy. I can't hold him responsible,
if I haven't any staying power."
"Staying power comes with the years," said Mrs.
Meigs.
"If it comes at all," said Cecile. "Oh, well! You'll
excuse me if I leave a little early to-night, Caroline?
I'm going dancing with Roger Kane."
"I don't want you to stay a single minute after you
want to leave. But aren't you too tired to-night for

"I'm too tired to keep still," said Cecile. "Want me to
help you with the sacred dishes?"
"You're dressed for dish-washing, aren't you?" said
Mrs. Meigs. "No, the dishes can wait. I'll do them to-
night when I'm alone. Cecile, did you hear from Richard
that he wasn't coming to-night?"
"I expected to find him here. Something must have
come up that kept him in Madison."
"Yes. I dare say I shall hear from him to-morrow,"said Mrs. Meigs briskly. But she didn't dare to catch
Cecile's eye as she said it. She had come first with the
grandchildren for so many years; it was hard to face
the fact that they were both getting to the point where
other people would come ahead of her.
Mrs. Meigs followed Cecile as far as the door of the
living-room. Her guests were all sitting in a circle.
That must herald the bringing in of the presents from
the hall. Mrs. Meigs wondered which one of them had
chosen to bestow a new hot-water bag on her. Maybe
they got together and drew lots. Wouldn't it be a joke,
though, if they'd all brought hot-water bags!
She paused to compose her features into an expression
of suitable gratification. Manners were manners. In the
pause she became aware that they were not talking about
hot-water bags.
"This is just what we always do," Jack was saying.
"We all get together on Mother's birthday and tell each
other that she ought not to go on living alone."
"Indeed she ought not to," chimed in Millicent. "It
isn't safe for one thing; and it's too much bother for her.
I noticed she hardly ate a thing to-night. Getting up such
a dinner is altogether too hard for a woman her age. She
will probably be in bed to-morrow to pay for it."
"It was a darn good dinner," protested Natalie's hus-
band. He had thawed unexpectedly in the last hour.
"That isn't the point," said Edith. "Mother keeps up
wonderfully for her years. But she's old. There's no get-
ting around that."
"We all talk about it," insisted Jack. "But we never do
anything except talk."
"We are going to do something this time," snapped
Edith. "I had thought of getting Dr. Stuart to say some-thing to her about taking things easier. After he played
bridge with her the other night at our house, I spoke
to him about Mother. But he just laughed and said he
hoped he had half her pep when he got to her age, and
her arteries must be in pretty good condition."
"Dr. Stuart never takes any responsibility," pro-
nounced Millicent.
"He's wise not to, maybe," said Jack. "Anyhow we
don't need his help. Our minds are all made up, aren't
they?" He looked at Natalie.
"Luckily it isn't a question of money," faltered Nat-
alie. Poor child, in her life there had been nothing that
didn't sooner or later turn into a question of money.
"It was quite ridiculous, of course, the way Father left
everything he had to Mother," said Millicent. "But then
he always did spoil her dreadfully. He gave her her own
way in everything."
"It wasn't ridiculous when he did it," said Jack. "His
money wouldn't have gone far, divided among all of us.
He knew it would keep one person very comfortably.
What he didn't know, of course, was that Mother would
survive him for so many years."
"No, of course he didn't. It's just a question of what
is best for her now," said Natalie.
"It's best for her to come to me," said Millicent.
"There's that bedroom we don't use on the third floor,
and we can have an extra bathroom put in especially for
her, if she doesn't care to use the servants."
"You and I might take turns having her," said Edith.
"I haven't any third story, but she's welcome to the use
of my guest-room. I've just had it done over, so it will
be quite fresh for her."
All her children had spoken. They were all arrayedagainst her. For a ghastly minute Mrs. Meigs imagined
their program carried out and herself established in Mil-
licent's third story, or in that purple operating-room at
Edith's. There would be no reading in the bathtub then,
no coffee at night, no prowling about in the rain. Her
children meant kindly, of course; it would have been
easier for her if they hadn't. King Lear had at least the
satisfaction of knowing that his daughters weren't act-
ing for his own good.
Mrs. Meigs thought gratefully of the way her husband
had left everything to her. At the time she'd been a
little shocked at the idea of not sharing with the chil-
dren. But he'd known what he was about. She felt a
quick gleam of thankfulness that it was she and not he
who was facing this crisis. In such a situation any man
was helpless. He had to live where he was bidden, and let
himself be taken care of.
But this was not the case with Mrs. Meigs, who had
spent long active years taking care of these very people.
She drew herself up, the way girls were taught to stand
when she was young. But she felt a sinking at her heart.
There were so many of them arrayed against her, and
they were such large imposing people, and so young.
There was a moment's pause in the talk as she came
into the living-room. Mrs. Meigs looked from one to an-
other of the faces she had so often washed and kissed,
and sometimes slapped. "Did I hear myself mentioned?"
she asked.
"We were only saying-" began Natalie, in that hesi-
tating manner which disarmed her mother. Poor girl,

a three-room flat, and one of those davenports that you
can turn inside out if you are as strong as a coal heaver.Millicent cut in, "Of course we were speaking about
you, Mother. We have decided that it won't do for you
to go on living alone like this."
"Indeed?" asked Mrs. Meigs with elaborate irony.
"And who is to stop me?"
"We want only what is best for you," Edith hastened

"I manage," retorted Mrs. Meigs.
"I know how you feel, Mother," said Natalie. "You

sacrifice that if you went to live with one of your chil-

you can't have living alone."
Across the living-room Cecile stood watching, her

brought her flowers bought with Roger Kane's money,

"You know we shall be very glad to have you come to

"Thank you, Ralph. I'll take the will for the deed."

"Have you got a lease here?" asked Jack. "If you have,
I can dispose of it for you.""And you needn't be afraid of the moving," added
Millicent. "I will attend to your packing up."

"Now, Mother, there's no use in your taking that

Mrs. Meigs interrupted. "It isn't a question of tone,

"But, Mother," protested Edith, "you talk as if we

"That's how I feel," retorted Mrs. Meigs promptly.
"I've seen the thing happen again and again. So long as

the old nuisance in the spare bedroom. Why, take my
friend Mrs. Bishop, in the apartment just across the

"That may have happened in some cases," said Edith,

"Edith," said Mrs. Meigs, "when you've lived to be
eighty, you'll learn that there aren't any exceptions."
In spite of her lofty tone, she was beginning to realize

Then Cecile spoke up. "You haven't even remembered

Edith turned on Cecile. "What concern have you

"Roger Kane is a catch. How did you ever get him,

"I've always wanted to meet Roger Kane," said Nat-

Just at that moment there were three taps at the bell.III
It was a prearranged signal. Cecile snatched her cloak,

"Yes. I noticed just the other day that yours was

But when other packages were laid with Millicent's,

"Thank you all for remembering me," she said. "I'm
going to save your parcels to open when I'm alone. Now

They acted a little shocked at the suggestion. All of
them played bridge in other places; but they had a feel-
ing that it was scarcely proper to enjoy one's self at a
family dinner.
Though they didn't settle to bridge, their battle front
was broken. Millicent related how a new chairmanship
had been thrust upon her, "because they all said that I

the most up-to-date in town, but simply never got any-

that small house. I'm thinking of putting in oil."
Mrs. Meigs wished Tip O'Neil had been there to hear
them. Here as well as back in Iowa where Tip came from,
a family party was a family party.
At eleven o'clock Natalie's husband said they must
be going. "We'll give you a lift," said Edith sweetly.
Edith always made such a point of doing things for
Natalie.
Millicent rose with alacrity. She had got her second
wind. "There's no hurry about your moving, Mother,"

"I might not," said Mrs. Meigs. "There's plenty of

"It's a very out-of-date flat," said Edith. "And such
a neighborhood!"
"It's a very friendly neighborhood," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I know everybody within three blocks, at least to

"While we're on the subject," said Jack, "you'd better

"In the bank," said Mrs. Meigs coolly.
"Why, Mother, I saw it in your desk last winter when

"If you keep going through people's desks, some day

"Compromising letters, maybe."
"Why, Mother!" said Millicent in a shocked tone.
"What a way to talk, at your age! You sent me to the

I receive," retorted Mrs. Meigs. But she had to forestall
rummagers by opening the tall old "secretary" herself.
Jack glanced over the lease. "This is fine. It permits you
to sublet. How would it be if I sent a real estate man here

"He'd be a very surprised real estate man," said Mrs.
Meigs, "when I shut the door so fast his nose catches in it."
"None of your violence, Mom!" said Jack, "or I'll be

Her only son, Jack, and her youngest; and he had just
called her "Mom." She glanced around to be sure that

a hotel. It's my opinion she'd like a home of her own, for

"Has she said so?" asked Jack sharply.
"Well, no. But she came here this morning, and of-
fered to help me"
"Oh, Estelle likes you! People don't live the way they
used to when you were young, though; and it's high time

We can manage the whole business without bothering

You must be dead tired."
She started to say, "Jack Meigs, you give me that
lease!" But his having the lease didn't amount to any-
thing; she mustn't be superstitious about a paper in a
desk. Anyhow the words didn't come; she had had
enough of argument for one evening.
Her eyes went past him to Edith, who had invited her
to Sunday supper and a game of bridge, and had turnedit into an ambush. The very idea of trying to drag Dr.
Stuart into their family dispute! She'd see Dr. Stuart
herself, if need be. She'd see Edith later, and Jack. One
by one she could deal with her children; but to-night
they were massed against her. She'd have it out with them
some other time.
There were the usual dutiful kisses, the usual polite
speeches. At last the front door banged on them. Next
year she'd set up the card-tables before they came. Noth-
ing like a little bridge, to keep people from talking too
much.
Anna had gone to bed in her room off the kitchen.
With an almost guilty sense of delight at being alone,
Mrs. Meigs washed and wiped the dinner dishes, and,
carefully mounting a chair, stowed them away in the
china cupboard.
She'd been on her feet an awful lot to-day, and the
back of her neck was beginning to ache with weariness.
She was hungry, too; she had eaten the slightest of din-
ners. She went to the ice-box for turkey meat, and made
herself a wonderful sandwich, with lettuce sticking out
around the edges and twice as much mayonnaise as a
sandwich needed.
But it didn't taste half so good as she had expected.
After three bites she put it down. She wandered back to
the living-room. In the warm air Roger's chrysanthe-
mums already smelled like weeds.
The sight of them reminded Mrs. Meigs of a duty
neglected. She selected the three smallest, which were a
lovely golden color, and arranged them carefully in a
little vase that her father had given her when she was a
girl. Then she carried them over and set them on the table
with her husband's picture.She leaned over and studied the photograph. It was
an excellent likeness, taken when he was about sixty.
He'd always said the children got their good looks from
their mother. They got their height from him; he was a
ruddy man, big and broad-shouldered, with an expres-
sion both shrewd and good-humored. He'd grown better-
looking as he grew older. The photographer had caught
the very twinkle in his eyes. In the picture he looked as he
used to when he caught her straightening things up, and
asked, "Well, Carrie, going to have company?"
Carrie was an odious name. Maybe that was why when
her first daughter was born, Mrs. Meigs had named her
Millicent. The romantic name was a bad fit for the
woman who bore it. Still, with a last name like Meigs,
you had to take the curse off somehow.
"Carrie" wasn't as bad, though, as his calling her
"Mamma." She'd always fought against that. It sounded
as if she and he were related only through their children.
He'd liked it though. If you came right down to it, it was
the fathers and not the children who sent flowers on
Mother's Day. How he'd laughed the time an embarrassed
stranger thought she really was his mother! He'd eased
off a little after that, though.
Eleven years since he died! At first it had seemed to
her that she couldn't go on alone. She had consoled her-
self by thinking that she wouldn't have much longer to
