go on. But later she had realized that if you've ever been
really married, you can't lose your husband. He was
with her now, the same as he always had been.
Only sometimes, she was sick for the sight and sound
of him, for the funny little scalp-lock that his hair-
brush never seemed to catch, for the way he scuffed one
foot when he was tired, for his everlasting twinkle whenhe had a joke on her. How he'd have loved to see the
grandchildren growing up! He had been fairly strict with
his own children, but Richard and Cecile never thought
of minding him. And somehow that struck him as the
best of jokes.
You had to go on living. When you reached the eighties,
the best thing to do was to treat your ears like a red flag,
and wave them in people's faces. But as she straightened
the three chrysanthemums she had brought him, the
photograph grew misty before Mrs. Meigs's eyes. Eleven
years is a long time. You keep so busy that for days on
end you half forget. But sometimes you remember.IX
WHEN she woke the next morning it was raining:
not a sharp determined rain, which Mrs. Meigs liked,
but a dreary drizzle. Though she could hear Anna stir-
ring about in the kitchen, and knew it must be nearly
eight, it was still so dark she had to turn on a light to see
the clock. The brick wall visible from her bedroom win-
dow was depressing on such a morning, and she was
tired. She had had trees to look at from her bed in the
old house. Mrs. Meigs had a good mind not to get up at
all this morning.
She had to leave her bed, however, to talk to Millicent
on the telephone. Millicent had missed a paper, a most
important list of committees, and thought she must have
left it somewhere last night. Millicent was always mis-
laying her documents, and they were always frightfully
important.
Search revealed it on the hall table. "Have Ralph stop
for it on his way down to the office," suggested Mrs.
Meigs.
"I'm afraid I'll have to get it myself, later in the day,
though goodness knows I have enough to see to to-day,"
said Millicent. "Ralph is always so hurried in the
morning."
But half an hour later Ralph appeared. He didn't
seem in any hurry; he accepted a cup of coffee from her
breakfast pot. Mrs. Meigs eyed, but did not connect, the
electric toaster that had come from Ralph's stock. Itmight embarrass him if she burned up half a loaf of bread
on his gift.
Ralph stirred and tasted, and then he sipped luxuri-
ously. "I hope you are coming to live with us, Mrs.
Meigs," he said. "Maybe you can teach our cook to make
coffee like this."
"Don't hold your breath till I move in," said Mrs.
Meigs. "Ralph, what do you think of this new beau of
Cecile's this Kane person?"
"Cecile is too young to think seriously about men,"
said Ralph. "Of course, I'd rather she played around with
boys of her own age."
"She half plans to go to college," said Mrs. Meigs.
"More than half, I hope. Millicent wanted her to make
her début before she finished her education. But I expect
her to go ahead and finish."
"Do you think college is doing Dick any good?" asked
Mrs. Meigs.
"He isn't learning much of anything, if that's what
you mean. But college is the minimum social require-
ment for a young man nowadays. The intellectual end
doesn't count." He grinned faintly. "I could teach Dick
more if I had him in the business with me. Goodness
knows too our business could stand some young blood."
Mrs. Meigs refilled his cup. "Business isn't any too good,
from what Cecile tells me."
"It isn't. And I'm not sure it's going to get any bet-
ter. You see we're jobbers, and the tendency is more
and more for the retailer to go direct to the manufac-
turer. I've thought of going into the retail end myself,
keeping the jobbing too, of course. But that would mean
a lot of work and uncertainty. So it's easier to worry
along as we are.""If a couple of thousand dollars would help you out,
I could loan you that much," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I wouldn't dream of taking your money, Mrs. Meigs,"
said Ralph. "Though it's mighty good of you to offer."
"Millicent has sometimes hinted that she should have
had a share of what her father left."
"Millicent has a husband to take care of her," said
Ralph grimly.
His face was brighter when he left. Such a quiet, wor-
ried man! Everybody took Ralph as a matter of course.
Even Cecile, though she sympathized in passing, took
for granted that her father's part in life was to work for
the rest of them.
The few minutes' peace had done him good that and
the coffee. He'd drunk all that was in her breakfast pot;
while she was waiting for more, Mrs. Meigs called Mrs.
Bishop, and invited her to lunch.
Her spirits rose further during the morning, when a
real estate agent showed up and announced that he'd
been asked to undertake the renting of the flat, and
thought he'd better look it over the first thing. Mrs.
Meigs didn't let him get beyond the front door. She
hadn't supposed Jack would really go as far as that-
certainly not in such a hurry. She'd settle with Mr. Jack
the next time she saw him. If he didn't understand Eng-
lish, she'd have to learn Spanish, and give him a piece
of her mind either way.
She and Mrs. Bishop were just sitting down to lunch
when Carter Brooks telephoned. "Is Natalie there?" he
asked.
All her life Mrs. Meigs had made a habit of telling the
truth. It was one of her principles, too, not to interfere
in the married lives of her children. But an impulse shecould not account for made her say, "Yes, she's here.
I asked her last night to come and eat cold turkey with
me. Do you want to speak to her?"
"Oh, no!" said Carter hastily. "I just wanted to be
sure she had money for a taxi. It's such a nasty day, and
she isn't always careful about not getting her feet wet."
"I'll tell her. Thanks for calling," said Mrs. Meigs.
He must think she was in her second childhood, to
believe that he would telephone like an ardent bride-
groom, for Natalie to be sure and take a taxi. All his
telephoning must indicate that he was getting worried:
there might be something beside gossip in those stories
about Natalie's running around with a younger man.
Natalie ought to think of a better excuse, though, than
always to tell him she was at her mother's. A nice pickle
Mrs. Meigs would have been in if Carter had taken her
at her word, and insisted on speaking to Natalie.
The cold turkey and salad were accompanied by coffee,
and thick slices of birthday cake by more coffee. "I'm full
up to here," said Mrs. Bishop, raising her hand to her
wrinkled throat. "Nowadays I never get a chance at
home to eat until I want to quit. Of course, the doctor
backs up Grace when she tells me not to overeat. But
once in a while-"
"Once in a while you like to eat until you're good
and full. So do I," said Mrs. Meigs.
Anna came in with the big silver coffee-pot. "Will I
put this in the living-room for you, Mis' Meigs? I thought
maybe you might like another cup while you're sitting
talking."
"That's thoughtful of you, Anna. Oh, just a minute!"
Mrs. Meigs turned away from the table, and felt for
the lump in her stocking. From it she produced a rollof bills, and peeled off a five. "You had extra work yes-
terday, and I want to give you a little something."
Anna took the money with a grin. "This'll buy me
a new hat. Or maybe I'd better put it in the savings-
bank."
"Better spend the money," said Mrs. Meigs. "Then if
the bank breaks, you'll still have the hat."
"Aren't you afraid you'll lose your money, carrying
it that way?" asked Mrs. Bishop.
"I've been carrying it this way for sixty years, and I
haven't lost it yet," said Mrs. Meigs cheerfully.
Anna set the coffee-pot on the living-room table. To
make room for it she had to move a heap of parcels, all
wrapped in white tissue paper. "Forget to open your
birthday presents, Mis' Meigs?" she hinted.
Mrs. Meigs had forgotten, what with Ralph and the
real estate agent, and the mysterious telephone message
from Natalie's husband. But what is simple forgetful-
ness when you're young is interpreted as "breaking up"
when you're old.
"I thought Mrs. Bishop would enjoy seeing me open
the packages," said Mrs. Meigs.
Mrs. Bishop did. She was beaming with expectation as
Mrs. Meigs fumbled the parcels over, looking for one
that resembled a hot-water bag. Better open that first,
and get it over with.
When it was over with, she handed the rest of the
packages to Mrs. Bishop. "You open them, and hand the
cards to me," she directed.
The first present was from Jack, a new pair of gloves.
"Oh, aren't they beautiful!" said Mrs. Bishop. "Such
nice soft kid! They have a fleece lining, too."
Mrs. Meigs laid a hand on one of the new gloves. Theglove fingers stuck out nearly an inch beyond her own.
"I suppose,” faltered Mrs. Bishop, "your son thought
the gloves would be more comfortable in cold weather
if they were plenty big."
"Five and three-quarters is my size, summer and win-
ter," said Mrs. Meigs.
The next present was a gray knitted shoulder wrap
from Estelle. "So nice and warm when you have your
breakfast in bed," cooed Mrs. Bishop.
"I haven't had breakfast in bed since Jack was born,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "It will come in handy, though, in case
I ever have another baby."
Mrs. Bishop stared. Then the color mounted to her
withered cheeks, and she tried to laugh. "Oh, it's a joke!"
دو
"I meant it for one," said Mrs. Meigs. "But Millicent
would have said, 'Mother, that's no way to talk at your
age!' " Secretly though she contrasted the soft tone with
the hideous purple of the shoulder wrap Grace had made
for Mrs. Bishop. Her family might be tactless, but at
any rate they weren't color blind.
The next present was from Natalie, somebody's trav-
els somewhere, with a lot of pictures of heathen temples,
and natives picking crops off bushes. "It has lovely large
print," said Mrs. Bishop.
"Yes, and I'm far-sighted. I'd have to prop it open
across the room if I wanted to read it. It's a nice present,
though," said Mrs. Meigs. "The man in the book-store
knows me. He'll give me three good love stories for it."
Edith's present turned out to be three pairs of very
thin black silk stockings. Mrs. Meigs held up their shim-
mering length, and moved them to watch the play of
light. "Now that's my idea of a real present," she saidcontentedly. "I can remember when only the rich wore
silk stockings, or people of bad character. But I love 'em."
All the children were accounted for. There was still
another package. "I don't know who that can be from,"
said Mrs. Meigs.
"Perhaps from Cecile?" suggested Mrs. Bishop.
"She brought me all these flowers."
"Yes, I noticed them. They're beautiful."
Mightn't it be Richard? Mrs. Meigs thrust that idea
aside without mentioning it. He hadn't appeared last
night, and there'd been no letter from him in the mail
to-day. Still she prolonged the suspense; and Mrs. Bishop,
as eager as if all the presents had been for her, guessed
wildly Cal Fletcher, and Mrs. Cronkhite, and Ralph, and
Carter Brooks.
It was from Richard, after all. It was a woolly jumping-
jack, accompanied by a scrawled note, "Dear Gram, can't
be with you on the joyous occasion, so I'm sending a sub-
stitute."
Mrs. Meigs grinned. Mrs. Bishop quavered, "Oh, isn't it
cute!" But she was plainly shocked at such a present on a
grandmother's birthday.
The jumping-jack had been left with Anna, it turned
out, when Richard came here to change his clothes on the
day of the card party. He'd planned the joke days ahead.
And here she thought he'd forgotten! "I tell you," said
Mrs. Meigs, "grandchildren make up for an awful lot."
It was a perfect afternoon to sit and rock and drink
coffee. Mrs. Meigs kept the jumping-jack on her lap as
she rocked. The quiet was grateful after yesterday's ex-
citement. She was still tired.
She'd have Natalie over for lunch some day soon. Notto-morrow, because Cal was coming to dinner to-
morrow, and company once a day was enough, even for
Mrs. Meigs. But if Natalie was doing what she appeared
to be doing, Mrs. Meigs wanted to be in on the plot. She
wouldn't take another chance of being caught in a fib.
The lamps were lighted before Mrs. Bishop rose to leave.
"I'm sure this has been a second birthday party," she
said politely, "what with cold turkey, and presents and
all."
"Don't you want to take home a few flowers?" asked
Mrs. Meigs. "And if you like those fleece-lined gloves,
you're welcome to them, I'm sure. They're a little nearer
your size than mine."
"Oh, they're just beautiful!" said Mrs. Bishop. "But
you couldn't give away a present."
"Couldn't I? I usually change 'em, and that's worse."
Mrs. Meigs picked up the gloves. Then she hesitated, gave
a half-ashamed laugh, and laid them back on the pile.
"I'll give them to you to-morrow or next day," she said.
"I always keep my birthday presents together for a few
days. Christmas presents, too. It's silly of me, I dare say.
But I do like to sort of gloat on the size of the heap."
She made up for the postponement by loading Mrs.
Bishop with maroon and white chrysanthemums. They
were Cecile's present; but after all Roger Kane had paid
for them.
After Mrs. Bishop had gone, Mrs. Meigs found "Bad
Women" on the couch in the living-room. Mrs. Bishop
had returned it without a word. "I guess she didn't like
it," chuckled Mrs. Meigs. She was glad to get it back, even
condemned by silence. She herself hadn't finished it, and
this would be a perfect evening to read.
She had lunched so heartily that a light supper sufficedher. "D'you mind if I take a girl friend to the movies
to-night?" asked Anna. "That five dollars you gave me
is burning a hole in my pocket."
"You go right along,” said Mrs. Meigs. "I think I'll go
to bed and read. If the door-bell rings, I'll just pretend
I'm not here.”
She established herself with Richard's jumping-jack
on the spread, where she could see it when she looked up
from her book. She had four pillows behind her, and a
powerful bulb in her bedside lamp. Her old clock ticked
away cheerfully on top of the wardrobe. What a luxury
it was to be alone sometimes, and accountable to no one!
For a day when she had felt like not getting up at all, it
was ending very well indeed.
Anna returned from her show, and stuck her head in
at the door to say, "Good night, Mis' Meigs."
"Good night, Anna," returned Mrs. Meigs without
looking up from her book.
It was growing late, and for all she cared it might grow
later. What fun it was to read in bed, with the house
quiet, and the knowledge that if you chose to read right
on till morning, that was your own lookout! In a maga-
zine article which had come under her notice a week ago,
a learned doctor had pronounced solemnly, "Reading in
bed is a most pernicious habit." Mrs. Meigs had read the
article in bed, so the joke was on the learned doctor. She
thought scornfully that he was probably one of those
people who turned out the light and crawled in, like a
dog.
It was half past two when the heroine reached the
marriage altar. Mrs. Meigs had been worried once or twice
for fear she wasn't going to make it. That showed the
author knew his business.She laid down the book, and paid a visit to the cold
turkey in the ice-box. Just as she bit into her sandwich,
the door-bell rang.
At this hour, that could be only one person. Mrs. Meigs
padded in her slippers to the front window, and opened
it a crack. Sure enough, she heard a familiar hail from a
car parked directly in front of the building. "Oh, Caro-
line! Unlock the door for me, will you?"
Cecile often came to spend the night when she knew
she'd be out late. It saved questions at home. But usually
Mrs. Meigs had some advance warning. "Did you tell
your mother you were coming here?" she asked when
Cecile entered.
"Telephone her if you like," said Cecile. She was wear-
ing her evening cloak, and long earrings that made her
look older. There were dark circles under her eyes, and
the eyes themselves were feverishly bright. Mrs. Meigs
had never seen her look like this.
Cecile sank into a chair, and kicked off her silver slip-
pers. Mrs. Meigs got a sleepy Ralph on the telephone, and
informed him of Cecile's whereabouts. She hung up be-
fore he had a chance to comment on the hour. "Want
some cold turkey?" she demanded.
Cecile shook her head. "I'm not hungry."
"Well," said Mrs. Meigs, "I am." She got her sandwich,
and sat down opposite Cecile to eat it.
Cecile met her eye defiantly. "Well, Caroline, you're
the first to hear the news. Roger Kane has just asked me
to marry him.”
"He might have asked you earlier in the evening," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, we danced, and had supper first!""When I was young," said Mrs. Meigs, "my idea of just
no fun at all was dancing with a fat man."
"Roger isn't fat-exactly," said Cecile.
"He isn't thin," said Mrs. Meigs. "Do you know, I
think I like cold turkey even better than I do hot? I'm
always sorry to see the end of it."
"You're a provoking woman, Caroline," said Cecile
with a reluctant smile. "Aren't you even going to ask.
what my answer was to Roger's proposal?"
"Why should I ask, and hurry things? When a girl
comes to spend the night, and you get really confidential,
there's nothing that's more fun than talking about the
men you've refused."
"But I didn't refuse him," said Cecile.
There was dead silence for a moment. Then Mrs. Meigs
said coldly, "Your mother will be pleased."
"Too well pleased. That's why I didn't go home to-
night. I couldn't face Mother's pleasure." Cecile rose, and
looked her grandmother straight in the eye. "Aren't you
going to congratulate me, Caroline? Technically I'm a
successful woman. I've managed to catch me a husband."
"As a catch, he's all right," said Mrs. Meigs defiantly.
"As a husband, I don't suppose he'd ever be unkind,
exactly. He's too well pleased with himself for that."
Cecile rummaged her pajamas out of the spare-room
closet. Ten minutes later she appeared on the threshold of
Mrs. Meigs's bedroom. "It's lonesome in the spare room,
Caroline," she said. "Can't I crawl in with you?"
Mrs. Meigs let her crawl past and lie down near the
wall. "Richard sent me a jumping-jack for my birthday,"
said Mrs. Meigs.
"He shows rare taste in presents," said Cecile. "Whenwe were kids, he used to give me marbles, and a baseball
glove, and roller-skates in his size. Not that he wasn't
a good enough brother, as they go. I'm selfish myself,
I guess. It runs in our branch of the family."
For a few minutes she turned and twisted. Then she
fell heavily asleep. She did not stir when Mrs. Meigs
turned on a light and looked at her. One bare arm was
curved over her head, and the shadow of her long lashes
made her closed eyes tragic. She looked very young in
her sleep, and very defenseless.
It's an awful business, when a new generation gets to
the marrying age. There wasn't a man on earth whom
Mrs. Meigs would consider good enough for Cecile. But
Cecile might have chosen one who was somewhere near
good enough. You don't expect mature wisdom at nine-
teen. The attention of an older man sometimes turns a
girl's head. And at home Cecile had always heard too
much about money.
It would have been different, though, if Cecile hadn't
known what she was turning her back on. At nineteen
Mrs. Meigs herself had said airily, "I think love is just
a thing they talk about in books. I don't expect it ever
to happen to me." But when it had happened, she'd
recognized it fast enough. And on her love she had built
her life.
She was satisfied with her life. But now that it was
almost done, couldn't she pass on the lesson of its full-
ness? What's worth having is worth paying for. The other
things you have to pay for anyhow, generally far more
than they're worth. So you might as well make your own
bargain.
Only the other day, Tip and Cecile had come scuffing
the fallen leaves like two children. Only yesterday hehad kissed Cecile in this very flat, and Cecile had for-
gotten everything but his kisses, and had walked on the
clouds, and felt the stars crowning her head.
Not that Tip would be an easy man to get along with.
And Mrs. Meigs would have preferred Cecile to wait at
least five years before she married anybody. But that was
more or less like saying you'd rather wait until you were
twenty-four before you were struck by lightning.
Presently the light disturbed Cecile; or perhaps a lin-
gering uneasiness waked her. She turned her face away,
and pretended she was still asleep. But Mrs. Meigs had
caught the gleam between her lashes.
"Cecile," she said sternly, "you're doing an awful
thing. You've promised to marry one man when you
love another."
"Love!" muttered Cecile defiantly. Then her voice
broke. "Oh, well! Love isn't everything."
"It may not be everything," said Mrs. Meigs. "But it's
enough."
"It doesn't last," said Cecile. "I don't last. Nothing
lasts."
When the light was out, she groped for her grand-
mother's hand. Still holding it, she cried herself to sleep.X
SHE had made it a practice never to interfere in her
children's love-affairs. But the grandchildren were dif-
ferent. Anyhow you couldn't call it interference if you
threw a rope to a drowning person. Mrs. Meigs went to
sleep feeling like a useless old woman. But after an hour
or two of light uneasy slumber, she was wide awake,
waiting for the day to begin.
When the clock showed half past seven she rose quietly,
taking care not to disturb Cecile. She hurried through
her bath, and drank her coffee standing. Then she put
on her best bonnet with the white lawn ties, and sallied
forth.
It was the first time in her eighty years that she had
ever visited a man in his flat. Moreover she interrupted
his shaving.
He came to the door in an old bath-robe, with one side
of his face all lather, and the razor in his hand. There
were dark circles under his eyes, and the hand that held
his razor was not entirely steady. But he wrinkled when
he saw her; and that wrinkling smile of Tip's always
swung the balance in his favor.
"Come in, Mrs. Meigs," he said cordially. "Sorry the
place doesn't look better. I was out late last night."
Mrs. Meigs took the easy-chair, and looked about the
apartment. It was a large, light room, smelling faintly of
tobacco smoke and fresh air. There were a lot of books
around, and somebody had started to repair the radio,and quit when the pieces were all out. This would do at
a pinch to start housekeeping in. Nowadays young peo-
ple didn't begin with a ten-room house.
Tip sat on the edge of the unmade bed, and grinned
at her. The girl who married him would have an unquiet
future. But security isn't everything. Mrs. Meigs looked
Tip in the eye, and asked, "Are you and Cecile on speak-
ing terms?"
"Cecile and I are never just on speaking terms," an-
swered Tip promptly. "We kiss or we quarrel. Generally
we do both."
"Cecile isn't a quarrelsome person," said Mrs. Meigs.
Tip grinned broadly. "If you think that, you ought to
hear her when she gets started preaching common sense
and the conduct of life."
"I've no doubt she has plenty of provocation," said
Mrs. Meigs. "But the question is, does her advice do you
any good?"
"That, or something, does. I've got a new job. Had it
since yesterday."
"That's good as far as it goes," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I'm going to sell life insurance. Funny," said Tip,
"but that's a thing I've never tried before."
"How hard are you going to try it now?"
"Well, it isn't much after eight o'clock, and you see
I'm already out of bed." Tip suddenly became earnest.
"There's another thing. You know that Western land
of mine I was telling you about the other day? I'm going
to sell part of it. Most of it I shall hold, of course. But I
want a couple of thousand dollars cash right now."
"It sounds to me as if you had turned over a new
leaf, Tip."
Tip was never serious for more than a minute at atime. "It was turned for me," he said with a grin. "I'm
just sticking it down. Me in the rôle of the go-getter
is funny. But it ought to be interesting for a while. It'll
be such a change."
"Are you as fickle in love as you are about work?"
asked Mrs. Meigs dryly.
"You don't see many girls' pictures around on the
walls, do you?"
She glanced around. "I don't see any."
"I took 'em all down," said Tip grandly, "the day I
met Cecile."
"Very flattering for Cecile, I'm sure," said Mrs. Meigs.
"But while you've been acting the play-boy, she's been
going places with Roger Kane."
"Maybe he's the kind of man she likes. Very well. If
she likes him, let her go with him. I won't stop her."
"You haven't stopped her," said Mrs. Meigs. "She
came in last night to tell me she's engaged to be mar-
ried to him."
Tip stared blankly. "Say that again. No, don't say it.
Of course her head has been turned by his money."
"Give the devil his due,” said Mrs. Meigs sharply. "He
made his money by going after it, I suppose. He's getting
Cecile the same way. I don't like Roger Kane. But I must
say he hasn't let any grass grow under his feet."
Tip struck his razor against the palm of his left hand,
cut himself, and looked down to see what had hurt. He
laid the razor on the bed, got up and walked to the
window. Then he began to pace up and down.
"It's kind of a shock," he muttered. "I thought she
was running around with him to tease me. But if she
thinks she wants to marry him-"
"She's promised him she will. And if I'm any judgeof character," said Mrs. Meigs, "he's a good promise
collector. But surely you're not going to sit down and
let him walk off with her right under your nose?"
"I could stop her all right," said Tip. "But I'm not
sure I ought to. Look here, Mrs. Meigs, you're devoted
to Cecile, aren't you?"
"She's the dearest thing in life to me," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Then I can talk to you," said Tip. The blood came
up under his thin skin. Through his teeth he said, "I can
make Cecile happier than any one else on earth. I've
done it time and again. But there are times when I make
her terribly miserable."
Mrs. Meigs drew a long breath. "My dear boy, that
doesn't matter, so long as you don't make her com-
fortable."
Tip looked at her wonderingly. "That's not the sort
of advice a fellow expects an old lady to give. All the
better of course if it isn't. Shows it must be true."
"If it's true, why don't you act on it?"
"Do you realize what you're saying? If Cecile were
married to me, she'd lead the life of a yellow dog."
"It's better to be a yellow dog," said Mrs. Meigs sen-
tentiously, "and know you're alive, than to be a pet
kitten and doze your life away."
"Oh, you gorgeous old darling!" cried Tip. He snatched
her out of her chair, and planted a hearty kiss on her
cheek.
"Why, Tip O'Neil!" protested Mrs. Meigs, as she
straightened her bonnet.
"You brought it on yourself," said Tip. "I'm only
marrying Cecile because she's your granddaughter, and
for the next sixty years I can look forward to her turn-
ing into an old lady just like you.""Get along with your blarney," said Mrs. Meigs. But
she liked it. She liked it very much.
Tip fell once more to pacing the room. "Sit down
again, and let me think."
"You've already done that once this morning. Be
careful, or you'll get the habit," warned Mrs. Meigs.
"You think too. We're fellow-conspirators. We've got
to figure out the best way to go at Cecile." Tip's eyes
twinkled. "I suppose she's still asleep at this ungodly
hour?"
"If you're thinking of sweeping her off her feet," said
Mrs. Meigs, "nine o'clock in the morning isn't any hour
for such an undertaking."
"Night would be better. Night outdoors, and moon-
light. Will there be a moon to-night, I wonder?"
"It's stopped raining," said Mrs. Meigs. "But it looks
every minute as if it would begin again."
"All the better it it does," said Tip. "Two people
in a car, on a wet night, with the rain sloshing on the
roof, and the lights reflected from the shiny pavements-
Why, that's perfect!"
"You won't lose any time, then? You'll try to see
her this evening?" urged Mrs. Meigs.
"Of course I'll see her this evening," said Tip eagerly.
"I'll have worked a whole day by that time, too. Wouldn't
it be grand if I could sell a policy right off, and lay that
before her as evidence I'm a changed character?"
"There you go!" said Mrs. Meigs. "The minute you
think how grand it would be to sell a policy, in your
eyes the policy is as good as sold."
"You have no confidence in me. You like me, but
you have no confidence. Very well!" said Tip, stickingout his chin. "I'll have to show you too what a swell head
of a family I'll make."
"I'm waiting to be shown," said Mrs. Meigs.
"You might pave the way for me a little," wheedled
Tip.
"I'd better not admit I've even seen you. And I'd bet-
ter get home," said Mrs. Meigs, "before I'm missed and
questions asked."
"Wait five minutes, and I'll take you," said Tip. "It
doesn't take me long to shave and bathe. I was shaving
when you came, wasn't I? What'd I do with my razor?"
"You left it on the bed," said Mrs. Meigs. She made the
bed while she was waiting for him.
It saved time, having him take her in his car. But
she got out at the corner for fear of being seen with him.
As Tip drove away he turned around from the wheel
and took both hands off to wave to her. Mrs. Meigs
stopped to straighten both her bonnet and her face.
Cecile, in her grandmother's dressing-gown, was drink-
ing her coffee and reading a magazine. "You're out early
to-day, Caroline," she said, without looking up.
"I went for a walk, before it started to rain again,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "By the way, I've a piece of news for
you. Tip O'Neil has a new job."
"It would be news if he had an old one." Cecile turned
a page with elaborate indifference. Suddenly she looked
up. "Are you such a friend of Tip's that he telephones
you all his news?"
"He thought I'd be interested," said Mrs. Meigs. "I
was. Very much interested."
Cecile shoved away her magazine, and rose reluctantly.
"I suppose I've got to go home and face the music.Mother's joy will be a little hard to bear, though. She's
firmly convinced that she invented Roger."
"That," said Mrs. Meigs, "is an awful slam on both of
them."
Cecile hunted out an old suit that she kept in the spare-
room closet, and borrowed a suitcase to pack her evening
clothes in. She moved draggingly, as if she were dead
tired.
Mrs. Meigs's exultation wilted. If it were only a clean-
cut choice between love and money! But Roger had all
the solid arguments on his side. And love as it had been
in Mrs. Meigs's day seemed to have gone as completely
out of fashion as the good substantial broadcloth and
double-faced satin that you wore one year for best and
another for second-best, and then it would pay for turn-
ing and remaking.
When Cecile was ready to leave, she stopped for a
cigarette. "Caroline," she said, "Roger's so much older
than I am, that I have hopes of being a widow some day.
When I am, you and I'll live together and have fun just
the way we used to when I was a kid and went to your
house to spend a week with you."
"You used to like to play changing places with me.
Remember? You'd say, 'Now you be the little girl,
Caroline, and I'll be the grandmother.' But if you're
marrying just to be a widow, it seems simpler not to
marry at all," said Mrs. Meigs.
Cecile's eyes followed her ascending smoke, and she
frowned a little. "I hope you won't ever let them argue
you into giving up your independence, Caroline."
The seriousness of her tone made Mrs. Meigs stare.
"You mean all that talk the other night? Bless your heart,
child, I've heard the wind blow before.""It blew up a bigger storm this time than usual," said
Cecile. "And yesterday Mother had a plumber at the
house, to see how much it would cost to put a new bath-
room in the third story."
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "Jack had a real estate man here
too. But I soon sent him about his business."
"Then you aren't actually considering giving up your
own home?"
"Indeed I'm not. It isn't only that I prefer to do as
I like, instead of swallowing what my children think is
good for me, but I want to keep on doing what I can
for other people, too. Of course the modern way to do
for other people is the way your mother does."
"Get up committees, and attend meetings, and peti-
tion the Legislature," agreed Cecile.
"I couldn't do that even if I was younger. I wouldn't
want to," said Mrs. Meigs. "What I like is to run my
home so that other people will find it a pleasant place to
drop in at. Dick, and your father, and Estelle, and Mrs.
Bishop have all been here in the last few days, just be-
cause they wanted to come."
"How about Cal Fletcher?" suggested Cecile with a
smile.
"Cal's coming for dinner to-night," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Do you think I'd better telephone and remind him?
Last time he forgot, and ate supper at his boarding-
house before he came. Not that that made much differ-
ence. He tucked away a pretty good meal when he got
here."
"You'd better call up," said Cecile, crushing out the
light of her cigarette. "What sort of job has Tip O'Neil
this time? Putting salt on the tails of birds in public
parks?""I'd better not spoil his thunder. He'll want to tell you
himself."
Cecile's face grew bleak. "I suppose I will see him
again-once or twice." She put her arms around Mrs.
Meigs, to kiss her good-by. But instead of lifting her
grandmother off her feet in a bear hug, she laid her head
for a moment on the old woman's shoulder.
Five minutes after Cecile had left, Anna called from
the living-room, "Oh, Mis' Meigs! You're wanted." Mrs.
Meigs, in her bedroom, decided that she really must
speak to Anna about such a way of announcing a guest.
But as she came out, her eye was caught by a suitcase.
That explained Anna's informality: Cecile must have
forgotten something and returned for it. There she stood
with her back to the living-room windows, fumbling
with her hand-bag, and tapping one foot nervously.
It wasn't until she spoke that Mrs. Meigs discovered
her mistake. This wasn't Cecile at all. It was Natalie.
"That's a joke on me," said Mrs. Meigs. "For a moment
I took you for your own niece. I've noticed, though, how
young you've been looking lately."
"Not that young," said Natalie ruefully. "If I really
were 'Cile's age, and just beginning everything-" Her
voice trailed off. She looked beyond her mother, not see-
ing what she looked at.
"Yes. What would you do if you were Cecile's age?"
prompted Mrs. Meigs.
"I- Oh!" Natalie blinked in bewilderment. She had
forgotten what she wanted to say. "Mother, would it
put you out if I came here to stay for a few days?"
"I'd love it, and you know I would," answered Mrs.
Meigs. "Is Carter out of town?"
"No," said Natalie. "I mean yes. Yes, of course he's out
८of town. He's gone on a business trip. He may be gone
several days. I don't know just how long. I got lone-
some, in the apartment all by myself. So I packed my suit-
case and came over, without even stopping to telephone."
She turned so that her face caught the light. Her eyes
were swollen with crying, and her lower lip trembled.
"Natalie, is anything the matter?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"No. No, of course not. Carter and I-that is, Carter
-I mean, we had a little quarrel before he went away.
That's all."
"Oh, that's nothing!" said Mrs. Meigs. "Or at least
it will be nothing, when he comes back and you can
fight it out with him. But you did quite right, not to
mope alone in your apartment and think how abused you
were."
She busied herself settling Natalie in the spare room.
"Cecile slept in this bed about ten minutes last night.
I don't think I'll change the sheets just for that. Wouldn't
you like a hot bath before lunch, and maybe a little nap?
You look worn out."
"My head aches," Natalie acknowledged. "Carter does
go on so, when he has an issue."
"He always did," said Mrs. Meigs. "The evening he
came to ask your parents' consent to marry you, he sat
talking about nothing until one o'clock in the morning,
before he got around to what he came to talk about.
Your father had given up hours before, and gone to bed.
You sat upstairs alone, wondering what on earth was
going on, I suppose."
"It seems a long time ago," murmured Natalie. That
in itself told a lot. Mrs. Meigs's own engagement was as
fresh in her mind as if it were yesterday. Just because
she liked to think about it, it had remained very vivid.And there'd been no years of quarreling and misunder-
standing to blur it.
When Natalie came from the hot bath, with lavender
salts, she allowed herself to be tucked in for a nap. Drow-
sily she reached for her mother's hand. "Sometimes I wish
I hadn't ever married. Just think what a nice old maid
I'd be by this time, if I'd gone on living with you. We'd
go to all the matinées together, and you could teach me
to knit, and in the evenings we'd play cribbage"
"And you'd tell everybody what a care Mother was
getting to be. No, thanks," Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I've
seen these old maid daughters who devoted their lives
to their mothers. I've seen the mothers, too. What's the
name of the insect that eats its offspring? Whatever
it is, I don't want to be it."
She enjoyed unpacking Natalie's clothes, and consult-
ing Anna about extending lunch for another person.
"It's nice having Mis' Brooks here," said Anna. "She al-
ways stays in the front part of the house, the way a vis-
itor should."
At the lunch-table Natalie looked a little better. She
chatted gaily about a luncheon party Edith was giving,
and how Millicent had got into the papers lately with
some new committee. "You'd think Edith would get
tired of the society stunt, and Mill of reforming the
world," she said. "But it seems they don't."
Beneath her gaiety she seemed nervous, and almost
frightened. Once or twice she forgot what she had started
to say; but when Mrs. Meigs began to talk, she inter-
rupted. That wasn't like Natalie.
"Did you let Carter know you were coming here?"
asked Mrs. Meigs suddenly.Natalie jumped. "No. No, I didn't let him know."
"Well, don't you think you'd better? You could send
him a telegram, or even just a postal. That's the polite way
to treat a husband, and might be handy in case he wanted
to say it with flowers, or anything like that."
"He isn't very likely to worry about where I am,"
faltered Natalie.
Mrs. Meigs was driven to wonder, had their quarrel
amounted to a final rupture? Carter's efforts to trace
Natalie, and the rumors about a young man seen with
her, and Natalie's nervousness to-day, and sudden
changes of mood-it all looked suspicious.
"Have you anything you want to tell me, Natalie?"
she asked.
Natalie flushed, and caught her lower lip with her
teeth to stop its trembling, just as she used to when she
was a child. Then her eyes fell before her mother's. "No,
nothing," she said. "What should I have to tell you?"
When they left the table, it was raining again. Natalie
prowled restlessly about the flat. "Like that game of
cribbage you were speaking of?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, not just now! If you were planning anything,
Mother, just go ahead with it as if I weren't here."
"I've got to telephone Cal Fletcher," Mrs. Meigs re-
membered.
She would ask Cal to stop and get her something on
his way here for dinner. That would remind him he was
coming, without telling him right out that she'd called
up to remind him. A magazine would do nicely; there
was a news-stand on the corner where he had to transfer.
Carefully thinking up a ten-cent magazine, Mrs. Meigs
took down the receiver.After some delay, Cal's landlady answered. Probably
the telephone at his boarding-house was down a back
passage; perhaps too the landlady considered the lodgers'
calls an imposition. Her tone was starchy enough as she
said, "Can't call Mr. Fletcher to the phone. He's sick."
"Sick? He was all right Sunday."
"He went out in the rain yesterday, and got wet. This
morning he had a chill, and didn't get up."
"Has he seen a doctor?"
"No. I wanted him to, but he wouldn't. Said he'd be
all right in a day or two. Oh!" The landlady's voice
changed. "This Mrs. Meigs, by any chance?"
"Yes, I'm Mrs. Meigs."
"Mr. Fletcher asked me a little while ago to call you
and tell you he can't come to supper. But I've been so
busy, I just didn't get around to it."
"Well, you tell him I'm sorry he's sick," said Mrs.
Meigs. "Make him drink a hot lemonade, if he'll take it.
I'll call up again in the morning."
She was disappointed at Cal's not coming. A little
worried about him, too. Men never knew enough to take
good care of themselves. He'd probably sat around in
wet shoes, and that was how he got this "chill."
Natalie had picked up a magazine and was pretending
to read. Obviously she wanted to be let alone, but Mrs.
Meigs wanted company. She decided to run in and see
Mrs. Bishop for a few minutes.
Mrs. Bishop had the best bedroom in her daughter's
flat. She was sitting in a padded arm-chair near the win-
dow, with her feet on a cushion. Some knitting lay on her
lap, but her hands were folded on it. She was watching
the rain.
Mrs. Meigs turned to Mrs. Bishop's daughter, who hadlet her in. "Find me a small table and two packs of cards,"
she directed briskly. "I'm going to let your mother beat
me at a few games of Russian bank."
"I wish Mother had your energy," said Grace admir-
ingly. "We do everything for her. But often she seems
so hard to rouse."
"Maybe you do too much for her," said Mrs. Meigs.
"She always seems lively enough to me. But then, I'm a
good little rouser."
Grace produced the cards and sat down to watch the
game. Mrs. Bishop was pleased to see her friend, but she
played listlessly. She seemed very old to-day. "And she's
years younger than I am!" thought Mrs. Meigs.
All at once she remembered what Cal Fletcher had
said about Dr. Manning. "Pretty feeble!" said Cal, ignor-
ing how his own joints creaked. She laughed out loud.
Mrs. Bishop looked up inquiringly.
"I was just thinking," said Mrs. Meigs, "how old the
other fellow always seems!"
Mrs. Bishop laughed at the remark; but behind her
mother's back, Grace scowled at Mrs. Meigs. Grace's
presence didn't help a bit, as the game dragged on. To
Mrs. Meigs, the too devoted daughter was a bogy. But
she tried to be just. Maybe even Grace all day was better
than being like Cal Fletcher, sick in a boarding-house
bedroom, old and lonely.
Cal must be looked out for, though; she should have
thought of that sooner. He couldn't afford a doctor for
every little snuffle or toe-ache. But she'd send her own
doctor to see him, and settle with Cal's pride when he
got better. She could fairly hear Cal say, with a reluc-
tant grin, "You always would have your own way about
everything, Carrie."At the end of the next game she excused herself,
though Mrs. Bishop hated to let her go. She wanted to
catch Dr. Stuart before he left his office.
As she stepped into her own hall, she heard Natalie's
voice. Natalie was telephoning, with an eye on the door.
"I'm sure there's some mistake. He expected the call.
May I leave word for him to call me? I'm at my mother's."
She repeated the number.
Mrs. Meigs advanced to take her turn at the instru-
ment. "I've just left word for Mrs. Pendergast to call me,"
said Natalie. "So if the telephone rings to-night, I may
as well answer."
"Suit yourself," said Mrs. Meigs shortly. Natalie might
know a Mrs. Pendergast; it wasn't the sort of name that
is made up in a hurry. But it was odd to refer to Mrs.
Pendergast as "him," and Mrs. Meigs had distinctly heard
that pronoun.
She herself was expecting an important call that eve-
ning. When Tip O'Neil had had his show-down with
Cecile, Mrs. Meigs ought to be the first to hear his news,
good or bad.XI
It was a restless evening. Natalie roamed through the
flat like an animal that resents its cage; she looked dead
tired, but she couldn't keep still. Mrs. Meigs knitted res-
olutely. But between her and her work there kept com-
ing Cal Fletcher, down with a cold and nothing to do
but count the cracks in the bedroom ceiling; and Cecile
laying her weary young head on her grandmother's
shoulder; and Mrs. Bishop at the window, like Whistler's
horrible portrait of his mother, staring straight ahead of
her, waiting for death.
They sat up till midnight; but the telephone refused
to ring. When she used to snatch her children from play
for the extinction of bedtime, Mrs. Meigs consoled them
by saying, "To-morrow'll be another day." But if to-
morrow was going to be another day like this one, she
wasn't sure she relished the prospect.
In the morning it was raining again; they were making
up this week for the fine weather last. But the news of
Cal was good. Mrs. Meigs telephoned Dr. Stuart, who
pronounced Cal's illness an ordinary cold. "He must be
careful, of course, at his age. I ordered him to stay in bed
for a day or two."
"You go up and see him again this afternoon," she
directed.
"Why don't you drop in for a while and cheer him
up?" suggested the doctor. "That is, if you're not afraid
of the rain."
"See Cal? See Cal in bed?" Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "If I
did such a thing the poor man would die of the shock.
You must remember he was brought up in the days when
people were modest."
She wasn't afraid of the rain. After lunch she wrapped
Natalie's large expensive book in several thicknesses of
paper and started down town to exchange it. Now that
Natalie was staying in the flat, it was a good time to give
her a hint.
She asked Natalie to go with her, but Natalie was still
hanging around the telephone. As a concession to the
weather, Mrs. Meigs rode in the street-car to the book-
store, where she selected two love stories, and a terrifying
affair called "The Night Club Hostess," with the picture
on the jacket of a girl menaced by two masked men with
revolvers. She didn't have the books wrapped, for on
the car going home she'd have time to dip into one of
them. The conductor would see that she got off at the
right corner.
With her books and umbrella, her hands were so full
that she rang for Anna to let her in. "Mr. Richard just
got here," said Anna.
Richard sat on the edge of the kitchen table, with a
glass of milk in one hand and a sandwich in the other.
"Hello, Gram!" he said. "I was hungry after driving,
and I always know where to come for a feed."
"Does that mean you haven't been home?" asked Mrs.
Meigs.
He grinned, and began to imitate Millicent. "I don't
see why you can't stay all night. It's ages since I've had
a good talk with you. It's a funny thing you can always
drive in to see some girl, but you never can spare a dayfor your own family. You guessed it, Gram. I haven't
been home."
"Then maybe you haven't heard the excitement in
your family," said Mrs. Meigs. "Cecile is threatening to
marry Roger Kane."
"Is she? She shows rotten taste," said Richard indif-
ferently. "But it's her own lookout."
"You'd protect your sister from a rattlesnake," hinted
Mrs. Meigs.
Richard guffawed. "When you have a prejudice, Gram,
you certainly ride it."
Of course Richard knew only half the story. If she had
told him the other half, he might have thought Cecile
guilty of something worse than "rotten taste." Roger
wasn't a rattlesnake, only a smug self-satisfied man twice
Cecile's age. It was marrying Roger and at the same time
being in love with Tip, that was intolerable. Or it would
be intolerable, if it happened.
Mrs. Meigs turned to ask, "Anna, did anybody call up
while I was out?"
"Not a soul, Mis' Meigs. The phone ain't rung all day."
Tip must have found it took longer than he had ex-
pected to make Cecile see reason. Perhaps he hadn't man-
aged to see her last night. No, of course he hadn't. Roger
would have claimed the first evening of their "engage-
ment." She must try to be patient.
She refilled Richard's glass of milk, and asked, "Are
you in town to see some girl? That's generally what
brings you."
"I am," said Richard. "I'm going to a dance. Luckily
this time it isn't a dress affair. I can change my collar
almost anywhere.""Are you going with the red-haired girl who fought
for you last week?"
"Lord, no!" said Richard with a grin. "That was last
week."
"You change pretty often, don't you?"
"That's kinder to the girls," said Richard. "Keeps 'em
from getting their hopes up."
Casual comradeship: that was what girls had to ex-
pect nowadays. Casual love-making too, of course; Rich-
ard would have thought the respectful "attentions" of
her girlhood a wonderful joke. "What would you do,"
asked Mrs. Meigs, "if one of your girls were to take you
seriously?"
"I'd run like hell," said Richard promptly. "As a mat-
ter of fact, though, there's not much danger. I diversify
my interests."
Richard was only a little younger than Tip O'Neil.
They both had the high spirits of youth and the flippancy
of their generation. There resemblance ceased. Tip was
impulsive, hot-tempered, likely to confuse what he saw
with what he wanted to see. Richard was far less generous
and much more self-indulgent. But Richard indulged
himself deliberately and intelligently; he was capable of
checking any impulse if it threatened his interests. Mrs.
Meigs couldn't picture his heart ever getting the better
of his head.
"Your father was in here yesterday," she remarked.
"He says business is bad."
"He's been saying that ever since I can remember,"
said Richard cheerfully. "That's Dad. But now I guess
it's really so."
"It has never occurred to you that you might leave
college and go into the business?""Of course it's occurred to me. In fact, Dad hinted
it to me pretty strongly last summer. But I decided against
it," said Richard, "for two reasons. I don't want to leave
college, you know, until my fun's up. And then, I don't
want to go into a run-down business and try to build
it up. It would be a darn sight easier, and more fun, to
build up a new business right from the beginning. Keep-
ing a family business going may be the correct idea; but
it doesn't thrill me especially."
Hard as nails, and perfectly clear-sighted. No mercy
for his worried, middle-aged father, no idea of throwing
himself into the breach. The worst of it was, Richard was
perfectly right. Ralph thought the proper course was to
go into the retail end, but he shrank from the enterprise
and the risk. Why should Richard do for him what Ralph
hadn't the nerve to do for himself? Anyhow Mrs. Meigs
didn't like the idea of Richard selling toasters and per-
colators over a counter.
Richard finished his lunch and began to wash his hands
at the sink. "Why don't you stay over night?" asked
Mrs. Meigs. "This is Friday. You might stay two nights
and drive back Sunday evening."
"Could I stay here?" asked Richard.
"Well, Natalie's using the spare room just now. But
I can move her in with me for a couple of nights."
"Better not, I guess," Richard decided. "If I stayed
here, Mother'd find out about it and have a few words
to say. I suppose she's tickled pink over Cecile's hooking
a man with so much money?"
"I haven't seen her since the hooking took place. But
I dare say," said Mrs. Meigs dryly, "Millicent is tickled
a deep scarlet."
At the front door Richard gave her a bear hug, andpatted the top of her head to show how far it was below
his own. "Some day I'll come here when I don't want
to eat," he promised.
"If you do, I'll be disappointed," said Mrs. Meigs. "I
just love to feed people."
She went to the window to wave after he got into his
car. He had stopped to greet a man getting out of another
car. The man was Jack. Jack and Richard always had a
lot to say to each other, in a tone of cheerful abuse. It
was too bad Jack hadn't a son of his own; he got on well
with young people.
Jack was grinning when he entered. "That nephew
of mine is getting sassier every day," he reported.
"He isn't any sassier than you were at his age," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, the sacred grandchildren!" Jack took a look
around. "Mill not here yet?"
"Did you expect to find Millicent here?"
"She telephoned me to meet her. Said she had some
news for me and it was too important to tell over the
telephone. Have you any idea what she meant?"
She meant Roger and Cecile, undoubtedly. "Sort of an
idea, though I don't see why she has to tell you here,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "I'm glad you've come, though. I've a
bone to pick with you. What did you mean, sending a
real estate man here yesterday to pester me?"
"I thought it would save time. Maybe though you
won't want to bother showing people through," Jack
admitted. "We can let the subletting go until after
you've moved out, if you like."
"You'd better let it go at least until you have my
consent to sublet," said Mrs. Meigs dryly. She was thank-
ful to be warned that Millicent was coming. She rear-ranged her new books on the living-room table, so that
the innocent jacket of "Sweet Clover" hid the wicked-
ness of "The Night Club Hostess."
Jack refused anything to eat, and lighted a cigarette.
"It's funny you've never learned to smoke, Mother," he
said.
"I have tried," admitted Mrs. Meigs with a chuckle.
"But the smoke got in my eyes and made me cough."
Jack looked a little shocked. "I didn't mean begin
now. It would be a bad habit to acquire at your age."
"It's a bad habit at yours," retorted Mrs. Meigs. "But
a person has to have some bad habits. I've often thought,
when I sit here alone of an evening, what fun it would
be to light up, and just smoke and rock, and rock and
smoke."
He caught her eye, and slowly his mouth widened.
"Perhaps you don't know how to smoke. Now if I were
to give you a lesson-"
Jack didn't believe she would take him up on that of-
fer. So she had to take him up. "All right. Give me a
cigarette," she demanded.
He lighted the match for her. "Draw in your breath,"
he ordered. But instead of drawing in her breath, she
blew it out. The match went out. He lighted another.
"Just take a single puff, and hold the smoke in your
mouth, but take the cigarette out," he insisted.
Miraculously the cigarette lighted. Once, twice, Mrs.
Meigs puffed. The taste in her mouth was like burning
wool, and her throat tickled. But she managed to keep
the cigarette going.
"That's the way!" said Jack approvingly.
In his high-school days she'd studied civics to keep
up with him and earn his respect. When he was awayat college she brushed up on her boarding-school French,
and wrote him letters beginning, "Mon cher fils." Since
he married, and especially since she'd had the grand-
children to interest herself in, she and Jack hadn't seen
so much of each other. His half-shocked approval made
her feel young again. She'd have learned to turn somer-
saults, if necessary, in order to keep Jack from feeling
that she was "just Mother."
They both started guiltily when the door-bell rang.
Mrs. Meigs crushed out her cigarette, and by mere force
of example Jack crushed out his. They were grinning
like a couple of idiots when Millicent sailed in on them.
She sniffed, and opened a window. "The air is close
here," she remarked. "Really, Mother, you should pay
more attention to ventilation."
"We called it just opening the windows when I was
young," said Mrs. Meigs. "I used to open windows be-
fore you were ever born, Millicent. You may not believe
it, but I did."
Millicent subsided heavily into a chair. "I've been so
busy to-day, if I hadn't telephoned Jack that I'd meet
him here, I wouldn't have tried to get it in."
"Maybe it could have waited," suggested Jack.
"But I shall be busier than ever from now on. Jack,
this is what I wanted to see you about," said Millicent.
"Cecile is going to marry Roger Kane."
"Roger Kane? He's a lot too old for Cecile," said Jack
bluntly.
"But he's such a fine, substantial man, and has such
charming manners! He brought me the loveliest flowers
to-day," said Millicent. "He and Cecile have gone this
afternoon to choose the engagement ring."
"I hope he has it charged on his account," said Mrs.Meigs. "Then he can get his money back when he has to
return it."
Millicent settled her with a look. To Jack she went on,
"I think we'll have a church wedding. So much is ex-
pected when a girl marries a wealthy man. We can't let
him think he's marrying into a family of paupers."
"Well, I'm glad if you're pleased," said Jack doubt-
fully. "But why drag me here to tell me this? I'm not
Cecile's guardian, you know."
"Any one could see why, I should think," said Milli-
cent. "I'll have to get Mother all moved and settled, be-
fore I turn to on the plans for Cecile's wedding."
So that was the idea, was it? Shove Mother, the old
nuisance, out of the way before you "turned to" at the
agreeable task of getting a rich man into the family. "I'm
afraid I'd be in the way of the wedding preparations,"
said Mrs. Meigs dryly.
"Oh, it's not that!" said Jack. "But there's no such
hurry. If Mother wants to wait a couple of months-"
"She ought to be moved and settled before cold
weather," said Millicent firmly.
"I don't want to move at all," said Mrs. Meigs. "I
don't know how many times I'll have to say that before
you believe it. But if you'll tell me, I'll keep count. And
as for the wedding you're licking your chops over, you'll
never get a chance to have it."
"How do you know?" asked Millicent, struck in spite
of herself. She often suspected Cecile of telling her grand-
mother things she didn't tell at home.
"Cecile has had a scrap with her best beau, and is en-
couraging Roger to spite him. It's an old trick, being
nice to a man just to make another man jealous. But
it still works.""If you mean that Irish whippersnapper, Cecile never
gave him a serious thought," said Millicent tartly.
Mrs. Meigs was fairly sure of her ground; sure enough,
at least, to have remained silent. But Millicent always
exasperated her into saying more than she meant.
"Seeing you're running the two things together, Mil-
licent," she said, "I'm willing to make a bet with you."
"That's no way to settle anything," began Millicent.
"It's a one-sided bet," interrupted Mrs. Meigs, "or I
wouldn't suggest it to you. But the day that Cecile mar-
ries Roger Kane, I'll move over to your house to stay-
and pull the bedclothes up over my head."
"You put things such a funny way," faltered Milli-
cent. "I'm not sure I understand what you mean."
"She means have the wedding first. Then she'll break
up here," Jack explained.
"That's the wrong way around," insisted Millicent.
"Mill, your head is pretty thick," said Jack with broth-
erly frankness. "You've been trying for ages to get
Mother to promise that she'd leave here and come to your
house to live. When she does promise, you don't get it."
"I promised only in case-" began Mrs. Meigs hastily.
"I know that. Only in case Cecile marries Roger Kane,"
repeated Jack.
"But she's going to marry him. So it all comes to the
same thing in the end," insisted Millicent.
Jack looked at his mother, and raised his eyebrows.
"It's all right. I get it," he said in a resigned tone. "And
I won't have anybody come to look at this place until you
give the word. You may forfeit a month or two in rent,
but that's simpler than being bothered."
He took Millicent away with him, on that understand-
ing. After they left, Natalie came from the spare room."Have you been in there all this time?" asked Mrs. Meigs
in surprise.
"I didn't feel like facing the family to-day," said
Natalie listlessly.
She was quieter to-night, but no happier. It was cozy
that evening, though, when the two of them sat down
together to read the new books; and Natalie showed her
dimples, and looked deliciously young and sweet for a
moment when she heard that her present had been ex-
changed for three.
It had been nice, having Richard come in to-day. Good
news too that Cal was better. His illness had worried her;
a strong breath of wind would blow him away. Jack
stood with Millicent in this silly idea of moving her.
Move her, indeed! But at least she had Jack for a witness
about her bet with Millicent, or her bargain, or what-
ever you chose to call it. It was a one-sided bargain-
but not on the side they thought it was.
It was odd, though, that she hadn't heard from Cecile
to-day. And there hadn't been a word from Tip since
yesterday morning, when he gave her his promise to
see Cecile and put his case up to her.
Cecile's despairing cry, "Love isn't enough! Love
doesn't last!" rang in her ears. Cecile's face kept coming
between her and her book. Mrs. Meigs rose and went to
the telephone.
Tip's number wasn't answered. That was good news.
It meant, of course, that he was with Cecile. It couldn't
mean anything else.
Mrs. Meigs half-regretted the rash promise that had
made her future hang on Cecile's. But when eleven o'clock
came, and then midnight, and still there was no word
from either Tip or Cecile, an ugly doubt raised its head.She'd been a fool to let Millicent exasperate her into
saying more than she meant. Yet she might as well mean
it, at that. Cecile's marriage to Roger, in Mrs. Meigs's
eyes, would be nothing short of a disaster. And if a dis-
aster happened to Cecile, it didn't make much difference
what happened to Mrs. Meigs. She had endured calam-
ities in her life. The death of her baby, the dark-eyed
little girl who had come between Millicent and Edith-
her husband's death-her own transplanting here. She'd
lived through them somehow. Yes, and found herself
enjoying life again, to her own astonishment.
But her future was too short now. Her eyes wandered
across to Natalie, drooping in her chair, pretending to
read, Natalie, who had nothing to tell" her mother. Ce-
cile looked like Natalie; everybody had always spoken
of the resemblance. But if Mrs. Meigs ever saw the day
when Cecile looked as Natalie did to-night, she would
be ready to quit. Life would hold nothing further for
her.XII
SATURDAY morning was always a busy time. Mrs.
Meigs had to keep an eye on the weekly cleaning; Anna
simply didn't see dirt. Then there were the preparations
for a big noon dinner on Sunday, and perhaps a com-
pany supper. And Saturday morning always seemed a
natural time to take back your library books, and call
for your shoes at the cobbler's, and regularly straighten
up all the loose ends of the week.
Mrs. Meigs wasn't half through when Cecile came in.
She was paler than usual, and looked in some mysterious
way older. But her manner was as brisk and offhand as
ever. "Hurry and get your bonnet on, Caroline," she
commanded. "I'm going to take you downtown for
lunch."
"But Natalie's going to be here for lunch," objected
Mrs. Meigs.
"She's included in the invitation," said Cecile.
Natalie refused the invitation, but urged her mother
to accept. Mrs. Meigs was easy to persuade. It was more
than a week since she had lunch downtown.
When they reached the street, Mrs. Meigs started
toward the corner, to wait for the street-car. But Cecile
guided her to the curb. "I'm driving you downtown,
Caroline."
There stood a shiny expensive roadster, enameled in
two shades of green. It had trick panels of plate glass
that could be slanted in every direction, and an amountof polished metal that was positively annoying. Mrs.
Meigs looked at it with distaste. "Where'd you get that?"
"It's mine if I want it," explained Cecile. "I thought
I'd take you for a spin, and if you liked it, we'd keep it."
Roger Kane was buying it for her, of course. Mrs.
Meigs had a good mind not to set foot in that car. "Load-
ing you with presents so soon, is he?" she demanded.
"He knew I've been wanting a car for a long time. I
think it was darn nice of him to get it for me," said Ce-
cile warmly.
Mrs. Meigs allowed herself to be established, and a rug
spread over her knees. The upholstery yielded to her
body; the seat beside the driver's fitted her as if it had
been made for her. It was sweet of Cecile to take her for
a drive the very first thing even if she didn't quite be-
lieve that the fate of the car hung on her say-so.
"I thought we'd have lunch first, and then pick up
Cal Fletcher and take him for a drive," suggested Ce-
cile. "If he's getting over a cold, it will be an easy way
for him to get outdoors."
"I hadn't got around to call up this morning," said
Mrs. Meigs. "But it seems as if by this time he must be
able to be out."
The wind whipped Mrs. Meigs's veil over her face.
Cecile reached across her, and closed one of the glass
panels. The sky was heavily overcast, and the wind
smelled like winter.
"It's Saturday, isn't it?" said Mrs. Meigs. "It doesn't
seem possible that just a week ago to-day we were eating
lunch outdoors, and basking in the sunshine."
Cecile's eyes were on the road ahead; her straight
young profile was severe. "No," she said with tight lips.
"It doesn't seem possible."Mrs. Meigs tried to sound casual. "Have you heard
from Tip lately? I saw him the other day, and he was
telling me about his new plans."
"That must have been very interesting," said Cecile
dryly. "No, I haven't heard from him."
"Maybe you've missed his calls," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Maybe," said Cecile.
Mrs. Meigs thought scandalous things about the ab-
sent Tip. Two days' procrastination had done his cause
infinite harm. Did he imagine Cecile was going to wait
around until he got good and ready? Much as she dis-
liked Roger, Mrs. Meigs gave him credit for common
sense. He knew that girls were like everything else in
life: the people who went after 'em were the people who
got 'em.
Then she remembered Tip's eagerness only two days
ago, and his frankness about his own shortcomings. How
his vividness had colored life even for an old woman,
and how it lifted Cecile out of herself! "Cecile," she said
suddenly, "I think something has happened to Tip."
Cecile glanced around, and smiled ironically. "Very
likely it has. Maybe Roger has hired somebody to kid-
nap him. That would be one way of getting rid of a
hated rival. Tip's probably lying in a dark cellar at this
moment, guarded by two men with revolvers."
"You'll at least wait and see?" urged Mrs. Meigs
eagerly. "I can't understand your not hearing a word
from him. The whole thing seems to me like a jig-saw
puzzle that has half the pieces mixed with another puz-
zle. Don't you think you might at least wait until you
hear what Tip has to say for himself?"
"Tip always has so much to say for himself," objected
Cecile. "He's certainly in strong with you, though. Any-body would almost think he was the grandchild and I the
outsider."
"Tip my grandchild indeed!" said Mrs. Meigs scorn-
fully. "I guess I still know an upstanding young man
when I see one. If I were only thirty years younger, I'd
have a try for Tip myself."
"Then I'd wish you joy of him," said Cecile.
"Thirty? Maybe while I'm wishing I'd better wish for
fifty years off," said Mrs. Meigs. "But give me only thirty,
and I'd make you sit up and take notice, Cecile."
"You often do, even as it is," said Cecile.
Having a joke with Cecile made Mrs. Meigs feel all
warm inside. She must have been more worried than she'd
realized. In the long run, things came out well enough.
You made them come out well.
They drew up before the town's best restaurant, Ris-
pigliosi's. All the best restaurants nowadays seemed to
be run by Italians. At the luncheon hour, Rispigliosi's
was always crowded.
Mrs. Meigs loved lunching out. The food wasn't gen-
erally as good as you got at home, but at any rate it
was a change. She liked the crowd and the noise and ex-
citement. As they moved down the room to the table
Cecile had reserved, Mrs. Meigs was pleasantly conscious
of the glances the two of them drew: the old lady in
her widow's bonnet, the pretty girl in her smart fur
jacket and tight felt hat.
Just as they were sitting down, a man passed them on
his way out. He was about thirty years old, and handsome
in a superficial fashion; but he had thin lips and cold eyes.
There was something disagreeable in the way he looked
Cecile up and down. Cecile spoke to him casually."Who's that?" asked Mrs. Meigs sharply.
Cecile dimpled. "It would surprise him to find there
was anybody in our fair city who didn't know who he
is. That's Charley Churchill, one of our most prominent
bachelors. Not just an unmarried man, you know, but
a bachelor with all the trimmings."
"I didn't like the way he stared at you."
"Oh, he wouldn't be interested in me! Charley never
goes in for girls. He's afraid one of them might hook him.
He's the sort of fellow who hovers around married
women."
Mrs. Bishop's daughter had seen Natalie lunching at
this very restaurant with a young man. "Do you know
any of the married women he hovers around?" asked
Mrs. Meigs slowly.
"I know more than one of them," answered Cecile.
"He believes there's safety in numbers. That sort of man
is not to be taken seriously. What you going to eat,
Caroline?"
"You order for me," said Mrs. Meigs. "I've been hav-
ing to think about food all morning."
"Clear soup, chicken à la king, imported endive with
French dressing," Cecile decided. "We'll order dessert
later. Coffee with your lunch, Caroline? Two big cups
of coffee with the chicken, waiter, and bring the salad
when you bring the soup."
She laid down the menu, and looked about her. "More
women than men here on a Saturday," she remarked.
Mrs. Meigs could always talk freely to Cecile. "I've
been worried about Natalie lately," she confided. "She's
been staying with me while Carter is out of town, and
she seems awfully fidgety and depressed.""I didn't know Carter was out of town," said Cecile.
"I thought I saw him yesterday."
"I dare say you did," said Mrs. Meigs. "He probably
isn't any more out of town than you are. I think they've
had some trouble. I wonder if it could possibly be over-"
"People can quarrel over anything, if they feel like
quarreling," interrupted Cecile. "Natalie hasn't had such
a gay life. If I were in her place, I'd amuse myself too."
Cecile knew or suspected more than she would ad-
mit. But it was comforting to be here with her, lunch-
ing and watching the crowd. Cecile set herself to be
entertaining; she retailed all the latest gossip, and told
two new stories that she wouldn't have dared to tell
Millicent. Mrs. Meigs enjoyed the gossip, and the sparkle
with which Cecile told it. She enjoyed her lunch, too.
She had always found eating a great resource.
They topped off the meal with French pastry. Mrs.
Meigs liked to have the waiter bring the tray around;
it was such an interesting moment when you hesitated be-
tween the chocolate éclair and the round cake topped with
pecans and a bit of candied cherry. You always regretted
afterward that you hadn't taken a different cake; but
at least it was an excuse for a second cup of coffee.
Cecile established her grandmother in the car again.
"Now for Cal," she said. "I don't quite like asking him to
sit in the rumble seat. Suppose he could squeeze in here
between us?"
"He doesn't take up much room," said Mrs. Meigs.
"And if we put him out behind, where he'd have to shout
his remarks, he wouldn't say anything."
They drove around to the boarding-house where Cal
lived. In the days of Mrs. Meigs's early married life, it
had been one of the finest residences in town. Now itneeded paint, and new shingles, and almost everything
else that a house could need. But lace curtains, not too
dingy, were looped back in the downstairs front windows
so that the light could get at the sword-ferns; and a care-
fully polished brass plate announced, "Table Board."
Cecile ran up the rickety steps, and pulled at the old-
fashioned door-bell. The landlady answered; she was
the hard-working grim type of landlady, not the slov-
enly. Cecile exchanged half a dozen sentences with her.
Then, to Mrs. Meigs's surprise, she went in.
She came back five minutes later, and opened the
door of the car on Mrs. Meigs's side. "Caroline, I wish
you'd come in a minute. I want you to take a look at
Cal."
The seriousness with which she spoke made Mrs. Meigs's
heart contract. "Do you mean he's worse again? The doc-
tor said yesterday-"
"I don't know much about illnesses," said Cecile.
"Maybe it's mostly that he's old and weak. But you'd bet-
ter see for yourself."
A dim stairway, grotesquely lighted by a window of
imitation stained glass. A second stairway, with no car-
pet. She hadn't guessed that Cal always had to drag
himself up two flights after meals. A long corridor, with
a slightly moldy smell. Then Cecile knocked at a door,
and Cal's voice, weak but recognizable, bade them
come in.
He was propped up in bed, wearing an old coat over
his nightshirt. After the darkness of the halls, his room
seemed bright; it had two windows. The bed was painted
iron, with the paint scaling off, the dresser dingy pine,
varnished to look like oak. There wasn't much other
furniture, but the room seemed very full: the wallswere covered with photographs and old framed pictures,
one chair was full of newspapers and another of cloth-
ing, there was a dish of apples on the dresser and a heap
of paper boxes in one corner, and a laundry package had
been opened and then deserted.
In bed that way, with his chin covered with gray
stubble, Cal looked like an old, old man. But he was
faithful to his principles. "I wanted to get dressed and
come downstairs to see you, Carrie," he said. "But Cecile
wouldn't let me."
"Cecile was quite right," said Mrs. Meigs. "She often
is right, though you mustn't tell her I said so."
"Now that you are here, won't you sit down and make
me a little visit?" asked Cal. "That is, if you can find
a place to sit."
Cecile cleared a chair for Mrs. Meigs. She herself sat on
the edge of the bed. It was a pretty sight, the fresh clear-
eyed girl sitting on the old man's bed. The thought flashed
through Mrs. Meigs's mind, "Old age has its privileges."
But Cal had eyes only for the old woman. "What you
been doing with yourself, Carrie?"
"Amusing myself," said Mrs. Meigs briskly. "And, of
course, telling my children and grandchildren what they
ought to do. To-day I'm giving Cecile my expert opin-
ion on a car. It's really a very nice car."
"I don't believe you ever get lonesome, do you?"
"Lonesome? Lately I've been thinking that the great-
est treat on earth would be to have some time to myself."
"You're a wonder, the way you keep occupied. It's dif-
ferent with me," said Cal wistfully. "I've been alone such
a lot since my wife died. Alone-such a lot."
Cecile got up and walked to the window. In her eyes
the old man was pathetic. Mrs. Meigs's sympathy wastinged with impatience. Cal could have "kept occupied"
a week just straightening up this room. He was tidy
about his person; but how did he every find anything in
this disorder?
"You should come over oftener to see me," she said.
"You needn't wait for an invitation, ever. Just call up, to
be sure I'm home."
"I don't want to be a nuisance," said Cal. "But you
ought to know how I like to come to your house. It's
always sort of a party for me. In fact seeing you any-
where is a party for me, Carrie."
"What nonsense!" said Mrs. Meigs. Just the same she
liked it. In her day a man paid a woman compliments.
Cal Fletcher had been paying her compliments for sixty
years.
She tried to live up to his idea of her. She told him
about Cecile's car, though she let him think Cecile's fa-
ther was buying it for her. She described her birthday
presents, and the exchange of one. "And I've learned a
new double at bridge," she said. "It's utterly insane, but
I'm going to spring it on my old ladies the next time
we play. They'll all get mad, but that will do them good.
Shake 'em up a bit."
Cal laughed at the idea of the old ladies being shaken
up. His laughter was interrupted by a spell of hard
coughing. Cecile turned away from the window. "Where
does it hurt you when you cough?" she demanded.
"It doesn't hurt me," gasped Cal.
Mrs. Meigs looked at him severely. "That'll do to tell.
You ought to have a mustard plaster on that cough."
"I'd rather have the cough," protested Cal.
"I don't blame you for that," said Cecile. "But you
must hurry and get well, so that you can come for aride in my new car some day soon. Caroline, it's time
we were getting along."
"You've only just come," said Cal, with a look of dis-
appointment.
"We'll come again to-morrow," said Cecile.
"Will you really? Then I believe I shall begin to like
being sick. In all the years I've known your grandmother,
this is the first time she has ever been to see me."
Mrs. Meigs looked hard at him. She had thought he'd
be shocked at the idea of receiving her in his bedroom.
Old age certainly has its privileges.
As she started down the stairs she said, "He was dis-
appointed that we left so soon. But I suppose having peo-
ple here does wear him out."
"I don't think he ought to be left here," said Cecile.
"He ought to go to a hospital."
"A hospital? He'd hate it."
"Maybe you're right. It won't do to make him think
he's too sick," said Cecile.
"He isn't very sick. I'm glad I saw him. But I don't
like the sound of that cough. I'm going to send the doc-
tor around again in the morning."
Call the doctor for Cal. Try once more to get Tip
on the telephone; Sunday morning he'd surely be at
home. Maybe hint to Natalie that Cecile had seen Car-
ter. It was going to be a busy Sunday morning. Think-
ing over all she had to do the next day, Mrs. Meigs was
silent in the car on the way home.
Much as she'd enjoyed the expedition, she loved the
feeling of getting home. Getting in where it was warm
and quiet, with your own things all about you-there
was nothing else quite like that. Cecile wouldn't come
in; indeed it was a good deal for a girl only two daysengaged to have already spent so much time on her
grandmother.
Mrs. Meigs let herself in with her latch-key, and paused
at the hall table to look for mail. Through the door to
the living-room she heard an agitated voice-Natalie's
voice. Mrs. Meigs opened the door.
Natalie was alone; she was talking over the telephone.
She saw her mother enter; but her agitation overwhelmed
her discretion. "But he is there! He must be there!" she
protested. "Why do you keep telling me over and over
that he isn't there? He's got to talk to me! He needn't
think he can get away with this!"
Mrs. Meigs walked up to the telephone, and covered
the mouthpiece with her hand. "Take a long breath and
count ten, Natalie," she said coolly. "Making a spec-
tacle of yourself to some switchboard girl isn't going
to get you anywhere."
Natalie dropped the instrument; the receiver swung
free. Mrs. Meigs hung it up, and set the telephone back
from the edge of the table, out of harm's way.
Natalie had sunk forward on the table, and was crying
like a child, with great noisy sobs that shook her from
head to foot. "Who is it you're so anxious to see?" asked
Mrs. Meigs. "Carter?"
"No, never, never!" choked Natalie. "I never want to
see Carter again."
"I hope it isn't that Churchill person?"
Natalie looked up, with tears running down her cheeks.
"What do you know about Charles Churchill?"
"Nothing," said Mrs. Meigs, "except that he has the
reputation of being popular with the ladies."
"That isn't true! flared Natalie. "They may run
after him, but he doesn't care for any of them. Peoplesay so many mean things; but Charley told me him-
self"
"Have you separated from Carter on his account?" in-
terrupted Mrs. Meigs.
Natalie rose to her feet. "Carter turned me out."
"Why didn't you tell me that in the first place?" de-
manded Mrs. Meigs. "You must have known I'd be on
your side."
"I was afraid you'd think of the scandal it might
make."
"I don't see why there need be any scandal," said Mrs.
Meigs firmly. "The thing to do is talk it over quietly,
and see what's best to be done; not lose your head, and
screech over the telephone."
"But I expected Charley to look me up right away,"
mumbled Natalie, crumpling her wet handkerchief into
a ball. "I waited for him, and days went by, and I didn't
hear, and then I tried to get in touch with him, thinking
maybe something had happened"
Mrs. Meigs took a clean handkerchief from her hand-
bag, and passed it to her. "I wouldn't cry over spilled
milk. Your precious Charley undoubtedly realizes that
there's a difference between playing around with a silly
married woman and marrying her himself."
A dreadful paleness came over Natalie's face; she
looked as if she were going to faint. Mrs. Meigs repented
her plain speech. "Sit down," she said gently. "Don't
mind my scolding, dear."
