Natalie sank into a chair. Mrs. Meigs, standing beside
her began to stroke her hair. "Mother's girl," she said.
"Mother's poor little girl. Do you love this Churchill
person, darling?"
Natalie nodded, and clutched her mother. Mrs. Meigsfelt something cold at her heart. Natalie loved the man,
and he was avoiding her. Many things had changed since
Mrs. Meigs's day; but the shame of loving unloved was
just what it always had been.
Not for worlds would Mrs. Meigs have revealed to
Natalie that she had seen Churchill this very day, and
he had taken an eyeful of Cecile. "He may be-just
hesitating," she fibbed.
Natalie looked up. "Oh, do you really think so?"
"I hope so. Anyhow in one way there's no harm done.
It was high time you left Carter, I'm afraid. You can stay
here with me, and let Carter do what he pleases. Do you
think he wants a divorce?"
"That's what he's threatening."
"Then we'll just have to beat him to it," said Mrs.
Meigs calmly.
"But Carter says he's going to get the divorce." Nata-
lie's very ears were crimson with shame. In a barely audi-
ble tone she went on, "He's going to charge-that he
caught Charley and me together.”
Mrs. Meigs flamed with anger. "Against his own wife
-he's going to tell a lie like that?"
Natalie drew away from her mother's protecting arm.
"It isn't a lie," she whispered. "He did catch us-in
Charley's flat."
Mrs. Meigs had a feeling of falling through space. You
knew things like that happened. If you stopped to think
you realized that they were going on all the time. But you
expected them to happen to other people's children. Not
to your own. Never, never to yours.XIII
SHE had Natalie drink a glass of milk, and got her to
bed. Now that she had confided her trouble to her
mother, Natalie seemed to feel easier. She sank into deep
slumber, and lay without stirring.
Mrs. Meigs moved restlessly about the apartment. Her
thoughts buzzed in her head. Only this morning her life-
long philosophy had supported her: things came out all
right in the end, because you made them come out that
way.
But to make this thing come out right, she should
have begun twenty years ago. She had made a point of
not interfering in her children's matrimonial plans. She
knew better now. She was going to interfere up to her
shoulders in her grandchildren's.
She sat up until midnight to call Tip O'Neil. Then
she read until two, and got out of bed to call him. When
the operator persisted in reporting, "No answer," she
talked to the chief operator, and to Information, and to
a young man with a brogue, who was very nice and
sympathetic, but didn't help her out a bit.
Tip had vanished, just when there was the greatest
need of him. Perhaps his new job had taken him out of
town; but she couldn't trace him through his office,
because he hadn't told her what life insurance company
he was working for. Shivering a little, because the steam
had gone down, Mrs. Meigs sat at her huge old secretary
and wrote a note addressed to Tip at his flat. She badehim telephone her the minute he returned to town. She
left the note lying where it would catch her eye the first
thing in the morning.
She woke with a stale feeling, as if she had slept with
the windows shut. She knew something terrible had hap-
pened yesterday, but at first she couldn't remember what
it was.
Soon enough it all come back. She closed her lips
grimly, glad that she had certain things she must at-
tend to. The first thing after breakfast, she sent Anna
out to mail her note to Tip. Then she telephoned Dr.
Stuart, and asked him to go see Cal Fletcher this morn-
ing.
In about an hour Dr. Stuart called her back. "Look
here, Mrs. Meigs," he demanded. "Have you any influ-
ence with Mr. Fletcher?"
"I have if anybody has," answered Mrs. Meigs with a
chuckle. "What's he done now? Got out of bed and
dressed because it's Sunday?"
"He won't get out of bed to-day, or for quite a while,"
said Dr. Stuart grimly.
"You mean he's worse?"
"I mean he's got pneumonia. I want him taken to a
hospital, but he says he won't go."
"Pneumonia," which would ordinarily have sounded
in her ears like a thunderclap, was the lesser of the threat-
ening evils. She had a vision of Cal being dragged away
from the cluttered room which was the only home he
knew, Cal thrust into a hospital ward, echoing to the
lightest tread, and smelling of antiseptics. That was the
way they treated paupers!
"He's quite right," said Mrs. Meigs firmly. "He can
have a nurse where he is, can't he?""He can, but it would be better-"
"You get a nurse, and tell Cal I said he was to have
her. You can tell him too that I'll be around this after-
noon to see how he likes her. I'll advance the money for
the nurse, and he can pay me back when he gets better.
But I don't want to hear any more talk about hospitals."
She hung up the receiver, and sat down abruptly in
her rocking-chair. Then she had time to think, "Pneu-
monia!"
Natalie came to see what was the matter. For a mo-
ment Mrs. Meigs looked at Natalie with actual hostility.
She wasn't old, and she wasn't sick. She was in a mess
now, but she had years and years ahead of her to get
straightened out, to go on living. Nobody has a right
to complain except the very old. They are the only ones
who have nothing ahead.
Cecile didn't telephone. Doubtless her plans for the
day centered around Roger. Mrs. Meigs told Anna to
have dinner at half past twelve instead of one. As soon
as dinner was over, she was going to see with her own eyes
whether they'd made things comfortable for Cal.
She was tying her bonnet strings when Mrs. Bishop
knocked at the door. She was quite excited and tremu-
lous. "My dear, the loveliest thing! Grace wants me to
have my friends for bridge to-morrow afternoon. You
can come, can't you?"
"Bridge?" echoed Mrs. Meigs scornfully. She had more
troubles than she could count on her fingers. And people
expected her calmly to sit down and play bridge with
them!
"It's the first time for ages that Grace has felt she
could entertain for me," fluttered Mrs. Bishop. "I'vebeen here so many times, and I'm so glad to have a chance
to return your hospitality."
"I'm sure it's lovely of Grace to go to all that trouble,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "But I've just had word that Cal Fletcher
is very sick. He hasn't a friend on earth nearer than me.
Couldn't your daughter play in my place?"
"But a party without you would be no party at all!"
protested Mrs. Bishop. Her withered old face, which had
been so bright a minute before, quivered with disap-
pointment.
"There, my dear, I'll come if Cal's all right! I'll come
with pleasure," Mrs. Meigs lied valiantly. "Will it be all
right if I let you know in the morning?"
"Yes, let me know. I think I'll tell Grace you're com-
ing. If I tell her anything else, she might want to post-
pone the party. Then Miss Lamb would be so disap-
pointed."
"Yes. Poor Miss Lamb!" A hint of her usual twinkle
appeared in Mrs. Meigs's eyes. "It must be awful to be
an old maid for over seventy years, don't you think?"
"Indeed it must!" In the glamour of her approaching
party, Mrs. Bishop forgot how often she had envied
Miss Lamb the comparative freedom she must enjoy in
the Old Ladies' Home. "Think of not having any chil-
dren to follow in your footsteps."
"That isn't the only thing she's missed. I wonder if
Miss Lamb ever had a beau?"
"She told me once that she did. She was engaged to a
young man, but he died."
All the old maids in Mrs. Meigs's experience had been
engaged to a young man who died. Of course, it might
happen; but it couldn't always happen. "I wish Dr. Man-ning would turn his attentions on her," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Still, it's no kindness to wish her that."
It was a long street-car ride to Cal's boarding-house.
At the corner where she transferred, a fruit-stand was
open. Mrs. Meigs bought two pounds of sweet red grapes
to take to Cal. The nurse probably wouldn't let him eat
them; but in Cal's and Mrs. Meigs's young days, nobody
could be officially sick unless a dish of grapes presented
by a friend stood on a table at the bedside.
She interviewed the landlady first, and gave her five
dollars "for your extra trouble." The landlady, who had
been all set to complain that the other boarders objected
to having any one so sick in the house, produced an af-
fable smile. She looked as if the exercise hurt her face.
"I don't suppose Cal has had a free five dollars in the
last twenty years," reflected Mrs. Meigs, toiling up the
first of the long flights of stairs. "When you're old, it's
tough not to have money enough."
At the top of the second flight, she paused to get her
breath. A door at the end of the hall opened a couple of
inches, and somebody peeked to see who was coming up,
and which door they went in at. "A regular boarding-
house!" thought Mrs. Meigs.
Cal was propped up on pillows, to ease his breathing.
But the covers were drawn neatly to his chin, giving him
something the effect of a head without a body. He man-
aged to get a hand free to shake Mrs. Meigs's. The nurse
tucked him in again, and smiled at Mrs. Meigs. She was
a red-haired woman about forty, with a tendency to
copper color along her nose. Her manner to Cal was
pleasant, but full of authority.
Mrs. Meigs smiled back at the nurse. "I'm not going
to stay long," she promised. "And I won't let Cal talk.It's something I've been looking forward to for a great
many years, having Cal where he's just got to listen."
The nurse retreated to the other side of the room, and
began to tidy the bureau. Cal scowled at her back, and
whispered hoarsely, "I don't like the idea of a strange
woman messing around among my things."
"No. She'll get them into such good order, you won't
be able to find a thing when you get up."
Cal chuckled feebly. Laughing made him cough. The
nurse glanced around. Mrs. Meigs produced and pre-
sented the grapes. The nurse looked at them doubtfully,
but took them to the bathroom to wash.
"What's her name?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Mrs. Barclay."
"Mrs., did you say?"
"She's a widow. Grass, I think," said Cal resentfully.
Mrs. Meigs's eyes twinkled. "It's hard for a woman
who's had a home of her own to come down to taking
care of cranky old men for a living."
Cal grinned reluctantly. "I suppose I'm lucky to have
such good care. Dr. Stuart told me you insisted on a
nurse; but I don't see how I'm going to pay her."
"Dr. Stuart will see to that. You can pay him back
when you get around to it. Besides, she gets only twenty-
five dollars a week," said Mrs. Meigs firmly.
"Only twenty-five dollars? Why, when I was young
the neighbor women used to come in when there was
sickness, and take care of the patient just out of good
will."
"That care was worth just about what it cost," said
Mrs. Meigs. "I remember when my father had pneu-
monia, they sealed up the windows, and hung a blanket
over the door to keep out drafts."Cal glanced at the two windows, both open top and
bottom. "Well, if you ask me, that was a pretty good
way to do."
"Cal, you're an old-timer," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Yes, and I'm proud of it," whispered Cal. Really, a
little pneumonia must do a man good. Not in years had
she seen Cal so self-assertive.
She began to tell him about Mrs. Bishop's proposed
party. It was the only thing which had happened in days
that you could talk about to a sick person. "Oh, you'll
have to go!" said Cal. "Don't you remember, you're go-
ing to teach them that double?"
She had planned to see him again to-morrow. Now she
couldn't; he'd worry if he thought she was missing a
party. He didn't seem nearly so sick as she had expected.
But when the nurse came back with the grapes-lucky if
she hadn't washed them in iodoform-Mrs. Meigs rose to
go. At the bottom of her intrepid soul there were only
two things she was afraid of: elevators that went down
too fast, so you felt as if somebody had grabbed out the
pit of your stomach, and trained nurses.
At the door, she glanced back. Cal thought she had
gone. He'd been "keeping up" before her. Now his eyes
were closed, and his mouth had dropped open. The look
of him frightened her.
She beckoned the nurse into the hall. "Is there a chance
of his getting well?" she whispered.
"There's always a chance," said Mrs. Barclay brightly.
Mrs. Meigs looked her scorn. Nurses never would tell
you anything. There was no chance of getting at Dr.
Stuart on a Sunday night; but as soon as she could reach
him, he'd tell her the truth.In the morning Mrs. Meigs called Dr. Stuart; but he
had left on his rounds. So she ordered some flowers to
be sent to Cal, and busied herself around the flat till
lunch-time, when she began to get ready for Mrs. Bishop's
party. Her spirits rose. If you couldn't see past your
troubles, the sensible thing was to turn your back on
them. Nero deserved credit for not trying to act like a
fireman when he wasn't one.
Mrs. Meigs was the last to arrive at the party. The
others were playing "three-hand" while they waited.
Daughter Grace hovered about them, keeping a watchful
eye.
"Hello, Mrs. Meigs!" quavered Miss Lamb. "We were
just wondering whether this is a birthday party."
"Dear, no!" said Mrs. Bishop. "I'm too old to celebrate
my birthdays."
"The older you are, the more point there is to celebrat-
ing," said Mrs Meigs.
"Perhaps Mrs. Bishop doesn't want us to know how
old she is," hinted Mrs. Cronkhite. "Now I've no objec-
tion to owning, I'm fifty-nine."
"You've been owning that ever since I've known you,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "Isn't it about time you shoved up to
sixty-one?"
Mrs. Cronkhite's face turned purple. Mrs. Meigs
looked her unflinchingly in the eye. There was a mo-
ment's dreadful silence. Then Miss Lamb faltered,
"Birthdays are the nicest days we have at the Home. We
always have a cake and ice-cream. We've had four this
month."
"Do you have candles on the cakes?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"The matron says it would take too many. But I wishwe might have, say one candle for every ten years.
Though maybe that would make it look like a child's
cake."
"Second childhood," said Mrs. Cronkhite, and laughed
at her own joke. She had a booming laugh like a man's,
and though she laughed seldom, when she did it always
cleared the air for her. "I guess I'm an old silly to think
I can keep on fooling people," she said, a little shame-
faced. "I'm really sixty-six."
"You don't look it," said Mrs. Meigs politely. "Isn't
it much better to tell how old you are, and have people
say you don't look it, than the other thing?"
It wouldn't do, of course, for them to go on being
so polite as all that. Now was a good moment to introduce
her informative double of a two bid. Mrs. Meigs ex-
plained it.
"I don't see that. I don't see it at all," said Mrs. Cronk-
hite flatly. "A double of a two bid is a business double.
It always has been so, and it always will be."
"But over a two bid of any suit," insisted Mrs. Meigs,
"you may want to tell your partner you have strength
in everything else."
"But then your partner would be expected to take
you out, wouldn't she?" faltered Miss Lamb. "I never
know anyhow when to take out, and if I'm going to have
so many more times to do it "
دو
"Taking out is idiotic," snapped Mrs. Cronkhite. "Go
ahead and bid your cards, I say."
"But I don't see," said Mrs. Bishop, "how a person ever
knows, on this new system, what her partner wants!"
Mrs. Meigs explained again. The other three followed
her tensely. "I see," boomed Mrs. Cronkhite. "But I don't
agree at all.""I don't see!" and "I still don't see!" insisted the other
two.
Mrs. Meigs smiled broadly. This would give Miss Lamb
something to take back to the Old Ladies' Home, and
Mrs. Bishop something to think about beside the weather.
From a scoffer at the two-double, Mrs. Meigs changed to
its ardent partizan.
All at once she felt a hand on her shoulder. She looked
up into the face of daughter Grace. "I really don't think
you'd better keep telling them about that," whispered
Grace. "It excites them too much."
Mrs. Meigs seethed. Don't excite them, indeed! Treat
your mother and her friends like children, who mustn't
be told Bluebeard too near bedtime! She thought of so
many good answers that she hesitated among them. In
the pause, she caught Mrs. Bishop's eyes fixed pleadingly
on her.
She couldn't use any of her good answers. Grace had
too many chances of taking it out on Mrs. Bishop.
Mrs. Meigs played silently, moodily. She was so de-
pressed that once or twice she led out her trumps.
When the refreshments came, cocoa had been substi-
tuted for coffee. "I thought this wouldn't keep you awake
to-night," Grace explained brightly.
And they all loved coffee better than anything else
on earth!
"I'm surprised Grace didn't feed us animal crackers,"
Mrs. Meigs whispered to Mrs. Cronkhite.
"She does treat us as if we were children," agreed Mrs.
Cronkhite. "This is veal night at my boarding-house,
too."
"You come home with me," said Mrs. Meigs. "I can
stretch to an extra plate, and I've my daughter stayingwith me. The youngest, Mrs. Brooks. I think you'll like
her."
It was almost like bragging to introduce Natalie to
Mrs. Cronkhite, after Mrs. Bishop's daughter. As they
settled down in the living-room, a mood of recklessness
came over Mrs. Meigs. She told Anna to hold dinner half
an hour. Then she telephoned for a taxi, and put on her
bonnet.
She had suddenly thought what a funny story she
could make of that cocoa. She was going to tell it to Cal
Fletcher. And yes, she thought she would tell him that
Grace Whitney had fed her mother's friends animal
crackers. It would be such fun to hear his shocked ex-
clamation, "Oh, Carrie! Not really?" and then to watch
his sheepish smile as it dawned on him, "Oh, that's one
of your jokes!"
The landlady admitted her. Through from the kitchen
came the nurse, carrying a steaming bowl. She stopped
when she saw Mrs. Meigs. "You've come to find out how
Mr. Fletcher is? He had a restless night. But on the whole,
things are unchanged."
"I've come to tell him a good story," said Mrs. Meigs
with a chuckle. "He'll sleep better to-night, after he's
heard this."
"I'm afraid I can't let you see him to-night," said the
nurse.
"Not let me see him? Not let me see Cal Fletcher?"
"He really mustn't be disturbed. He's too weak to
stand excitement. But on the whole, his condition is
good."
That was nurse language for "He's worse." Mrs. Meigs
looked at the landlady for confirmation of her suspicions.The landlady became interested in a speck of dust on the
banister. That was confirmation enough.
"Just tell him I called to inquire," said Mrs. Meigs.
She made a good retreat. Small as she was, she was capable
of great dignity.
Luckily she had told the taxi to wait. Secure in the
corner, she pulled her veil forward over her face. Not
that any one would see her; but her veil was a shelter
in this bitter moment.
Cal wasn't going to get well. While she had been play-
ing bridge that afternoon, Cal had lain there, sick and
feverish, and alone. In a sense we are all alone when we
die. But if she'd married Cal years ago, as he wanted her
to, he wouldn't be gasping out his last days in that dusty
room up two flights of stairs, with a dim gas-light whis-
tling overhead, and a strange woman-a woman he'd
disliked at first sight-leaning over to feel the pulse flut-
tering in his scrawny wrist.XIV
NATALIE, Cecile, even the threat against her own
independence, had all receded into the background.
Against the sixty years that she had known Cal, even her
own children seemed like strangers.
She called Dr. Stuart's office the first thing in the
morning. He said he was going to see Mr. Fletcher at
once, and would let her know. Mrs. Meigs went out to
the kitchen and started to make an apple pudding. She
couldn't just sit down and wait for news.
In the kitchen Cecile found her. "I'm exercising the
new car, Caroline," she said, carefully offhand. "I'll drive
you over to Cal Fletcher's, if you like."
"I was there yesterday, and the nurse wouldn't let me
see him," said Mrs. Meigs bitterly.
Cecile helped herself to a piece of apple. "It'll be all
right to-day," she said over her shoulder. "I've just been
talking to Dr. Stuart."
Mrs. Meigs' heart gave a leap. Cal was better, then!
They would let her see him. The next instant suspicion
descended again. "Did you call Dr. Stuart?" she de-
manded.
"He called me," answered Cecile. The doctor had
called Cecile, when he had promised to call Mrs. Meigs.
He hadn't wanted to tell her how things stood.
Cecile and Natalie helped her change her dress, got
her wraps on, bundled her into the car. Mrs. Meigs, who
ordinarily hated even to accept an arm over a crossing,
let herself be taken care of like a baby.At the boarding-house Cecile followed her upstairs.
The noise of Cal's breathing filled the room. He lay with
his eyes closed, the bedclothes drawn up to his chin. Only
the choke and rattle of his breath told that he was still
alive.
Mrs. Meigs turned to the nurse. "How long has he
been like this?"
With her lips the nurse shaped the words, "He'll hear
you."
He had heard her. His eyelids fluttered. "Well, Carrie?"
he whispered.
Mrs. Meigs advanced toward the bed. On her way she
had to pass the dresser. In the midst of its unnatural
tidiness stood a prim bouquet, a calla lily surrounded by
carnations in three colors. "Did Dr. Manning send you
those flowers?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
The ghost of a smile passed over Cal's face. "I didn't
know I was as sick as that," he whispered.
"Your flowers are on the window-sill, Mrs. Meigs,"
said the nurse, fighting with a yawn. "They're perfectly
beauti-" The yawn won.
Mrs. Meigs turned on her. "Were you up with him
much in the night?"
"I was up all night, nearly," admitted Mrs. Barclay.
"Then this is your chance to get a nap." Mrs. Meigs
drew a chair up to the bedside. "I'll sit with Cal for a
while."
The nurse looked doubtful. Cecile said, "I'll stay too."
Cal's eyes had closed again. The nurse laid a hand on
his pulse, then spoke to Cecile. "I want to take a bath,
and put on a clean uniform. If you notice any change,
call me at once."
She left them alone with the sick man. Cecile pickedup a book, and sat down at the foot of the bed. At first
she stared over the top of the page. Then she fell to read-
ing, with some show of interest. There was no sound in
the room but the old man fighting for breath.
All at once Mrs. Meigs shivered. With the windows
open that way, the temperature was that of outdoors.
Cecile couldn't have been so absorbed as she pretended.
She came over and laid a hand on her grandmother's.
"You can't stay in here, Caroline," she whispered. "It's
too cold for you."
"Don't make me go away," whispered Mrs. Meigs.
The words sounded queer in her own ears. Why hadn't
she simply said, "I'm not going away"?
"There must be a warm room where you could wait."
Cecile glanced at the figure on the bed. Then she took
off her fur jacket. "Put this on," she commanded. "And
I'll get a rug from the car."
Mrs. Meigs was an incongruous figure in her widow's
bonnet and the smart tan fur jacket that Cecile wore
so jauntily. But when she was tucked up in the rug, the
warmth was grateful. "What are you going to do with-
out your coat?" she demanded.
"I'll run along home, and get another one. I'll come
back for you later in the day. But you can telephone
before that, if you want me," said Cecile.
Mrs. Meigs was glad to be alone. The nurse came and
went. Her presence had ceased to matter. Mrs. Meigs
was conscious of nothing but the pitiful figure on the
bed.
He hardly seemed to be Cal any more. He had sunk
to the primitive level of fighting for every breath. He
had to go on living as long as he could. But why did he
have to? Death is no tragedy to an old man. If he couldonly die, and have it over with! The cruelty of fighting
for every breath! She couldn't leave him now. Time
seemed to stand still for the two of them; yet it was
measured by the smothering gasps that made her own
chest ache in sympathy.
Presently the nurse came with a cup of tea for her.
Mrs. Meigs looked at it, but did not offer to take it. "Per-
haps you'd rather go down to the dining-room and have
something?" suggested Mrs. Barclay. "It's lunch time."
"You go have yours," said Mrs. Meigs.
She was conscious of a terrible thirst, but the tea
choked her. She threw aside the enveloping blanket, and
went to the bathroom for a drink of cold water.
On her way back she encountered Dr. Stuart, in his
big overcoat, thudding along with a doctor's careful
tread. For a moment the sight of him comforted her, just
as she had always been comforted by the arrival of the
old doctor his father, whose sympathy and understand-
ing were worth more any day than his old-fashioned
pharmacopœia.
Then all at once she hated the young doctor, only be-
cause he was young and strong, and wearing his over-
coat, and going about his daily round and because he
had years and years ahead of him. Yet she had no reason
to hate him on his own account; he had always stood by
her.
She waited at the foot of the bed while the doctor bent
over and looked at Cal. He didn't even lay a finger on
the old man's pulse: merely glanced at him, and drew
back.
Mrs. Meigs followed him into the hall. "He's dying,"
she said. It wasn't a question, merely a statement.
Dr. Stuart nodded gravely. "I could keep him alivea few hours longer by the use of oxygen, and maybe
saline injections."
"But he wouldn't get well?"
"No. It would just be keeping that poor old heart
beating a few hours longer."
A few hours! At Dr. Stuart's age a few hours might
seem like nothing. To Cal they were everything. She
felt a surge of wild rebellion at the doctor's callousness:
to assume that a few hours more of life were of no im-
portance, because they were hours in the life of an old
man!
Then she saw that he was watching her. "Shall I keep
him along?" he asked quietly.
But she couldn't give the word. Suddenly she felt faint,
and grasped at the door for support.
Dr. Stuart put an arm around her, and swung her back
to the chair at the bedside. Meeting his steady professional
gaze, Mrs. Meigs felt a fresh anxiety. "You won't send
me away?" she begged. "I'm quite strong and rested,
really. It's just the idea-"
Then she took a fresh hold of herself, and said de-
fiantly, "I won't go if you do send me."
The corners of his mouth twitched. "That's more like
you. You had me worried for a minute, when you
sounded so meek." He opened his medicine-case, and
handed her two small white pills. "Here, take these.
You'd better have the nurse bring you a hot-water bag.
It's a pretty raw day for October."
"You have a pill for everything!" teased Mrs. Meigs.
"Even for anxiety."
He hadn't any pill for old age. But that was an ail-
ment that brought along its own remedy.
He looked at the nurse's chart, gave her a few direc-tions, and left. Mrs. Meigs's hostility to him had vanished.
But when the nurse brought the hot-water bag, she
waved it away. Hot-water bags were for sick people.
Anyhow she'd always hated the "squdgy" feel of them.
The afternoon wore on. Her thoughts wandered a
little. If Tip O'Neil had returned to town and found her
letter, he might call up to-day. She hoped Natalie would
realize the importance of the call, and take the message,
or at least explain where her mother was. Was Natalie
still feeding herself on false hopes that the Churchill per-
son was only waiting for the storm to blow over: that he
intended to come around presently and marry her? Even
if he should, it would be out of the frying-pan into the
fire. "He married the co-respondent!" The very words
set Mrs. Meigs's teeth on edge.
Then it all seemed faint and far away. She had been
sitting here for days, months, years. The afternoon light
grew fainter. Dusk began to gather in. Again the nurse
brought her hot tea. This time she drank it.
On the other side of the room a clock ticked, telling
off the last minutes of Cal's life. Now that she had noticed
it, the sound grew louder and louder. Mrs. Meigs began to
count the ticks.
This wouldn't do. She got up and began to move about
the room. She wondered if Anna had got a nice brown
on that pudding. She probably had. She was a good cook;
and unlike some girls, she didn't resent it when her mis-
tress came into the kitchen and cooked too.
The nurse was tending Cal now. Not a nice life for a
woman, not at all a nice life, fussing over sick people.
Mrs. Meigs had taken care of her children when they were
sick; but your own children were different. She'd taken
care of her husband, too, in his last illness. How he wouldhave hated it, if he'd had to have a strange woman mess-
ing around him!
Cecile came to the bedroom door. She had changed to
a tweed suit, and her cheeks were pink with out-of-doors.
"Not ready yet?" she whispered.
Mrs. Meigs shook her head. Cecile glanced at the bed.
"I'll wait in the car," she decided.
She thought it wouldn't be much longer. The landlady
came to the door, too. She'd miss Cal. He'd lived with her
several years, and she wouldn't find anybody else who'd
put up the way Cal had with everything that was handed
to him. He never had had the nerve to kick.
Cal had been in love with her when they were both in
their teens. It had been years after her own marriage that
he finally got married himself. Mrs. Meigs was glad when
he married; she didn't like to think of any man living
the forlorn life of an old bachelor. His married life hadn't
been much of a success, though. Not that he was actively
unhappy; it was just dreary and dull. But that mattered
very little now. Even Cal was past remembering. In a
few hours more, it would all be as if it had never taken
place at all.
The nurse lighted a gas-jet, and pinned a sheet of paper
so that the light wouldn't shine on Cal. Then she went
off downstairs somewhere, and came back with a screen.
Mrs. Meigs stiffened at the sight of it. She'd heard some-
where that in hospital wards they set up a screen around
the beds of people who were dying.
But Mrs. Barclay arranged the screen to keep the draft
off Cal. It shut off the last of the twilight too. Mrs. Meigs
sat down again at the bedside. The gas-jet hummed and
whistled. A window-shade rattled. Then the silence
seemed to grow deeper and deeper; there was no soundin all the world but Cal's labored breathing. And even
to that she must be getting used. Or else his breath itself
was growing fainter.
All at once Cal opened his eyes. He saw her, and knew
her. Quite forgetting where she was, Mrs. Meigs leaned
closer to him, and smiled.
He smiled back. In the faintest whisper, yet in just
the eager tone he had used in speaking to her all these
years, he asked, "Did you have a good time at your
party, Carrie?”
A surge of gratitude went over her. He had picked up
his life just where he'd dropped it three days ago. He
knew everything, remembered everything. He was go-
ing to fool them all!
Drawing a long breath, she started to answer him.
Then all at once his mouth fell open. The nurse stepped
quickly between Mrs. Meigs and the bed. When she drew
back, Cal's eyes were closed. The harsh breathing that
had been going on ever since Mrs. Meigs could remember
had stopped abruptly. The nurse was watching Mrs.
Meigs.
It was the end she had been waiting for, with a tense-
ness that swallowed her every perception. Cal was dead.
Her name had been the last word on his lips. He had put
off dying just to ask her, "Did you have a good time at
your party?"
It was more than she had any right to expect. He
turned to her, just as he had been turning all these years,
to draw into his own dull life something of the vividness
and gusto of hers.
But as Mrs. Meigs stooped over, and groped under the
bedclothes for his lax hand, she felt a pang of fierce re-
bellion. The idea of Cal going, Cal who had never saidan unkind word in all his eighty years; and there were
men like that Churchill left to cumber the earth!
The nurse must have fetched Cecile. The next thing
Mrs. Meigs knew, a strong young arm was drawing her
away from the bed, and she heard a cool young voice
giving directions and addresses. Dr. Stuart would take
charge of everything; and if they needed outside help,
they could call on Cecile's father, or on her Uncle Jack.
In the car going home, Mrs. Meigs told Cecile what
Cal's last words to her were. Cecile said, "That's beautiful,
darling. That will give you something to remember about
him always." The rest of the way she was silent. She
knew Mrs. Meigs didn't want to be talked to just now,
even by her.
Her own apartment looked strange to her; all the
cheerful commonplaces of her everyday life there seemed
to lie very far behind. Anna and Natalie and Cecile all
hovered about her. She had a hot bath and drank some
soup.
"Do you want to go to bed now?" asked Cecile.
Extraordinary how competent the child had showed
herself to-day. Never obtruding herself, she had still
managed to stay in control of the situation. In the bed-
room she arranged the window, pinned up the curtains,
saw that the thermos bottle was filled. "Should you like
to have me stay here to-night, darling?" she asked.
Mutely Mrs. Meigs shook her head, and reached up to
turn out the light. She didn't want any one with her just
now. Not even Cecile.XV
SHE let them bring her her breakfast in bed. She had
always despised breakfasting in bed. It was a lazy habit.
But the morning after Cal's death, she was too listless
to fight off well-meant attentions.
About the middle of the morning she dressed. But she
couldn't settle to anything. People came and went: Mil-
licent bustling and efficient, Edith sleek and soft-spoken.
Cecile came early in the afternoon, and later Dr. Stuart.
He pretended he was making a social call, but Mrs. Meigs
knew well enough it had been prompted. Well, let him
come if he liked. There was nothing he could do for her.
Cal Fletcher had gone; and with him he had taken a
part of her life. Nobody was left now to call her "Carrie."
She'd always hated the name; but she hated worse the
idea that there was nobody left to use it. She was "Mrs.
Meigs" even to her bridgers. Cecile's calling her "Caro-
line," which she'd always loved because Cecile did it,
was the sort of joke a girl has only with very old people.
There was nobody now to talk over old days with,
nobody who remembered her when she was a girl. Yes,
and who had always thought of her as if she were in a
way still a girl. The sting of being very old is that you
outlive all your generation. Old age doesn't matter, when
it's only a question of failing strength or senses: you can
use a cane and get stronger glasses, or, if worse comes to
worst, get somebody else to read to you. But when youlive along among younger people, you become nothing
but a relic.
Cal's funeral was to be held on Saturday; they had put
if off a day, to accommodate a second cousin who lived
in California. The cousin from California would inherit
Cal's little property. Cal had always shrunk from spend-
ing his principal. That was one of the fundamental
maxims when he was young: Never spend your principal.
He'd had to pinch the last few years, but he'd kept the
precious principal intact, to go to a cousin whom he
hadn't seen in forty years.
Mrs. Bishop came in the second day, and Dr. Manning.
Mrs. Meigs was better pleased than usual to see him. It
struck her that there were a lot of women fussing about
the place. Once or twice she was exasperated into telling
somebody to let her alone. But for the most part, the
fussing seemed to go on at a distance. Between Mrs. Meigs
and her children, even between Mrs. Meigs and Cecile,
there was a cloud that obscured them, and dulled her
perception of what they said.
Cecile helped her dress for the funeral. Mrs. Meigs took
special pains with her appearance. Cal had always thought
her pretty; many was the time she had selected fresh
gloves, or coaxed a wave in her hair, for his benefit. This
was the last time she'd ever dress for him.
The funeral was held in the undertaker's chapel. "The
undertaker's chapel" had always struck Mrs. Meigs as the
last word in desolation. If people had no definite church
connections, they surely ought to be buried from their
homes. But for an old man who lived in a boarding-house,
the undertaker's chapel was the destined end.
Mrs. Meigs's bridge-playing old ladies turned out in
a body for Cal's funeral. All her daughters were there,and Cecile, of course. At that, the chapel wasn't a third
full. There was something pitiful in the smallness of the
funeral.
The cousin from California sat down in front; he was
all there was to represent "the family." Opposite him,
with his hands braced firmly on his umbrella, sat Dr.
Manning.
Mrs. Meigs had never attended a funeral she could
get out of going to. They were simpler than they used
to be, which was something; but they were all idiotically
made to a pattern. A minister, procured by Millicent,
who had served on civic committees with him, talked
about "our departed brother" and "the fulness of his
years." Mrs. Meigs snorted. If somebody had talked about
Cal, gentle, self-effacing, loyal soul that he was, there
might be some point to that.
She sat like a picture of decorum, with her black veil
across her face. But her thoughts began to wander. Mil-
licent had been inquisitive yesterday about Natalie's
prolonged visit to her mother. It was nerve-racking to
have Natalie's affairs hanging fire so. Perhaps Carter
didn't intend to do anything drastic. But he surely
couldn't expect Natalie to ask his forgiveness, when he
had turned her out.
Cecile had taken off her gloves; gloves always bothered
her. Her slim sun-browned hands were clasped in her
lap. Roger had given her a ring with a diamond like a
head-light, but she wasn't wearing it. Still, she had it.
She was drifting along, with her engagement becoming
every day more of a settled fact. Not a word had reached
Mrs. Meigs from Tip. She couldn't go on holding to her
theory of an accident, a mysterious "something" that had
happened to Tip. He had just gone off somewhere tosulk. Never, never had Mrs. Meigs been so disappointed
in anybody.
She had felt a little giddy when she got out of bed
this morning. She'd stayed indoors too much for the last
few days. Dr. Stuart always liked to tease her about walk-
ing down town. It was only ten blocks; nothing at all,
if she took her time.
She'd always walked a great deal. Every so often her
husband used to lecture her about the heels she walked
in; she was a little vain of her feet, and had always been
particular about her shoes. Nowadays, though, they made
pretty shoes with sensible heels. There was really no ex-
cuse for the kind of shoes Cecile usually went around in:
golf or no golf, those flat-heeled Oxfords were an abom-
ination.
The minister finally got through his random remarks,
and the soloist stood up again. The organ played the
opening bars of "Abide With Me." Mrs. Meigs put back
her veil, to get a little more air. Her eye lighted on Dr.
Manning, a row ahead and across the aisle. During the
sermon he had gone to sleep; at the organ music he
nodded deeply, opened his eyes for a brief moment, but
couldn't keep them open.
"How funny he looks!" thought Mrs. Meigs. "I must
be sure and remember to tell Cal about it."
Then it came over her with sickening suddenness, she
couldn't tell Cal. All these years she'd been saving up
funny things to tell Cal. But never again would Cal listen,
amused and a little shocked, while she "took off" her
bridge-players, and Dr. Manning, and the drug-store
man's horror over the books she wanted to draw from
the circulating library.
The chapel swam about her. The funeral was over.It was time for everybody to leave. Millicent rose, and
Edith. Mrs. Meigs sat rooted in her seat. She saw Cecile
look at her anxiously, and wished she hadn't put back
her veil. But she lacked the strength to reach up and
struggle with it.
Dr. Manning was awake now. He stepped into the aisle
and looked uncertainly about him. He wasn't quite sure
what came next.
To her own surprise, Mrs. Meigs found herself beckon-
ing to him. "Dr. Manning," she said, "won't you ride in
the car with me? We are Cal's two oldest friends, and it
seems to me that we ought to ride out to the cemetery to-
gether."
After that everything went so rapidly the next thing
she knew, she was back in her own apartment and Anna
was making a cheese soufflé for lunch, and warning
Natalie that it mustn't be kept waiting. After the morn-
ing's agitation, Mrs. Meigs was both delighted and an-
noyed to find herself at home again, and things going
on pretty much as usual.
As soon as lunch was over, she began to clear out a
drawer in her chiffonier. When she was in the mood for
it, she loved having something to straighten. It had oc-
curred to her that she might find some silk pieces which
would give Mrs. Bishop the start on a new quilt. Or at
least the start of a new collection of silk pieces-Mrs.
Meigs didn't believe Mrs. Bishop would ever get around
actually to start the quilt.
But she left the drawer with half its contents scattered
on the floor, and began to wander about the apartment.
She tried to read. She even tried solitaire, though she had
always considered solitaire the last refuge of an enfeebled
mind. But she couldn't settle to anything.Her restlessness was succeeded by a numbing lassitude.
After all, why should she bother to keep busy? All her
activities were either pure time-killing, or an interest in
other, younger lives. Anyhow they must soon cease al-
together. What was the good of pretending to go on, try-
ing to keep up some show of zest, laughing when people
teased her about being "the youngest of the party"? In
the end it all came to nothing.
You might keep on, at a cost nobody knew but your-
self-keep your flag flying, keep up your pitiable pre-
tense that the tag end of life is worth having. But some-
thing would show you up, sooner or later. Something had
showed her up. Dr. Stuart had said that nothing was the
matter with her. Nothing was, except that the main-
spring was running down.
That night there was a killing frost. Well she remem-
bered, in the days when she owned a yard and flower beds,
how she had hated that first frost, and the awakening
to blackened flowers and withered leaves. It always made
her feel that this time summer had gone away, never to
return. Yet before she was really settled for the winter,
the seed catalogues began to come.
Well, killing frosts didn't concern her now. She hadn't
a growing thing to her name, except the potted chrys-
anthemums and rose-geraniums that filled the windows
in the living-room. Nobody kept the windows full of
plants nowadays except old ladies. If you asked her, there
was no reason why old ladies should do it.
Sunday morning her telephone rang time after time.
Cecile suggested a ride. Then Ralph and next Edith each
with the same idea. "Don't you ever talk to each other
in your family?" asked Mrs. Meigs. In the afternoon
Jack appeared, ready to take her."I've a good mind," said Mrs. Meigs, "to buy a car
of my own. Then I'll have some chance of spending a
peaceful Sunday afternoon at home."
But she went with Jack. It wasn't so easy to get rid
of some one who didn't telephone. He tucked her up in
a rug, and Estelle politely sat in the back seat with her.
They took the usual Sunday afternoon ride: went to see
how the new Civic Auditorium was getting on, and then
drove out to admire the way the suburbs were building
up. Unexpectedly Mrs. Meigs found herself interested in
the suburban building.
"Houses are all pint size nowadays," she remarked.
"In my time they were gallons. But I always did like
the idea of a house. In an apartment, you're never really
by yourself."
"Do you like to be by yourself?" asked Estelle.
"By myself, but next door to somebody I care for,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "Drive slower, Jack. These bungalows
are nice, aren't they? I suppose anybody could get a fur-
nace boy. There'd be two bedrooms on the ground floor,
one for me and one for Anna. These are for sale, too,
aren't they?"
"But Mother, you're going to Mill's to live," said Jack.
"She's counting on it."
Mrs. Meigs began to argue, "I said I'd go if Cecile mar-
ried Roger Kane. She hasn't married him yet."
"Oh, but it's all settled!" said Estelle. "Didn't you see
their engagement was announced in the paper this morn-
ing?"
"I don't get a Sunday paper," said Mrs. Meigs. "The
daily papers are bad enough, but they do give you some
news. The Sunday paper is filled with nothing but rub-
bish.""I have that part of the paper here somewhere," said
Estelle.
She rummaged in a pocket on the car door. First she
brought out the news section. Holding it at arm's length,
Mrs. Meigs glanced casually at one or two columns. There
was a flood in Colorado, she noticed. A flood in the desert!
Nowadays things were always getting out of place. Mrs.
Meigs had laughed at Mrs. Bishop's habit of saying that
"nowadays" things weren't what they used to be. But
there was something in the idea.
She remembered the way Cal Fletcher had always
scraped and saved to pay the taxes on his Western land.
Well, the California cousin could pay 'em now. Mrs.
Meigs wished him joy of them. Where else had she heard
lately about somebody who was building great hopes on
the possession of Western land? Oh, yes! Tip O'Neil. At
the thought of Tip O'Neil Mrs. Meigs's lips set tight.
She dropped the paper as if it burned her.
Estelle was carefully folding the society section to
show a picture. Cecile in evening dress, with a formal
announcement of her engagement, and a little paragraph
of palaver about Roger's business interests and his clubs,
and Cecile's popularity as "one of the best-liked members
of the younger set."
"Didn't her picture come out well?" asked Estelle.
It did. Even in the newspaper reproduction Cecile's
eyes were wistful. But Millicent couldn't have had this
news printed without Cecile's full consent. Mrs. Meigs
stared at the picture until it dimmed before her eyes.
Well, among them they had done it. Cecile was going
to marry her good catch, and Mrs. Meigs was going to
Millicent's to live. It wasn't merely her hasty promise
that was taking her; her promise she could have explainedaway. But the mainspring of her resolution was broken.
After all, it didn't make much difference now what
her arrangements were. She wouldn't have long to en-
dure them. She was an old woman. Two weeks ago she
hadn't been old. The sight of her own white hair and
wrinkled cheeks in a mirror could still give her a sur-
prise, because she didn't feel any older.
Old age doesn't come steadily, a year at a time. At
seventy-nine, she had still held her years a joke. Only last
week she had said to Millicent, "Not too many happy
returns of the day." Yet she had actually expected to
keep on having birthdays as long as she cared to have
them.
Old age begins the day you cease to impinge hopefully
on the future. The future had crumbled in her hands.
"I wouldn't have to stay at Millicent's all the time," she
said aloud.
"Of course not," Jack assured her. "But you may as
well go there first. She's had the plumber make an esti-
mate on a new bathroom for you. It's to be on the third
floor, right next to your bedroom."
"Really!" said Estelle. "Jack, can't we find a bunga-
low that's finished? Maybe it will give your mother some
ideas on her new bathroom."
"Surest thing you know," said Jack heartily.
He selected a handsome bungalow, on a corner. Be-
cause this was Sunday afternoon, an agent was on duty
to show it. Jack beckoned to him, and gave Mrs. Meigs
an arm as she stepped from the car.
They went in through the back door. In the kitchen
Jack straddled a saw-horse and lighted a cigarette. "Go
on," he said moodily. "I'll wait for you here."
"What's the matter?" asked Mrs. Meigs."Oh, nothing!" said Jack.
Estelle threw an amused glance at him. "Every once in
a while Jack reverts to type," she said. "He pictures
himself a householder, with a tool bench in the basement,
and his fingers itch to go out and solder something."
"He had a tool bench in the basement when he was a
boy," said Mrs. Meigs. "Jack is awfully handy about
tinkering."
"It's not that so much," said Jack. "But I'm forty,
which means that next year I shall be forty-one. Every
year we pay out, just for a bedroom and bath, more than
a couple like us could have lived on in your day, Mother.
And that isn't taking into account that our meals and
laundry are all at hotel prices."
"We're only asking a thousand-dollar down payment
on this bungalow," said the agent brightly.
"I haven't the thousand," snapped Jack.
"A hotel is the modern way to live," said Estelle. "It's
all very well for a man to say he prefers a home of his
own. But it's the woman who has to take the responsi-
bility, even if she can afford to hire competent help. I
suppose Roger and Cecile will start housekeeping with
at least two servants. Money certainly makes things
easy."
Mrs. Meigs led the way toward the front of the house.
The dining-room had built-in cupboards, and the living-
room built-in bookcases. "These modern bungalows
don't take much furniture," said Estelle. "What a pretty
fireplace! I love an open fire on winter evenings."
The agent stooped to show how a slit in the bottom of
the fireplace allowed the ashes to be brushed directly
into a bin in the basement. "The housework in a place
like this does itself," he insinuated.Mrs. Meigs investigated the casement windows. "I've
always liked these windows that open like a door."
"They're complete even to the rods," said the agent.
"Fine net curtains, gathered both top and bottom!"
hummed Estelle. "This place could be made awfully
pretty."
"Are the bedrooms downstairs?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"I've always liked the idea of going upstairs to bed. It
seems more respectable, somehow."
"They open off a separate hall. That insures privacy,"
the agent assured her.
Estelle inspected the bedrooms. There were two of
them, with a bath between. "What a duck of a bath-
room!" said Estelle. "It does give you ideas, doesn't it?"
"Estelle," said Mrs. Meigs, "I believe you're more do-
mestic than you let on to be."
"Not a bit!" said Estelle. "But I am sick of being
cooped up in one room. When Jack leaves after break-
fast, and I think of a whole day ahead of me, sometimes
I could just shriek."
"Do you shriek?"
"I shriek about an hour later, when I get dragged into
a bridge game."
"Not in the morning, surely?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Well, yes. I often play even in the morning.”
Mrs. Meigs wasn't such an old fogy that she went
around declaring a woman shouldn't get married un-
less she wanted children. But she did believe a woman
shouldn't get married unless she wanted to be married.
The idea of a lot of so-called wives who sat down at the
bridge table the first thing after breakfast was too much
for her. She marched to the kitchen door.
"Jack," she called, "come here!"Jack came. His mother looked him up and down. "A
married woman is entitled to a home," she said coldly.
"You haven't provided Estelle with one. And the worst
of it is, you've tried to throw the blame on her."
"I never-" began Estelle. Mrs. Meigs nudged her
sharply.
"We live in a hotel to make it easy for her," protested
Jack, reddening.
"But she's tired of having it so easy. She wants a little
place of her own," said Mrs. Meigs. "You do, Estelle.
You may not know it yourself, but you do."
"I'm not sure I should know how to run a house,"
faltered Estelle, aghast at her own daring. "But I would
like to try it, for a change. It seems as if I could be happy
hanging my own curtains, and telephoning my own or-
ders to the butcher and the grocer-"
Jack took fire too. "And I could cook the chops when
I got home from the office. It would be great to have
meals where everything didn't taste as if it had been sit-
ting around for a week."
"We could always go out to dinner if we wanted to,"
chimed in Estelle. "But it would be nice sometimes to
fry an egg and listen to the rain on the roof.”
"We'd get all these new electrical devices. Ralph would
give us a discount. And you could have a woman come
in mornings to do the heavy work," said Jack.
"It would be like playing house." Estelle was rosy
with excitement, under her mask of orange powder. "Oh,
Jack, couldn't we at least try it?"
Jack's face fell. "I don't know where the money is to
come from. It's taken nearly every cent I make, just to
run us.""How much have you got in the bank?" demanded
Mrs. Meigs.
Jack and Estelle both looked startled. They had for-
gotten her presence. But Jack recognized her right to
the question. "About seven hundred. But there are al-
ways the bills to take care of."
"I can let you have three hundred," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I'll take your note for it, but you needn't pay me any
interest."
"We can talk it over to-morrow, perhaps," agreed
Jack.
"To-morrow the bungalow may be sold to some one
else,” said Mrs. Meigs. What she meant was, "By to-
morrow you will both have had a chance to cool off."
She turned to the agent. "Will fifty dollars bind the
bargain until to-morrow?"
"I haven't got fifty," said Jack.
"Jack Meigs," said his mother, "will you stop telling
everybody what you haven't got?" She turned her back,
and felt for the familiar lump in her stocking. Her roll
yielded fifty dollars for the agent, and left five for her-
self.
The agent, grinning, leaned over to draw up a mem-
orandum of agreement on the sink. Estelle watched him
with a smile of content. "An hour ago I hadn't an idea
of moving into a house of my own," she said. "Now I feel
as if it were the one thing I had been looking forward to
all my life."
Jack's chest swelled. He thought he had done it all, of
course. Best to let him think so. But Mrs. Meigs wanted
to put in one word for herself.
"I'm glad if I'm helping you get it," she said. "Butdon't you think, Jack, it's a little unfair that while I'm
helping you to a home of your own, you should be doing
everything you can to turn me out of mine?"
Jack's face grew red. "Mother, I didn't ask you for
any money."
"Hush, Jack!" said Estelle. "That isn't what she
means." She turned to Mrs. Meigs with a smile of con-
scious kindness. "We're taking this place partly on your
account. We hope you will come and stay with us many,
many times."
"That's right," said Jack. "Come whenever you're
tired of Mill's."
For a minute, Mrs. Meigs would have liked to bump
their two stupid heads together. Being kind to the old
lady! As if she had ever asked for their kindness. As if
she asked anybody, any time, for anything except just
to let her alone!
Since Cal's funeral, her life had been like a bad dream.
But she seemed to have lost the power of waking up. She
knew exactly what she ought to say to these two wretches.
But she didn't say it. She allowed herself to be stowed
away in the car, and her feet tucked up, the way she
hated.
She was amazed at herself for allowing it. She had
lived through other crises that were so much worse, and
emerged triumphant. But now the strength had gone
out of her. She was an old woman, and she no longer
cared even to deny it. She was licked at last.XVI
THE better part of a week went by, and her apathy
deepened. Her apartment seethed with her children. They
could hardly fail to be self-assertive, being her children.
But looking at them these days, Mrs. Meigs wondered
who had ever started the superstition that "the children"
were the prop and comfort of anybody's old age. It wasn't
that she wanted them to be "grateful" to her. Only the
silliest sort of old woman expected her children to spend
forty or fifty years in gratitude to Mother. But it did
seem that they might have gone their own way, and let
her go hers.
Estelle came over to show her samples of curtain ma-
terial, and to use her sewing-machine. Estelle was as ex-
cited as a bride over setting up the establishment she
should have had twenty years ago. Jack came, to explain
that he had borrowed the three hundred dollars from
some one else, and to return her fifty. Edith came, much
excited over the plans for Cecile's wedding. The date
hadn't been definitely set, but Edith thought Cecile would
agree to the first week in December. For the wedding,
Edith urged her mother to lay aside her mourning, and
wear a soft silk dress of delicate dove gray. Edith loved
to stage everything; she pointed out that Mrs. Meigs,
dressed for the part, and ensconced in a front pew at the
church, and in a special place at the reception, would
attract almost as much attention as the bride herself."It's supposed to be Cecile's wedding, not mine," said
Mrs. Meigs.
"Now, Mother, you know how devoted Cecile is to
you," protested Edith.
The alliance between Cecile and Mrs. Meigs had al
ways been a byword in the family. But these days Cecile
seldom came to see her grandmother; and she never came
alone. Of course she was very busy just now. But nights
when Mrs. Meigs took her book to bed with her, she
couldn't help listening for the long ring at the door-bell,
and Cecile's hail, "Oh, Caroline! May I come up?" The
hail never came.
Mrs. Meigs read late every night now; she was aware
that the privilege would soon be taken from her. But
however late it was when she turned out her light, sleep
was slow in coming. Sometimes during the day she nodded
in her chair. Natalie watched her anxiously.
"Mother's breaking up. I never saw any one fail
so suddenly," Mrs. Meigs overheard Natalie say to
Edith.
Poor Natalie was sweet to her. Edith was sweet too,
in spite of her idiotic scheme for dressing her mother
up like a doll and making her Exhibit A at Cecile's
wedding. But neither of them could stand between Mrs.
Meigs and the sweeping energy of Millicent.
Millicent came every day now, not only to talk over
plans, but to hustle and bustle, and paw over Mrs. Meigs's
possessions. "We must weed out with a firm hand," she
would announce, opening drawers and viewing their con-
tents with disapproval. "You haven't carried that hand-
bag in years, Mother. And what are you saving those
gloves for? Every finger through. Something ought to
be done with these napkins. Nobody uses these hugedamask napkins any more, even for dinner parties. The
laundry work is such a consideration."
"I'll go through things myself," protested Mrs. Meigs,
"if you'll give me time."
"But, Mother, don't you realize that there isn't that
much time? Here Cecile is to be married in a little more
than a month, and I want to get you all moved and set-
tled before I have to make the final arrangements for her
wedding."
"Let it go till after Cecile's wedding. You know how
long plumbers take," said Mrs. Meigs with a ray of hope.
"It would be just like them to start the job, and land
you with a wedding reception on your hands, and the
new bathroom for me only half in."
"Oh, we're postponing the bathroom! The expense
would be pretty heavy, and it will take all we can rake
and scrape to get Cecile married properly. I want her
wedding to be a credit to us,” said Millicent.
She had renigged on the new bathroom; Mrs. Meigs
could use the servants'. Mrs. Meigs could have the new
bathroom put in at her own expense; but she wasn't go-
ing to. It had been a part of Millicent's original offer.
She would have been a lot freer from interference
at Edith's; but the modernistic horrors of Edith's guest-
room would have kept her awake nights. In the third-
floor room at Millicent's, she could at least have her own
things. That is, she could if Millicent left her anything
to move. Millicent's motto these days was, "We'll put
that out for the janitor."
Once Natalie ventured a protest. "You ought to re-
member, Millicent, old people cling to their old things.
What looks like trash to you may have associations for
Mother.""Help me with this table, please," was Millicent's an-
swer. "It's a clumsy thing. Most of this furniture should
have been disposed of when the old house was sold. It
isn't old enough to be antique. It's just out of date."
"It's awfully solid and well built," panted Natalie,
straining her wrists to move the table.
"Mother might give some of it to you, if you like it
so much," suggested Millicent.
Natalie looked frightened. She didn't know how much
Millicent guessed of her domestic difficulties. She might
be driven presently to take refuge with one of her sis-
ters. Charles Churchill had left for Florida. She'd writ-
ten him two letters, which might have been forwarded,
but were never answered. Once she had gone so far as to
write Carter; but that letter she was afraid to send.
Millicent's "weeding out" improved the looks of the
apartment, if Mrs. Meigs could have got used to it. But
it seemed like the beginning of the end, the day two
stout barrels and a quantity of excelsior were delivered.
"It takes so long to pack dishes," Millicent explained
brightly.
"Don't you touch my best china," warned Mrs. Meigs.
"You've always promised that set to Cecile for a wed-
ding present," hinted Millicent.
That was true. Mrs. Meigs hated the idea of Roger
Kane eating off her wedding dishes. Still, a promise was
a promise. "I'll pack it for Cecile," said Mrs. Meigs
quietly. "But why the hurry? She hasn't yet made up her
mind where she's going to live."
"Oh, the dishes can be stored, if necessary!" Having
carried her point, Millicent went on graciously, "You
can bring that old clock you're so fond of with you, if
you like. To keep in your own room, I mean of course.""Nobody needs a clock under your roof," said Mrs.
Meigs enigmatically. "I think I'll give mine to Estelle."
The next afternoon she set to work to pack the sacred
dishes. Each piece must be wrapped in paper, and the
piles bedded firmly in excelsior. It would be a long job;
and the wrapping of each piece seemed to Mrs. Meigs
like a little chip off her independence.
She had finished the dinner plates, and placed them
one by one at the bottom of the barrel, when she heard
a cry from the living-room. She went to see what was
the matter.
The afternoon mail had just come. Natalie was stand-
ing with an open letter in her hand. In stricken silence
she passed it to her mother. It consisted of three type-
written lines. Carter Brooks was instructing his lawyer
to bring suit for divorce, and Natalie would hear from
him.
For a moment Mrs. Meigs felt guilty. She should have
kept her home, if only as a refuge for Natalie. But Mrs.
Meigs was no longer the head of the family. She was a
protected, beloved, but useless appendage. With a sooth-
ing word or two she handed back the letter. Then she
returned to her packing.
A few minutes later she paid the price of her immunity.
Millicent came steaming in and discovered Mrs. Meigs
packing dishes. She began at once, "Mother, you mustn't
stoop over that barrel. The blood all goes to your head
when you stoop. You may have a stroke. And you
mustn't climb on a chair to get those dishes down. Don't
you realize you might fall and break your hip? At your
age such an accident would probably be fatal. It would
certainly cripple you. The bones would never knit."
Without ever taking off her gloves Millicent couldgive the effect of rolling up her sleeves. This time she
actually rolled them up. Briskly if a trifle heavily-she
was a large woman, and hadn't inherited her mother's
dainty feet-she mounted the chair in Mrs. Meigs's
stead.
"You know, Millicent, I never allow anybody but
me to touch that set of dishes," Mrs. Meigs warned.
"Nonsense, Mother!" snapped Millicent. "You mustn't
encourage yourself in the idea that there are a whole
lot of things nobody but yourself can do. That sort of
delusion is childish."
As she said "childish" she dropped the soup tureen. The
cover broke in two, and the bottom in twenty. Anna,
hearing the crash, came to the kitchen door. Seeing Mil-
licent's plight, she grinned from ear to ear. "Mis'
Meigs wouldn't ever let me touch them dishes," she an-
nounced.
The telephone rang. "Anna, go see who that is,” com-
manded Mrs. Meigs.
Millicent was scarlet with fury. "Really, Mother, the
way you encourage that girl in her impertinence-! I
had thought of taking her myself when she gets through
here; she's a better cook than mine, and I thought that
with the exercise of a little firmness, I might make some-
thing of her. But I can see it's hopeless."
"You used to have that trick even when you were a
little girl, Millicent," said Mrs. Meigs. "If you felt your-
self hopelessly in the wrong, you always changed the
subject."
"I'm sure I'm sorry I broke the tureen," said Millicent.
"But no one uses soup tureens any more."
"I suppose you did it on purpose," said Mrs. Meigs."I suppose breaking tureens is a part of your famous
weeding out."
Anna came to tell her she was wanted on the telephone.
"Sweep up these pieces, Anna," directed Mrs. Meigs. "I'll
be back in a minute."
Over the telephone came a voice she had hoped to hear
some time ago, and had pretty well given up expecting
to hear. "Mrs. Meigs, I've just found a letter from you,
asking me to call you up."
"Why, Tip O'Neil!" gasped Mrs. Meigs. "You have
been away, then! When did you get back?"
"Just now." Tip's voice sounded weary and dispirited,
quite unlike himself.
"I wish you had come back sooner."
"I hadn't any choice in the matter. I guess it wouldn't
have made much difference anyhow."
"Maybe not. Just the same I'd like a little talk with
you. Can't you come to see me?"
"Yes, I can," said Tip uncertainly.
But as she started to say, "Can't you come right over
now?" a clatter from the dining-room warned her that
she'd better not. "Hold on a minute!" she said. "Where
are you now?"
"I'm at home."
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "I'm afraid it will compromise
you, if I'm seen going to your flat too often. Just the
same, I think I'd better come and see you."
"Oh, all right!" agreed Tip. In a brisker tone he added,
"Yes, that will be better. When can you come?"
"Now," said Mrs. Meigs. "As fast as a taxi will
bring me."
In her bonnet and coat, she went as far as the dining-room door. "You can let those dishes go until I get back,
Millicent," she announced.
Millicent, towering upon the chair, demanded, "Why,
where are you going?"
"Out," said Mrs. Meigs.
The clash with Millicent had stimulated her. But in
the taxi her spirits sank again. The way Tip had turned
up missing when he could least be spared was more than
coincidence, she was afraid. Tip was that sort of person.
Undoubtedly he'd been missing all his life just when he
was needed.
Still, it was only fair that she should listen to what
he had to say for himself. Catching him badly in the
wrong might reconcile Mrs. Meigs to Roger. That is, it
might almost reconcile her.
She walked up Tip's stairs, and at her knock he opened
the door. He was still dusty from a long train journey,
and he looked tired, dispirited, almost haggard. But at
the sight of her he showed his old wrinkling smile. "I
think I'm the only man you've ever run after like this,"
he said. "But likely you've made 'em all think that."
After the dimness of the hall, the sudden shift to day-
light again made Mrs. Meigs blink. As she grew accus-
tomed to the brightness of the apartment she blinked
again, not believing her eyes. From the davenport near
the windows a young woman rose and confronted her.
Silhouetted against the light she was slender and lovely;
and even at the moment of interruption her eyes turned,
not to the newcomer, but appealingly to Tip.
So this was the explanation! Tip had been off some-
where getting himself married! A nice escape Cecile had
had. No wonder he hadn't given any explanation
his absence."Mrs. Meigs, this is Mrs. O'Neil," said Tip. "Won't
you call her Molly, though? 'Twould make her feel more
at home."
"I hope she will get to feeling at home very soon," said
Mrs. Meigs stiffly.
"Molly, you'll love Mrs. Meigs," said Tip gently. "She's
the grandest old lady in America. Let me take your coat,
Mrs. Meigs."
"No, thank you," said Mrs. Meigs. "I can't stay long."
Tip moved forward a chair for her. Ignoring it, Mrs.
Meigs sailed toward the davenport, which stood facing
the windows, its back to her. She was half-way there
when from the seat of the davenport came an unmistak-
able cry.
"He's waking," said Molly.
Mrs. Meigs covered the remaining space to the daven-
port with a speed that would have done credit to a
person half her years. There lay a baby about three
months old, roused from a nap, and setting himself to
cry. All her life Mrs. Meigs had known only one thing
to do when a baby cried. She picked him up.
The feeling of him cuddled to her shoulder, with his
warm downy head against her cheek, set a current of new
life running through Mrs. Meigs. "You haven't forgot-
ten how to hold a baby, I see," said Tip with a grin.
She hadn't. And she didn't lay him down when she
turned to ask, "Tip O'Neil, whose baby is this?"
Tip looked hard at her. Then as her suspicions dawned
on him, he began to laugh. Instead of answering her
question, he sat down on the arm of the davenport and
had his laugh out.
"It's Molly's baby," he said between guffaws. "And it's
my second cousin, if you want to know. Now tell mewhat it was you were thinking. But you better whisper it
in my ear. It might not be fit hearing for Molly."
Molly smiled faintly. Now that she had turned so the
strong light struck her face, Mrs. Meigs saw that it was
pale and drained, and the blue eyes were swollen. It was
the face of a woman who had cried until she could cry
no more, in the tragic grief of youth. Worn as she was,
she looked to be scarcely twenty.
Mrs. Meigs groped in her memory. "Is this the cousin
you were telling me about?" she asked. "The cousin in
the West?"
"It is," said Tip shortly. He glanced at Molly, as if
he didn't like to go on.
The baby lifted his head from Mrs. Meigs's shoulder,
and went back to his crying. "Is he hungry?" asked Mrs.
Meigs.
"There you called him 'he' right off! I can't remem-
ber not to say 'it.' " Tip turned to Molly. "Take him, dar-
ling. You needn't mind Mrs. Meigs. She's had children
of her own; and I'd be willing to bet something they
weren't brought up on the bottle."
"Tip, that tongue of yours will get you into trouble
yet," warned Mrs. Meigs.
Tip's tongue was silent while he watched Molly settle
herself with the baby's head against her round white
breast. Mrs. Meigs liked the look of him at that minute.
She drew him to the other end of the room. "Tip, did
something happen there in the West?"
Tip's face stiffened. Looking to make sure Molly was
not listening, he whispered, "There was some dreadful de-
fect in the new government dam. When they started to
use it, it broke, and flooded all the country below. The
engineer committed suicide.""Oh, that must have been the flood I read about in the
newspaper! Was that girl's husband-?"
"He lived there to look after my property for me. He
was caught below the dam. They found his body the next
morning-drowned."
Her head whirled as she tried to get the full import
of the disaster. "Then your Western land, that you were
banking on, won't have the water it needs?"
"It's gone back to desert. To desert and worse, to
waste and wreckage. But can I even think of that,"
asked Tip, "when I remember Dennis snatched from life
-and he had everything to live for?"
"I didn't know Dennis," said Mrs. Meigs. "I'm think-
ing about you."
"I've got Molly and her baby to look out for now, if
that's what you mean," said Tip. "There was no good in
her staying out there, of course. So I brought her home
with me."
"You can't keep her here," said Mrs. Meigs.
"I've just the one room, of course," said Tip. "But
I'll go somewhere else for the night, and let her get a
good long sleep. What I'll do tomorrow" He shrugged
doubtfully.
Mrs. Meigs felt for the lump in her stocking. Then she
checked herself. There were times when you couldn't
offer money. Shelter for the girl and her baby: that was
what she could have offered, if she'd had a proper home
of her own. But to adopt a penniless young widow and
a three-months-old baby now would be the last proof
her family needed that Mother couldn't be trusted any
longer to look out for herself.
"Tip," she said slowly, "this is a complete upheaval,
and I don't know yet what to advise. But if you'll giveme a little time, I'm sure I can think of something."
"That's mighty sweet of you," said Tip. "But it's my
responsibility. After all, my looking out for Molly won't
be any more than people did as a matter of course in
my grandfather's day. But I don't need to tell you that.
I guess we've got soft nowadays. I've been feeling aw-
fully sorry for myself lately. You make me ashamed
of it."
Mrs. Meigs's face flushed. Tip was giving her credit
that wasn't due her. Letting down the way she had
lately, she'd quite forgotten that in other people's eyes
she might be a symbol of stanchness.
"You'd better have some dinner sent in here," she
said. "I'll telephone you this evening."
She smiled at Molly across the room, and moved toward
the door. Tip opened it for her before he asked, in an off-
hand way, "How's Cecile?"
"Asking how Cecile is, as you show me out the door,"
said Mrs. Meigs, "is as bad as sending your love to some-
body in a postscript."
It was half past four when she left him. Heavy clouds
obscured the remnant of daylight, and a November wind
whipped her veil about. Mrs. Meigs started to signal a
taxi. Then she paused, not certain whether she wanted
to go home just yet.
Tip's complication was the last straw in an over-
weighted situation. Mrs. Meigs's home was gone. Carter
was divorcing Natalie. Cecile was marrying Roger Kane.
Worst of all, things weren't quite as they had been be-
tween Cecile and her grandmother. They never could be
again, never quite the same.
It was more than any one person could handle: alto-
gether too much for a little old lady of eighty, so frailin her widow's weeds that a passing policeman was
pausing this very minute to be sure the rising wind
wouldn't blow her into the gutter.
Mrs. Meigs didn't know what she was going to do
about a single one of her difficulties. But under the stimu-
lus of Tip's admiration, and her own shame at not de-
serving it, she felt the old core of resolution harden
within her.
She didn't know what she was going to do. But she
knew she was going to do something.XVII
CARTER BROOKS was an unimportant man in a
fairly important office. Mrs. Meigs knew the name of the
firm he worked for, but she had to look up the address
in the telephone book.
The girl at the switchboard took her name and re-
layed it in. On a stiff chair outside the railing that di-
vided the reception-room, Mrs. Meigs sat watching the
inner doors, and wondering whether Carter would look
very different in his business surroundings. Men some-
times did. Millicent's Ralph was much more authorita-
tive in "the store" than he ever dared to be at home.
Carter's face was a little too narrow, and his shoulders
drooped. Aside from that, he was good-looking enough.
He seemed surprised when he saw Mrs. Meigs. "I didn't
get your name over the telephone," he explained.
"Nobody ever can get it, and nobody can spell it,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "If I'd known half the trouble it was
going to make me, I'd have had my husband change his
name to mine instead."
The girl at the switchboard laughed. It's nice to have
your sallies appreciated. Still, it showed Mrs. Meigs that
she and Carter couldn't talk here. She wasn't sure that
he had a private office. "Carter," she asked, "how do you
feel about taking a lady out to tea?"
"What lady? Oh!" He smiled at his own stupidity.
"That's a good idea, if you're the lady. I suppose it
wouldn't take more than half an hour?""I don't want to be a nuisance. You're sure you can
get away all right?"
"Oh, yes! I've worked several evenings lately. I ought
to have a little time coming to me." He was starting for
his hat when a sudden suspicion checked him. "Are you
-alone?"
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "Not only alone. Nobody knows
where I am. Did you ever stop to think what fun it is
to be off somewhere and not have a soul in the world
know where you are?"
"That very thing has happened to me a good many
times lately," said Carter. "I haven't found it such fun."
When they reached the street, he suggested Rispigliosi's
restaurant for tea. "Too expensive," Mrs. Meigs decided.
"Isn't there some little place right around here?"
"There's Peggy's Tea Room. I have lunch at Peggy's
every day. In spite of the name, though, I think they'll
faint if we go in there and ask them for afternoon
tea."
"Then we'll ask for coffee," said Mrs. Meigs. "I like
the custom of afternoon tea, but for the tea itself I don't
care especially."
"I like coffee better myself," said Carter.
A single waitress was on duty in Peggy's Tea Room;
one belated luncher was leaving just as they entered.
They established themselves at a corner table. "Coffee,
toast, and marmalade," ordered Mrs. Meigs.
"Same for me," said Carter. "And better bring a big
pot of coffee."
Mrs. Meigs poured the coffee, and did the honors as if
it had been her own table. Carter began to relax. Mrs.
Meigs eyed him shrewdly. A shy, proud, obstinate man,
who would draw into himself at a hint of trouble. He'dbeen working evenings lately, he admitted; probably he
hadn't been speaking to a soul for days, except in the
way of business.
"Put some extra butter on your toast," she urged. "You
can stand it. This marmalade is good, isn't it? Tastes like
home-made."
ter.
"You still keep your interest in food," remarked Car-
"I've always believed in having good meals, and then
in eating them," said Mrs. Meigs. "I suppose that shows
I'm an old fogy. All the younger people nowadays are
dieting. They're afraid they're going to get fat, or their
doctor has warned 'em not to eat meat, or something."
"It's a damper on sociability," agreed Carter. "You
can't get up much steam when everybody is sitting in
a corner by himself nibbling a graham cracker."
"That sounds like a party I went to just the other
day," said Mrs. Meigs. She went on to tell him about
Grace Whitney's bridge for Mrs. Bishop. It wasn't man-
ners to accept hospitality and then make fun of the
food; but the things she could talk about to Carter were
limited in number.
Carter laughed, and then looked at her wonderingly.
"Do you always make a funny story out of everything,
Mrs. Meigs?"
"So many funny things always happen to me," ex-
plained Mrs. Meigs. "Of course when I go out to tea
with a man, I put my best foot forward, and tell all my
funny stories. Men are easier to talk to anyhow."
"For a woman, maybe they are. Women are easier
for a man. Why, I remember the evening I came to ask
you and Mr. Meigs about marrying Natalie" Hestopped abruptly, and flushed. He hadn't wanted to be
reminded of that faraway evening.
"My husband went to bed early, and left me to deal
with you, didn't he?" Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "That was
a special case, though, you must remember. Fathers never
feel very friendly toward the young men who want to
marry their daughters. They always seem to have an
idea the girls are being kidnapped."
"Haven't mothers the same idea?"
"No, they haven't. At least, I never had. Of course
I was sorry to see my girls leave me. But in their court-
ships I lived my own over. It hurt my husband, too, to
realize that each of his girls in her turn looked on her
young man as a rescuer. He had loved making a good
home for his children; and it hurt his feelings to see his
girls act as if they were fairy princesses shut up in a tower,
and as if a perfectly ordinary young man were a knight
in armor who came to carry them off."
Carter struggled with the idea. "But do girls feel that
way-all of them?"
"Of course they do. I know I did. And I was an only
child, and terribly spoiled at home."
"Then if all girls do feel that way, it must be a blow
when they find out that marriage is just well, just be-
ing married."
"I'm a little afraid," said Mrs. Meigs gently, "some of
them start in then to wait for a second rescuer."
Carter flushed. He had the human need to justify
himself, but perhaps he didn't want to hurt an old lady's
