feelings. He said in a low tone, "I don't know how much
Natalie has told you."
Mrs. Meigs forced herself to meet his eye. "She hastold me everything. I'm awfully ashamed. You read
about such things in the newspapers; but when they
come right into your own family, it can't help being a
blow."
"I'm within my rights," said Carter slowly.
"When people begin to talk about their rights, they
want to justify their anger. Of course you are angry,
Carter."
"I was furious at first," admitted Carter. "I wanted
to get back at her. If they'd come to me decently, and
told me they were in love-"
"That would have taken a good deal of courage."
"Well, that wasn't what they did. Now there's noth-
ing for me to do but get out of their way."
"People have a right to be either married or single,"
said Mrs. Meigs. "I suppose you want your freedom, too."
"She wants to marry-Churchill, doesn't she?" asked
Carter.
She mustn't let him find out that Churchill didn't
want to marry Natalie. Cast-off and marked-down-no,
she couldn't admit that humiliation. "I don't think she
ought to marry him," she said slowly. "They'd be getting
a very bad start."
"But what will she do, if she doesn't marry him?"
"Marry some one else, I suppose, eventually. She's still
a very pretty woman. And if a woman has once had a
home of her own, she won't willingly get along with-
out it."
"Meantime she can stay with you, can't she?"
"It would be hard luck for her if she could. I never
think much of the life a daughter leads with an old
mother: trailing about from one old ladies' party to an-
other, and keeping track of the things Mother loses, andplaying cribbage with the old lady in the evening, and
going to bed every night at ten o'clock."
"That doesn't sound to me very much like your house-
hold," said Carter.
"Whether it is or isn't, is all beside the point. You
haven't heard the news, evidently. I'm giving up my
flat, Carter."
"No? You don't say so! I knew they were all at you
that night of your birthday party; but I thought you suc-
ceeded pretty well in standing them off."
"Things have happened since," said Mrs. Meigs. "Quite
a lot of things."
"Have some more coffee," said Carter hastily. "It's too
damn bad, Mrs. Meigs. Are you going to live with your
children?"
"Yes, and drink cocoa when I'd prefer coffee, and take
a taxi when I'd rather ride in the street-car." Mrs. Meigs
smiled grimly. "I'm more in need of rescue this very
minute than any girl who ever thought of herself as an
imprisoned princess. But I'm too old now to believe in
fairy-tales."
Carter lighted a cigarette, and smoked slowly. "This
business of Natalie's must have worried you terribly,
coming right on top of other troubles, too. I'm not go-
ing to do the worst thing I can think of, you know.
Not now. I believe I shall charge desertion. That's a nice,
respectable charge."
"You want to go ahead with this divorce, Carter?"
"I could let her get it, I suppose. That's considered
the gentlemanly thing, isn't it?" Carter flushed. "She
could charge non-support."
"You mustn't be so unfair to yourself," protested
Mrs. Meigs. "You've always supported her.""Not the way I'd like to. I've never made quite enough
money," said Carter bitterly. "Natalie has seen her sis-
ters have more than she had. She must have known her
friends talked about her not having a car, and wearing
Edith's cast off clothes."
"Money is a nuisance," said Mrs. Meigs warmly. "If
you have enough, you can ignore it. But when you
haven't got money, it may be frightfully important."
"It's about the most important thing there is. I'll do
the best I can for her in the money way now, I promise
you."
Natalie would have a quiet divorce, then, some money
to get along on, a degree of independence while she
looked about her. This wasn't so bad. It was better than
Carter would have done for her if he'd just been left to
stew in his own juice.
"Thank you, Carter," she said quietly.
The waitress yawned, and approached to drop a check
on their table. "I guess they want to close," said Carter
apologetically. "They don't keep open for dinner."
Mrs. Meigs reached for the check, and then remem-
bered, she mustn't insult his poverty. She picked up a
packet of matches instead, and stowed them away in her
handbag.
It was dark when they reached the street. The wind
had fallen, and she felt a drop of rain on her cheek.
"You'd better have a taxi," said Carter.
"Not unless it really rains. I like to ride in a street-
car, and see the people," objected Mrs. Meigs.
They began to walk the two blocks to her car line.
Mrs. Meigs had never liked Carter especially; he was
naturally too apologetic to suit her. But he had been in
her family circle, and in a sense belonging to her. Whenthey parted to-day, it would mean a final separation.
And at her age, separating from any friend was a piece
gone out of her life.
"Maybe you'd better go along on the car, to help me
off at my corner," she hazarded. "The conductors are
pretty busy at this time of day. You needn't walk up to
the house with me if you don't want to. It's only half
a block."
"I'm not going to push you on the car and desert
you," said Carter. "It isn't every day a lady picks me up
for afternoon tea."
The street-car was crowded. Some one rose at once
to give Mrs. Meigs a seat. As she sank into it, a man stand-
ing in the aisle turned. He towered over her, clasping a
huge black cotton umbrella. When Mrs. Meigs saw who he
was, she tried to hide behind Carter.
She wasn't quick enough. "Hello, there!" shouted Dr.
Manning. "You alone?"
She shook her head, and pointed to Carter. Dr. Man-
ning grinned from ear to ear. "Up to your old tricks!"
he bellowed.
"He's my son-in-law!" shrieked Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, is he? Then what's a duty for him will be a pleas-
ure for me. I'll be glad to see you home. It's beginning to
rain, and you haven't any umbrella."
"I live only half a block from the corner," began
Mrs. Meigs. She was aware that everybody around was
grinning. Deaf people were an awful nuisance. Still, you
could sometimes take advantage of them. She lowered
her voice to say to Carter, "If you'll only walk to the
door with me, please! I can't stand him to-night."
"Want me to get rid of him?" asked Carter.
"He's a very old friend. But if he goes home withme, he'll come in. And once he's in, he never knows
when to leave."
Carter nodded, and managed to spread his newspaper
so that it sheltered her from Dr. Manning's beaming
glances. Acting gallant to her in a street-car, at the top
of his lungs! Really, the old idiot ought to be shut up
some place.
As the car approached her corner, Carter helped her
through the crowd. Dr. Manning shoved his way after
them. "This isn't your corner," said Mrs. Meigs.
"It isn't a bit out of my way," insisted Dr. Manning.
"I'm taking Mrs. Meigs home," said Carter. "So you
really needn't bother."
"But you haven't any umbrella," insisted Dr. Mann-
ing.
He got off with them, and spread the huge cotton
shelter. Carter took Mrs. Meigs's elbow, and set off at a
brisk pace. On their heels walked Dr. Manning. It was
raining hard now, and the drippings from his umbrella
streamed on top of Mrs. Meigs's bonnet.
"You must miss Cal!" he shouted.
"Of course I miss him," said Mrs. Meigs. She could
hardly bear to speak of Cal yet; and to have his name
shouted at her in the street-!
Dr. Manning blithely stepped on her heel, and didn't
know the difference. "Course, I couldn't say anything
myself," he boomed. "But the other day, when I got
back from Cal's funeral, my daughter-in-law said to
me, 'This leaves an inside track for you, doctor.' Yes,
ma'am, that's what she said. This leaves an inside track
for you." He guffawed.
"If I ever marry again," said Mrs. Meigs with freez-
ing dignity, "I'm going to get me a young husband.""Now that's encouraging!" roared Dr. Manning.
"That's the first time I ever heard you admit you'd like
to get married again."
"I didn't say-" began Mrs. Meigs.
Carter pressed her arm. "Don't argue back," he whis-
pered. "Just step along as fast as you can."
Arm in arm, the two of them scurried the last re-
maining yards to the door of Mrs. Meigs's building. Dr.
Manning's long legs easily kept pace with them. "I have
some money of my own," he boomed. "And this um-
brella for a rainy day. Ha, ha, ha! You don't have to
decide to-night, Mrs. Meigs. But think it over. Meigs
and Manning. 'Change the name but not the letter,
marry for worse and not for better.' Still, it might be
better at that."
Mrs. Meigs went up two steps, to get her head on a
level with his before she faced him. "Come over some
day," she shrieked. "But be sure and telephone before
you come. I can't ask you in now, because Carter's com-
ing for dinner."
Dr. Manning was still standing at the foot of the
steps when she and Carter hurried into the building.
"You'll have to come up to my door," whispered Mrs.
Meigs. "He can see us here."
They raced up the stairs. Outside her door, she turned
and looked at Carter with a sheepish smile. He laughed
out loud. "I supposed when women got to a certain
age, they weren't pestered by persistent suitors."
"When a woman gets old, it's worse than ever," said
Mrs. Meigs. "Then the old buzzards think their atten-
tions will be welcome. The very idea!"
Carter laughed again. This time Mrs. Meigs joined in
the laughter. "It is funny, in one way. But you'd thinka man might show a little more sense by the time he has
one foot in the grave."
They were making noise enough to be heard through
a closed door. At this moment it opened. There stood
Natalie, concern giving place to relief when she saw
Mrs. Meigs once more accounted for. "Mother, you've
been gone so long, I was beginning to worry."
She stopped abruptly. She saw who was with her
mother.
"Just a minute till I explain!" Mrs. Meigs hooked an
arm through Carter's, and drew him into her entrance
hall. "Carter has just rescued me from the worst fate
that can befall a woman. I mean, being bored to death."
"You ought to keep a better eye on your mother,"
said Carter. "If I hadn't been there to protect her, she'd
have been picked up. I can wait in the hall, Mrs. Meigs,
until I'm sure he's gone."
He laid a hand on the door-knob. He did not look at
Natalie again. But just for an instant, he hesitated. In
that instant, Mrs. Meigs saw clearly what lay before
him this evening. She hazarded a suggestion. "Why
don't you take Natalie out to dinner with you? I'd ask
you to stay here, only the whole place is in a mess."
"Oh, no!" whispered Natalie with a frightened look.
At the same moment Carter said stiffly, "Sorry,
but-"
"It would be the nicest way to say farewell," insisted
Mrs. Meigs. "There used to be a song when I was young,
'We do not speak as we pass by.' But that's out of date
now."
"We'll speak all right," said Carter. "You're looking
well, Natalie.”
"The rest has done her good," said Mrs. Meigs."Yes. I'm I'm feeling well," said Natalie.
Carter fumbled again with the door-knob. Mrs. Meigs
stepped past Natalie, and said over her shoulder, "See
you soon, Carter. Thanks a lot." She could still move
fast, when the occasion warranted it. She slipped into
the living-room, and left them together. But she didn't
quite close the door.
"That's a good idea of your mother's," said Carter,
"unless, of course, you have another engagement."
"Are you asking because mother told you to?" de-
manded Natalie. "Or do you really want me to go with
you?"
Mrs. Meigs shut the door and moved away. It's awk-
ward to be caught eavesdropping; and of course there's
no point to it when you already know what the people
are going to say.
The door reopened, to admit Natalie alone. "I don't
know what's come over Carter," she said doubtfully.
"He's more genial than I've seen him in years. And he
insists that I go out to dinner with him."
"Go ahead," advised Mrs. Meigs. "Have him take you
to Rispigliosi's, and order French pastry for your dessert.
It's very good there. And do try to make yourself look
like something. This is sort of a farewell party; and the
way you've been moping around here for the past two
weeks, he won't think he's losing much."
Natalie disappeared into the guest-room. Carter came
in, took a chair near the door, and lighted a cigarette.
"I don't know whether I ought to be doing this," he
apologized. "But we have a lot of things to talk over."
"Take my advice, and forget 'em," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Those women who write columns of advice in the
newspapers always recommend 'a frank talk' to hus-bands and wives, to settle all their difficulties. If you
ask me, most married couples talk to each other a lot
too much, and a lot too frankly."
"We're hardly what you could call a married couple,"
said Carter ruefully.
Mrs. Meigs took off her bonnet, and settled herself in
her rocking-chair. "You still living in the same flat,
Carter?"
"Yes. I haven't got around to look for a new place
yet."
"Then after you've had dinner, why don't you take
Natalie up there for a smoke? You might give her a
cocktail too. You have them sometimes, don't you?"
"That sounds to me like an improper suggestion,"
said Carter, passing it off as a joke. But he added im-
mediately, "The flat was cleaned to-day, so it'll look all
right."
"She brought only a suitcase with her," said Mrs.
Meigs. "You can take her up to get some more clothes.
Then once there, offer her the cocktail."
Carter shook his head. "Beginning all over wouldn't
change either of us. I suppose that is your idea, that we
should begin all over?"
"My idea?" asked Mrs. Meigs innocently. "What
makes you think I have any idea?"
"I never expected to see Natalie again, except per-
haps in a lawyer's office. But here I am waiting to take
her out to dinner. Queer, to know a person as well as I
know her. She always takes two cigarettes' worth of
time to get her dress changed." He lighted a second
cigarette.
"Take a rocking-chair while you wait," suggested
Mrs. Meigs.He shook his head, and looked hard at her. "It strikes
me you schemed all this pretty cleverly."
"Schemed nothing!" said Mrs. Meigs. "How was I to
know Dr. Manning would be on the street-car?"
"I don't know how you knew it. But I'd take my oath
in court to this," said Carter. "You're a pretty deep
old lady!"
She felt that she didn't deserve the compliment. Luck
had been with her for a moment. But it was only since
Tip had made her so ashamed of herself a few hours
ago that she had deserved any luck.
Natalie appeared, administering a last dab with her
powder-puff. "I borrowed a hanky from you," she
whispered. Natalie always ran short of handkerchiefs.
Carter followed her out, with a last glance of mingled
amusement and suspicion at his mother-in-law. "Don't
hurry back," said Mrs. Meigs. "I shall be sitting up late
to-night."
The dining-room was in a clutter of excelsior and
partly packed dishes. Mrs. Meigs had her dinner off a
tray in the living-room. Then she settled herself sternly
to play solitaire. She couldn't have kept her mind on
a book; and she was afraid that if she didn't keep her
hands busy, she might be found with her fingers crossed.
By ten o'clock Mrs. Meigs could sit still no longer.
She set to work desperately packing dishes. She was
tired, and her hands shook; she nicked a cup. She put it
out for the janitor, and picked up her knitting.
Finally she gave in, and just sat watching the clock.
No news must surely be good news. If they didn't call
her by twelve, she'd have courage to believe-
Why, this was superstition! Twelve o'clock nothing!
You couldn't go dragging a baby around in the middleof the night. At twenty minutes past eleven she ignored
the quivering uncertainty that beset her, and called Tip
O'Neil.
"Bring Molly and the baby here for the night, Tip,"
she directed. "They can have my guest-room, until to-
morrow at least."XVIII
JUST before midnight Carter telephoned to say, "That
was a good idea about the cocktail, Mother." He had
always addressed her as "Mrs. Meigs." Funny, Carter
starting in after all these years to call her "Mother."
Natalie came for her suitcase the next morning. Mrs.
Meigs missed her. She had taken Molly downtown, to
buy a baby-carriage.
Tip telephoned at noon, to see how things were go-
ing. He was putting in a hard day's work at his new
job, but he promised to come around in the evening.
Mrs. Meigs was still talking to him when Millicent
sailed in.
Millicent couldn't stay; she had to take Cecile to the
dressmaker's, and after that they were going to look
at apartments. "There'll be a place for the sacred dishes,
you see," she explained. "Luckily dear Roger doesn't
have to consider expense."
Molly was in the guest-room with the baby, and the
door was closed. Anna sidled toward it, keeping an eye
on her enemy. "You mustn't be late in meeting Cecile,"
said Mrs. Meigs.
"I am never late," proclaimed Millicent. "I thought
while I'm here, I'd like to see Natalie for a minute.
Yesterday at a committee meeting I heard a story-it
came through Carter's lawyer-" She broke off, scowl-
ing at Anna.
"Mis' Brooks has gone home," announced Anna."Really?" boomed Millicent. "Then it was just
gossip!"
"What was the gossip?" asked Mrs. Meigs innocently.
Then she bit her tongue. Through the closed door came
the unmistakable howl of a hungry baby.
Millicent turned on her mother a glance of militant
inquiry. "You didn't know I had a baby, did you?"
asked Mrs. Meigs. "I should have warned you not to talk
so loud, or you'd wake him."
Millicent's eye said plainly what she thought of that
kind of joke. But Mrs. Meigs's humor always took her
at a disadvantage. "I'm sure I don't know what you're
up to now," she said uncertainly. "I wish Natalie had
stayed here, though. She was better than nothing."
Mrs. Meigs moved toward the front windows. "Have
you a taxi waiting, Millicent?" she hinted.
Then she saw who it was that was waiting for Milli-
cent: Cecile, in her new car. Cecile, who used to run in
almost every day to say "Hello"-Cecile didn't come
in now even when she was at the door.
Mrs. Meigs's relief at having Natalie settled once more
oozed away. Even the satisfaction of a joke on Millicent
didn't help her now. Millicent bustled away; but she'd
be back. She'd be back and she was riding in triumph
over her mother. Well, she shouldn't go on riding, that
was all. One resource was still left to Mrs. Meigs: flight!
Wild visions of Europe floated through Mrs. Meigs's
mind. She'd often talked of Europe; but she'd never
actually got around to it. Europe! That was a trump,
an ace of trumps. It was worth spending her principal,
to put the Atlantic Ocean between herself and Mil-
licent.
But of course she never led her ace of trumps so soon.If she mentioned Europe her children might send for a
strait-jacket. She would, for the first few weeks, go
back to her old home town in Ohio, where she and her
husband and Cal had all been young together.
She hadn't been there for several years. When she did
go, her first visit was always to the cemetery where her
parents were buried. The whole place, nowadays, seemed
to her like a cemetery. Strangers and youngsters sat in
the places where her elders used to sit.
On second thoughts, she didn't want to go there
now. The shade of young Carrie Eaton would meet old
Mrs. Meigs in those streets. Beside, in this life you
couldn't go back. You had to go on. Even at eighty, you
had to go on.
She didn't want to be among ghosts. She wanted to be
among young people. Young people! She knew where
there were a lot of them. One of them belonged to her.
He'd never been as close to her as Cecile; but then, he'd
never shrunk away as Cecile was shrinking now.
Funny, that she should be reduced to Richard. But
he'd always been very fond of her, in his casual way.
Mrs. Meigs liked to think that he got his casualness
from her. This moment she found herself thirsty for
casualness. Devotion, she had decided, was an awful
nuisance.
She went to the telephone, and called long distance.
Richard was in his room when her call went through.
That was a good omen.
"Dick,” she asked, "can you make it convenient to
drive over here this afternoon? Nothing has hap-
pened," she hastened to assure him. "But I've decided
it's time something did. And I want to see you the
minute you can get here.""Will this evening do?" he asked. "I'll start right
after dinner, and with luck I can make it by half past
ten."
"Fine!" said Mrs. Meigs. "I'll look for you then."
She'd go back with him this very night. If she found
she liked it in Madison, she might open a rooming-
house for students, and postpone Europe. How shocked
Millicent would be at the idea of her mother renting
rooms! But short of going to court and having the old
lady judged incompetent, which the rest of the family
would never permit, there wasn't a thing Millicent could
do about it!
The baby-carriage arrived by special delivery the
first thing after lunch, and Mrs. Meigs sent Molly and
the baby out for a long walk, while she herself paid a
hurried visit to the bank. First she thought of taking a
bond with her, just "in case." Then she decided on
the cash instead. Five one-hundred dollar bills made a
big lump in her stocking. The feeling of it gave her
confidence.
Back in the apartment, she handed one of the bills
to Anna. "I want you to stay here and take care of
Mrs. O'Neil for a few days," she said. "If Mrs. Graham
insists on finishing the packing, let her do it. But you
needn't put yourself out to help her. I'm going away
until the confusion is over."
"I've been thinking lately, I s'pose it's about time
I began looking for a new place," said Anna.
"You can go on staying with me, if you've no ob-
jections to going out of town," said Mrs. Meigs.
"Out of town? I thought you was going to Mis'
Graham's."
"She thinks I am. But she's mistaken, Anna.""It's not the only thing she's ever been mistaken in,"
said Anna with a grin.
"Don't be impertinent to her," warned Mrs. Meigs.
"At least, not any more than you can help. And keep
your eyes closed for a few hours, Anna, and ask me no
questions. Then if they come down on you to find out
what you know, you won't know anything."
"Don't they know where you're going?" hinted
Anna.
"Do you call that not asking any questions?" de-
manded Mrs. Meigs.
She shut herself in her bedroom, and began to pack.
A suitcase and a traveling bag would go in Richard's
car. They would hold all she really needed. The many
things that were dear to her, she'd have to leave behind.
A month before she would have been heart-broken at
the idea of turning her back on her own furniture,
her prized dishes, her pet clock, the rose-geraniums that
would grow for her when they died for anybody else.
She was so excited that she could hardly eat her
dinner. Molly, tired from an afternoon of wheeling the
new baby-carriage, subsided into a big chair instead of
putting her baby to bed. Mrs. Meigs picked him up,
with a sidelong glance at his mother. Doubtless Molly
was still numb with the shock of what had happened
to her. But Mrs. Meigs hoped Molly wasn't the sort who
came to enjoy being a problem to other people.
She had just got the baby undressed and laid in his
buggy, which would do him nicely for a bed until he
was a little bigger, when she heard the door-bell ring,
and Anna go to answer. That was Tip, of course. He
had promised to look in this evening.
"In here!" called Mrs. Meigs.But it wasn't Tip who appeared in the doorway. It
was Cecile. "Well, what on earth-?" she began.
"Mother said you had a baby staying here, she gathered.
But I thought-"
"You thought I was beginning to have delusions?"
asked Mrs. Meigs. "Or maybe you thought Millicent
was."
"Well, what are you doing with a baby?" demanded
Cecile.
"I'm thinking of adopting him," said Mrs. Meigs with
a chuckle. "I guess that would put your nose out of
joint, wouldn't it?"
Cecile smiled uncertainly. Before she could ask any
more questions, Mrs. Meigs suggested, "Don't you want
to pick him up for a minute? Modern babies aren't
supposed to be picked up, I know. But if we want to
cheat a little, we won't tell on each other."
Cecile approached the buggy; she stood looking down
into it, with her hands in her coat pockets. "Your gen-
eration went goofy over babies, Caroline," she said. "But
I don't see where the kick comes in. Seems to me they're
an awful lot of bother."
The baby looked up at her. Cecile continued to stare
down at him, unsmiling. All at once he put out his
hands to her, and crowed.
Cecile started and blushed. "Does he mean he wants
to come to me?" she demanded. But without waiting
for an answer, she picked him up.
Over his head she smiled at Mrs. Meigs. "I suppose
you're looking forward to being a great-grandmother,
Caroline."
"A great-grandmother?" echoed Mrs. Meigs indig-
nantly. "Indeed I'm not! Being a grandmother is won-derful. You have all the fun of the children, and none
of the responsibility. But being a great-grandmother is
an invitation to climb up the family tree and stay
there."
"Well, we won't argue about it just yet." Cecile laid
the baby back in his buggy, and patted his head softly.
"Caroline, do you want to do something for me?"
"You don't need to ask that. There never was a time
when I didn't do everything you asked me to."
"I know. But I thought you might draw the line at
deception. Fact is, though, I committed you before I
asked. I told Mother I was coming here to spend the
night."
"To spend the night-to-night?" Mrs. Meigs felt like
an eloper surprised by the irate parent just when the
rope ladder comes down.
"That's what I told Mother. If she calls up, you can
make some excuse for me. Then to-morrow I'll go back
and face the music." She turned away, avoiding her
grandmother's eye.
"Just what is it you are trying to let me in on?"
asked Mrs. Meigs.
Cecile sat down on the edge of the bed, and lighted
a cigarette. "Don't smoke in here," said Mrs. Meigs.
"It's time the baby went to sleep, anyhow."
Molly was snuggled into a corner of the living-room
couch. Mrs. Meigs took Cecile into the dining-room.
"Now tell me what this is all about. You want your
mother to think you're here when you aren't here.
Why?"
"The fact is," said Cecile, "I'm not up to following
out the plans Mother is buzzing with. Roger's all right
in his way. He isn't romantic, and I'm not in love withhim. But he's a decent sort, and I think I might do a
whole lot worse than to marry him."
"Indeed?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Yes, indeed," said Cecile. "But I can't endure having
him chucked down my throat morning, noon and
night. Just the way Mother says 'Dear Roger!' you'd
think she invented him. I can't go through with getting
up a big trousseau, and having a big wedding, and all
the ghastly entertaining they inflict on brides. My face
is stiff already with trying to smile, and the ghastliness
has hardly even begun."
"If you feel that way about it," interrupted Mrs.
Meigs, "it shows conclusively that you have no busi-
ness to be marrying him. There's nothing that's more
fun for a girl"
Cecile waved for silence. "I can't," she said through
her teeth, "I can't stand it to look ahead to a set day
for my wedding, and see it getting nearer and nearer."
"So you're running away?" asked Mrs. Meigs hope-
fully. Embarrassing, when the whole family decided
to run away the same evening. But perhaps Cecile's
flight could be worked in with Mrs. Meigs's own.
"Roger is driving me down into Illinois to-night,"
said Cecile. "We're only fifty miles or so from the State
line; and in Illinois it's possible to get a license and be
married at once, without any waiting."
Mrs. Meigs felt the room swim about her. "You crazy
child, you mustn't do this! Not in such a hurry, any-
how. You don't realize what you're doing. Wait a week,
and try giving your mother a hint. Then if that doesn't
work-"
"Did you ever know a hint to work with Mother?"
asked Cecile. "It's all settled, Caroline. My bag is inyour front hall, and Roger's waiting until I telephone
him. I arranged it that way because I'm not sure I ought
to ring you in on this. But if you thought you could
do this much for me, it would be the simplest way."
Did it make much difference whom Cecile married?
She had turned to her grandmother for help. In the
face of that blessed fact, Mrs. Meigs was tempted to
concede Roger as a lesser issue.
Then she remembered Cecile with the baby in her
arms. Climb up the family tree indeed, for the pudgy
infants Roger Kane would be father to! "I hate to find
myself siding with Millicent against you," said Mrs.
Meigs. "But if you start to do this hateful thing, Cecile,
I'll telephone Millicent to come for you."
"Why not call out the police?" asked Cecile, with a
trace of her old twinkle. She moved toward the living-
room door. "If I asked him to wait a day or two, would
that satisfy you?"
"Will you do that?" asked Mrs. Meigs eagerly. "If
you insist on running off with Roger, darling, of course
I can't stop you. But I have a feeling you don't really
want to do such a thing. You're just rushing into it,
to keep from hearing the voice of your own heart."
"That's the most sentimental thing I've ever heard
you say," said Cecile.
"It's the first important thing I've ever asked of you,
Cecile. Is it too much?"
Cecile threw an arm around Mrs. Meigs, and drew
her into her stride. "No, darling, it isn't too much. It's
a great opportunity, really. It's the first chance I've
ever had to make you admit afterward that you've been
in the wrong."
The old note of teasing was music to Mrs. Meigs'sears. "You'll postpone your trip into Illinois, Cecile?"
"About day after to-morrow you'll be sending me
off," said Cecile. "Think of poor Roger's feelings,
though, when I telephone him that he won't be a bride-
groom to-night."
"Do you have to telephone him?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Darling, I can't just leave him hanging." Cecile
reached for the telephone, and puckered her brows to

Molly in a corner of the couch. "Oh, sorry!" she said,
and drew back her hand.
"Molly's just going into her own room," said Mrs.
Meigs. "This is my granddaughter, Molly. She's been
admiring your baby."
"The baby has a mother, it seems," murmured Cecile.
"Yes. Mrs. O'Neil-Tip's cousin," explained Mrs.
Meigs.
"Oh, yes!" said Cecile. At the name her half smile
had frozen on her lips. She didn't even wait for Molly's
languid exit. She called Roger's number.
He answered instantly. He must have been sitting
beside the instrument. "Roger?" said Cecile. "It's all
right. You can come for me any time. I'm at my grand-
mother's. You know the way."
"Do you need him to take you back home? Didn't
you come in your own car?" asked Mrs. Meigs.
"Just a minute, Caroline!" Cecile listened at the
telephone. "Yes, of course that's what I mean, Roger.
That won't take you long, will it? Remember, I'm
...
waiting."
She hung up, and faced her grandmother. "Cecile,
you promised you wouldn't!" begged Mrs. Meigs."I'm sorry if you thought it was a promise, Caroline.
Because I've changed my mind."
She hadn't changed her mind. The mere mention of
Tip's name had done it for her. She was running away
from Tip, from the very idea of him, from the danger
to her security that the thought of him invoked.
"Cecile, I can't stand this! If you won't stop for your
own sake, then for mine!"
Mrs. Meigs broke off in despair. There was no arguing
with that flinty young face.
"I wish you liked Roger a little better," said Cecile
coolly. "But after all, the relatives never are pleased-
not all of them, anyhow."
She sat down, and lighted a cigarette. Mrs. Meigs sat
down opposite, keeping a tight rein on herself. A tense
silence lengthened. Mrs. Meigs could hear the clock
ticking two rooms away. If she could have trusted her
knees to support her, she would have got up and
stopped it.
As Cecile reached for a second cigarette, the door-
bell rang. They both jumped. "I didn't think Roger
would be quite so prompt," murmured Cecile. "He
wanted to have the car oiled and gassed, and stop for
a minute at his club."
She looked around with a dutiful smile. Mrs. Meigs
could feel her own face hardening.
But the expressions of both changed with comical
suddenness, as Anna ushered in Tip O'Neil.XIX
TIP
was the only one of the three who was not em-
barrassed. He stood in the doorway of the living-room,
his face lighted with his old grin. "You're a sight for
tired eyes, Cecile! Blooming as a rose, the same as
always!"
"It's only two weeks since you saw me," said Cecile
curtly. "Did you expect me to be much changed?"
"Everything else is so changed, I guess I did." Tip
turned to Mrs. Meigs. "I hear Cecile has put it in the
paper that she's marrying her fat friend. Why don't
you explain to her that it isn't at all a nice idea?"
"If you stick around, you can explain it to him your-
self," retorted Cecile. "He's coming in a few minutes
to call for me."
"I'm not sticking, thanks," said Tip. "It's well
known that the Irish never stay where they aren't
wanted. I'll just have a word with Molly, Mrs. Meigs. Is
she still stiff with the sight of strange faces?"
"She doesn't take much interest in anything but the
baby," admitted Mrs. Meigs.
"Well, that's what we have to expect yet a while." He
turned toward the door. "Where have you got her?"
Mrs. Meigs looked at Cecile. Cecile was staring at
Tip, unable to believe that he was actually walking
away from her. Pride and anger would have sustained
her nicely in an open quarrel; but she wasn't going to
allow him to turn his back."You might at least-" she burst out. "You might
wish me good luck."
Tip spoke over his shoulder, with a flash of his usual
humor. "Oh, you're sure to have good luck! I've used
up all the bad."
"Is that the only message you have for me," asked
Cecile hotly, "on my wedding night?"
He wheeled at that. "Your what? Say it slower. I
thought you were going through the usual rigmarole of
long engagement and society wedding."
"You thought wrong. That isn't my idea. We're go-
ing away to-night. You might you might wish me
luck."
"I do," said Tip. "I do, most heartily."
"Then won't you shake hands?"
Slowly he crossed the space between them, and held
out his hand. Laying her hand in his, Cecile looked into
his eyes. "Tip," said Mrs. Meigs abruptly, "I think you
owe Cecile an explanation. You went off without a
word to her, you know; and since you got back, there
hasn't been any chance to tell her."
"Explanations are dull hearing," said Tip. He pressed
Cecile's hand, and dropped it.
Cecile tried to speak lightly. "Your explanations are
always good, Tip. Won't you treat me to one more of
them, just for old time's sake?"
"No, I won't," said Tip shortly. "If I can't have your
love, I won't take your pity."
Cecile's eyes were fastened on him, as if everything
else in the world had grown vague to her. "Tip, this may
be the last thing I ever ask of you. Won't you please
stop beating about the bush, and tell me?"
"It tells very easily, darling," said Tip gently. "Thenew government dam broke. My Western land, that I
was counting on so hard, is worse than desert now. In-
stead of a poor man, I'm a ruined one."
"Did this happen-lately?"
"The day after I made up my mind to sell part of it
for you and me to get married on and keep the rest
to blossom for our children and our children's children."
The whole bitterness of his disappointment came
flooding over him. He sank into a chair, and stared
gloomily straight ahead of him.
Cecile's eyes never left him. Her lips barely moved
as she whispered, "But they'll build the dam again."
"After years and years, maybe," muttered Tip.
"And you were counting so on that! Oh!" Cecile's
voice broke. "I didn't want this to happen! I didn't
want this to happen to you!"
She took a step toward him. He looked up, and put
out a hand to her. The next moment she was kneeling
beside him, drawing his head down on her breast.
"They've gone and hurt you so!" she whispered. "I
didn't want any one to hurt you."
"Well," asked Mrs. Meigs sharply, "what are you go-
ing to do about it?"
Cecile started, and drew away a little. But Tip's arms
were around her still; Tip's tired head was heavy on her
breast. "I'd forgotten you were there, Caroline," she

"I'd leave you to yourselves with pleasure," said Mrs.
Meigs, "if there were time to spare. But there isn't. Any
minute that door-bell may ring, and Roger Kane come
marching in."
"I can't see Roger now!" gasped Cecile.
"Roger be damned!" said Tip. "You belong to me.""I'd better go home to-night," said Cecile. "I'll make
some excuse to Roger. And then to-morrow, Tip-I'll
see you to-morrow."
Postponement has wrecked many a generous impulse.
"To-morrow is too long to wait," said Mrs. Meigs. "Ce-
cile, you've made your arrangements for getting mar-
ried to-night. Why don't you switch bridegrooms, and

"Good God!" cried Tip. "Do you want her to marry
a man under sentence of execution?"
"Oh!" said Cecile. "I belong to you, do I, until it's a
question of really belonging? Then you sidestep."
"Just for that," said Tip, "I've a good mind to put
you under my arm, and go off in the old rattler."
"We'd better use my car," said Cecile crisply. "It's a
long trip to Illinois."
"Too long," said Tip. "I know a man in the License
Bureau here. We can rout him out, and get a special dis-
pensation. The evening is yet young. By ten o'clock-"
"If that isn't just like you!" Cecile smiled. "I suppose
if I marry you, all our lives we'll never have any money,
but you'll always know a man who can get you a pass
for ringside seats."
They stood up, facing each other. "You willing to

Cecile nodded, breathless. "It's not so awful as the
other thing. If you knew how miserable I've been these

"I didn't want to give you a sob story," interrupted

I can't take advantage of you like this."
Cecile stamped her foot. "Will you stop talking andcome on? Hurry, hurry, hurry-before I come to my
senses again!"
"Before you lose them again, you mean," said Mrs.
Meigs. "Yes, Tip, you'd better hurry."
Tip whirled on Mrs. Meigs. "It's a mean trick on
Cecile. But I'm going to play it. After all, it isn't every
day a girl asks me to marry her. You might even say she

"Then there isn't a minute to lose,” warned Mrs.
Meigs.
"All right," said Tip. "Let's go! You're coming along,
of course, to represent the family?"
"Don't insult Caroline," said Cecile crisply. "She's
coming along as bridesmaid."
Mrs. Meigs shooed them toward the door. "I've a date
of my own this evening, if you'll be good enough to run

Cecile kissed her. Tip looked as if he would like to.
Mrs. Meigs pointed sternly to Cecile's suitcase. They
ran downstairs, like two children off on a picnic.
Mrs. Meigs got to the front window in time to see
Cecile start toward her own car. Tip swung her away
from it, into his. Mrs. Meigs watched the car out of sight.
She was on the point of turning away when she saw
a Hispano-Suiza approach from the other direction, and
draw up in front of the building. She had barely time

Roger had just been shaved. That was why he'd
stopped at his club. Well, his vanity had cost him a
bride.At the sight of Mrs. Meigs he smiled uneasily. "Ce-
cile ready?" he asked.
"Cecile isn't here," said Mrs. Meigs coolly. "Won't

"Has she gone out for something? She telephoned me
to call for her here. She can't have gone far. Her car

"Did you notice whether her parking light is on?"

Roger sat on the edge of a chair. "I don't know
whether you're in her confidence, Mrs. Meigs."
"Oh, quite!" said Mrs. Meigs. "Cecile has gone down
into Illinois to get married. They used his car. That's

Roger stared at her with his mouth open. "She got

Roger understood that his bride was not forthcoming

"Oh, you'll find out!" Mrs. Meigs assured him. "All

He began to pace up and down, uneasy and puzzled.
In a way she was sorry for him. She wanted to give
Cecile and Tip time to find Tip's man from the Li-

could, she intended to tell Roger the whole story. She
would let him down just as easy as she could, too. Afterall, his fault had been nothing but loving Cecile-lov-
ing Cecile, and being a little too old for her.
But Mrs. Meigs's sympathy froze suddenly. Roger's

"Why didn't you go over there, if you had to see

"I intend to find out what's going on here, and I
can't do that at Mrs. Graham's," snorted Roger.
Mrs. Meigs had frantic thoughts of dashing out on

But of course that would only drive Millicent out on

Millicent found her playing solitaire, and Roger
walking the floor. "Will you tell Mrs. Graham about

"You tell her if you want her told," said Mrs. Meigs,

"Mrs. Meigs has been telling me a cock-and-bull

"Eloped? Where? With whom?" demanded Millicent.
"With Tip O'Neil," said Mrs. Meigs. "She was go-

Millicent favored Mrs. Meigs with a glance of deep

they'll want to take 'em back."
"There may be something in the story," said Roger.

"There's nothing you can do about it anyhow," sug-

"I'm not so sure!" Roger began to assert himself.

"It's her lookout," said Mrs. Meigs.
Roger glared at her. There was no mistaking what he

"It isn't too late!" Millicent was rallying from the

"She is of age to marry in Illinois," said Roger.
"You looked it all up for her, didn't you?" asked

"If they went to Illinois-" began Roger hastily.
"Did they, Mother?" asked Millicent.
Mrs. Meigs didn't answer. She was busy laying a black
ten on a black jack, which confused her game.
"Of course they did," said Roger impatiently. "This

I don't suppose you want the police in on this."
"If I can lay hands on them," said Millicent, "the
police won't be necessary."
Mrs. Meigs looked up from her solitaire. "Don't be a

"I'll settle with you later, Mother," said Millicent.
"I suppose you've been aiding and abetting this whole

"Don't give me credit where it isn't due,” said Mrs.
Meigs. But when she went to the window to watch the

aider and abetter must feel. Her accidental mention of
Illinois had led to consequences. Well, it wouldn't do

She locked her suitcase, and left both that and her
bag just inside her bedroom door. Then she sat down to
wait for Richard. If she hadn't already decided to leave
town, she'd have had to go now. There wasn't a city in
the country big enough to hold both her and Millicent,

darling scheme of a rich marriage for Cecile.
Mrs. Meigs was walking anxiously about, wishing
that Richard would come, when she heard a familiar

Meigs in alarm.
"Didn't you expect it to?" Tip took Cecile's hand,

O'Neil."
"It's done? Already?" gasped Mrs. Meigs.Cecile smiled. "It doesn't take so long. But I was
afraid you wouldn't believe it, unless we told you. Any-

"Roger and your mother are out hunting you," said

"Here?" echoed Tip. "You're right, they've already

"To-night? Here?" Cecile blushed like a pink peony,

"I can sleep in the spare room with Molly," said Mrs.

"A double bed. That's talking!" said Tip.
They both looked at Cecile. "Here, in Caroline's

Then she lifted her young head proudly, and looked

In the bustle of settling them, Mrs. Meigs could not

her little girl for the last time.She was crying quietly when Richard found her.
"You've got to take me away," she whispered. "You're

He sat down beside her on the couch. "I've never seen
you like this, Gram. Buck up and be yourself. Who's

"I've lived through worse things," sobbed Mrs. Meigs.
"When my baby died, and your grandfather, and when

"Well, you came through, didn't you?"
"But I had time on my side then. I could afford to

He glanced at her shrewdly. "You're tired out. Does

"A little. But I can ride back with you all right. The

"You're planning to drive back to Madison with

She hadn't explained it to him at all. She'd seen so
clearly this afternoon that it was the only thing she

"Just let me think a minute," she pleaded.

"There's no place for you to sleep. I've a house full,"
said Mrs. Meigs.
"Oh, I'll go home for the night!"
She had a feeling that she mustn't let him go home
now. That would give Millicent a chance to get in herfine work. But it had been a long, hard day. "If I could
sleep for just ten minutes-" she pleaded.
"Good idea!" Richard arranged the pillows, and
swung her around on them. "How's that? Comfy?"
"Lovely," she said drowsily. "But don't let me sleep
more than ten minutes. I've such a lot to tell you."
She was faintly aware of his covering her, and open-
ing a window. The next thing she knew the light was
in her eyes. She put up a hand to adjust the lampshade.
Then with a gasp she sat up and looked about her. She
was alone in the room; and the light that had waked
her was daylight.XX
SHE had slept all night with her clothes on. Such a
thing had never happened before in her life; and for a
minute she couldn't think why it had happened now.
She lay and stared at the morning sunshine streaming
in through the window. After all the rain they'd had
lately, it was a beautiful day.
She remembered now; Cecile was married. It seemed
to Mrs. Meigs that Cecile ought to be still playing with
dolls. Should she ever learn to think of her grand-
daughter as a married woman? Married life with Tip
O'Neil wouldn't be a bed of roses. But that streak in
Cecile which had threatened lately to turn to hardness
would come out as strength now that she was married
to the man she loved. "She married the man she loved!"
That wasn't the same as saying, "She lived happy ever
after." But it put a woman straight with life. Cecile
was straight now.
Waking so early had given Mrs. Meigs first chance
at the bathroom. She dumped lavender salts into the
tub with a liberal hand; she was feeling stale and
crumpled. She had a big day ahead of her. She must try
to see Natalie before she left. She wasn't easy in her
mind about Natalie, having to let herself be forgiven
and reconciled. It was all right as long as Carter's first
flush of generosity lasted. But that sort of thing gave
a man something to hold over a woman.
Mrs. Meigs had to put on the same clothes she{
taken off; still, she felt better after her bath. She was
fastening her collar when she heard the door-bell. Anna
was just getting up; Mrs. Meigs went to answer.
It was Richard, yawning a little in the morning
freshness. "Gram, have you any idea what has become
of Mother?" he demanded. "She hasn't been home all
night."
"The last I saw of your mother," said Mrs. Meigs,
"she was setting out on a long auto ride with Roger
Kane."
"Dad said she got a telephone call, and dashed off to
look for Cecile." All at once the joke dawned on him.
"Don't tell me my precious Mom has been out all night
with a man!"
"It looks to me very much as if she had," said Mrs.
Meigs. "Don't shout like that, Richard. You'll wake up
some of the sleepers."
Richard lay down on the couch to have his laugh
out. After a minute Mrs. Meigs began to laugh too.
Richard relished the joke on Millicent; Mrs. Meigs saw
in her mind's eye the chase Millicent had been leading
Roger Kane.
"By the way," Richard asked presently, "where is
Cecile?"
"Here," said Mrs. Meigs. "Cecile is married to that
young Irishman I like so much. They spent the night

"Well," said Richard, in another gale of laughter,
"I wouldn't like to be in your shoes when Mom finds

"Richard," said Mrs. Meigs solemnly, "I want to go

Give the excitement a few days to die down. Only it

"The music is too loud now," said Mrs. Meigs.
"They're turning me out of my own home, you know."

"What do they expect you to do with yourself? Hang

"Divide my time up among my children."
"Rubbish!" said Richard flatly. "Lucky you don't

"But Dick, I have paid attention. I told them I'd go."
Richard stared. "What on earth made you do that?"
"Temporary insanity, I guess," said Mrs. Meigs

much what happened to me from now on. But oh,

with the hired girls. But if I live in anybody else's

Richard slapped his pockets for his cigarette-case, pro-

"Yes. I don't suppose I had any right to drag you

"H'm! You should have sent for me sooner!"
Richard's eyes followed his ascending smoke. "Give me

"She has a guest-room that she wants me to take thechill off. And she wanted to dress me up like a horse
and buggy for Cecile's wedding."
"Well, that's out. How about Natalie?"
"I think Natalie would like me to stay right here."
On Natalie's account, she added to herself, she ought
to stay right here. She ought always to keep a home,
as a possible refuge for Natalie.
"It simmers down, then, to Mom and Uncle Jack?"
"Yes. Your mother has half my packing done al-
ready, and Jack has taken my lease, to see if he can't

Richard grinned. "We can blackmail Mom now. But
I guess I better get hold of Jack before he leaves for

He went to the telephone, and called Jack's hotel.
Mrs. Meigs went to the kitchen. "Anna," she said, "I

Mrs. Meigs and Richard were the first breakfasters.

She had felt in duty bound to make the suggestion.
When he turned it down, Mrs. Meigs felt relieved. His

His clear-sightedness she loved. It seemed to her that

She was pouring a second cup of coffee for him when

Just the same, there was a shade of truth in the charges

Richard came from the dining-room, and with a

"Cecile loved having that car," said Mrs. Meigs with

Tip put an arm around her. "I'll never make Cecile

"He hasn't bought a new car, that I know of." Mrs.

But Cecile walked back to Mrs. Meigs. "I'll call you

He was in the driver's seat when she turned back

Mrs. Meigs watched Tip's car down the block. Mustn't
watch it out of sight; that was bad luck. Anyhow
she mustn't waste time standing here, while her whole
future simmered in the house behind her. She had a
policy to live up to. At eighty, you couldn't go back,
you mustn't even look back.
She was still standing there, however, when the car
turned the corner, though she didn't see it turn. Her
keen old eyes had suddenly dimmed.
Something had gone from her to-day that would
never come back. Yet it was only partly for herself that
she was crying. Those two were young. Time, that eats
up everything in the end, was still on their side, and
working for them. But under the bravery of youth lies
a horrible distrust and fear.
They couldn't know how kind and lovely life may
be. Worse still, they couldn't know that in the black
days strength comes somehow, to endure, and to hope.
She knew, but she couldn't tell them. She knew, but
she was old. No one could give them a helping hand in
all they'd have to face. She loved them, but she was
old. Old age knows; but across the years, love itself is
helpless.XXI
SHE was starting back into the building when Jack
drove up. They walked in together as Roger Kane was
saying, "I couldn't make her marry me if she didn't
want to. But a man doesn't like to be publicly jilted."
Jack pricked up his ears. Millicent raged, "I'm not
going to sit by and see my life-work ruined. Cecile isn't
of age. I'll go to court and have this marriage annulled."
"It's already in the morning paper. Haven't you seen
one?" Jack took a folded paper from a side pocket.
"There you are, second column, 'Society Girl Weds by
Special License. Married last night by Justice Cana-
han-'"
Millicent looked at the paper, and collapsed into a
chair. Roger turned a withering glance upon her. "There
they were married all the time you were having us
drive on every bad road in northern Illinois!" he said.
"And making me get out at every filling station to ask
questions, because you were afraid the chauffeur would
miss something."
Richard grinned broadly. "That sample ought to make
you glad you aren't getting into our family."
"It does," said Roger with emphasis. "Just the same,
a man doesn't like to be publicly jilted."
"You said that before," said Richard blandly. "Did
Cecile leave the keys to her car, Gram?"
"Not with me," said Mrs. Meigs."She'll send them, then," said Richard.
Roger looked pretty flabby after being up all night.
"You'd better have a cup of coffee," suggested Mrs.
Meigs. "It's all ready."
"Oh, no, thank you! No, thank you!" said Roger.
He seemed a little shocked at the idea. "My man can
get keys from the auto salesrooms. If that's all-" He
looked at Millicent. "I guess it is all."
He beat a hasty retreat. "It was all his own fault,
really," said Mrs. Meigs. "He put the idea of eloping
into Cecile's head."
"She needn't have agreed to it," stormed Millicent.
"The very idea of my daughter doing such a thing! It's
an insult to the mother when a daughter elopes. An
insult!"
"Well, you brought it on yourself," said Richard
unsympathetically. "Shoving her on a fellow like that!
Wasn't he sorry for himself? And not a word about
Cecile!"
"I brought it on myself?" echoed Millicent. "I've
never wanted anything but what was for Cecile's own
good. I've gone without things I needed in order to
have her prettily dressed. I've pinched and scraped, and
left her free to enjoy herself. And here Roger had just
bought her that lovely car-”
"You're right, Millicent," said Mrs. Meigs. "You have
been a good mother, as you saw it. Cecile had got to
the place, though, where she had to begin to do her own
thinking."
"Well, it's all over anyhow but the shouting," said
Jack. "You better send out announcements, Mill. Won't
look quite so much as if we'd had a fight in the family."
"That's right," said Mrs. Meigs. "Come on now andhave some breakfast, Millicent. You must be famished.
Had yours, Jack?"
She led the way to the dining-room. The half-packed
barrels of dishes were a reminder of her own difficulties.
She looked significantly at Richard. Richard grinned
at her.
"Did you bring along that lease, Jack?" he demanded.
"Well, hand it over."
"I don't quite see why," said Jack. "It's all settled
that Mother is to break up here, and take turns living
with her children."
"It was settled," said Mrs. Meigs. "At least, you
thought it was. But it's come unsettled again. I've
changed my mind."
"Now see here, Mother," said Millicent. "Don't begin
that all over, please."
"It's already begun," said Richard coolly. "The only
question is, are we going to agree without fighting about
it, or do we have to have the fight first?"
"Richard, how do you get in on this?" demanded
Millicent. "And what are you doing here anyhow?
You're supposed to be in college."
"Gram sent for me," said Richard. "She wanted to
tell me something. She's probably tried to tell you too,
but you wouldn't listen. She doesn't want to leave here.
She feels that if she does give up her own home, it will
mean the beginning of the end for her. If you pack her
off to our house, she thinks she won't live another
year."
"Old people often get those ideas," said Jack.
"Very likely," said Richard. "But are you willing to
take the risk?"
Slowly Jack drew the lease out of his pocket. "It'sabsurd, I know. But if we did insist, and then anything
happened, I'd feel like a murderer."
"That's the boy." Richard took the lease, and passed
it to Mrs. Meigs. "Lock that up, and don't let it get out
of your hands again. Not that they could do anything
about it without your signature, I suppose. But it's a
bad idea to let Jack think he can handle your business
for you."
"That's enough from you, young man," said Jack.
But it's hard to squelch a nephew who is four inches
taller than you, even if Jack hadn't always encouraged
Richard to be saucy. He accepted a cup of coffee from
Mrs. Meigs, and prudently changed the subject. "Estelle
will be disappointed, you know, Mother. Since you
worked that bungalow trick for her, she's beginning
to think the sun rises and sets in you."
Millicent turned on him a look of scorn. "Really,
Jack, the last person you listen to is always the one you
think is right. Mother isn't fit to run her own affairs,
let alone keep house. She ought to have her money put
in a trust, and the income paid to some responsible
person who will look out for her."
Mrs. Meigs bit her tongue, and reminded herself that
Millicent was tired out. But Richard's mouth drew down
at the corners, and his eyes flashed. At that minute he
looked like Millicent.
"Mom," he said very distinctly, "you make me sick."
"It's not surprising you take your grandmother's part
against me," retorted Millicent. "She has always put
you and your sister up to”
"That's enough!" interrupted Richard. "You've given
yourself away now. You've always been jealous because
Cecile was fonder of Gram than she was of you. Whywouldn't she be? Everybody is. You try to run people,
and Gram tries to make 'em happy."
"Jealous? Jealous of my own mother?" Millicent was
purple with rage. "How dare you say a thing like that
to me?"
"If it isn't true," said Richard, "prove it isn't. Let
Gram go on living her own life. She does a darn good
job of it."
Millicent looked about her wildly. For once, she was
at a loss for words. Jack chuckled. "He's got you this
time, Mill."
"B-b-but," stammered Millicent, "the arrangements
are all made for Mother to come to me." Her eye lighted
on the barrels. "Why, even the dishes are half packed!"
Mrs. Meigs had an inspiration. "I believe I'll give you
those dishes, Millicent. You always did like them; and
being the oldest daughter, I think you have sort of a
claim on them."
"Give me those dishes? But you always said they were
to go to Cecile!" Millicent's eye gleamed, however, and
she hastened to add, "But of course I'll consider them a
trust for Cecile. They will come to her in the end."
"If there's anything left of them by that time!"
thought Mrs. Meigs, remembering the soup tureen. Aloud
she said, "You can take them in my place, Millicent.
Dishes belong on a shelf; but I don't quite feel that
I do."
"I was doing it for your own good, Mother," faltered
Millicent. How often had Mrs. Meigs listened to that
speech about her own good! Only the tense was changed
now. She felt giddy with sudden relief.
"I'm living up to our agreement, Millicent," she said
blandly. "I promised to come to you only if Cecilemarried Roger Kane. I don't want to rub it in, but-"
"You don't need to rub it in," said Richard with a
broad grin. "If you aren't going to have another cup of
coffee, Mom, you'd better let Jack drive you home."
"I think he can take me as far as Edith's," said
Millicent. "I do want to send announcements; and
Edith always knows all about such things."
A minute later Mrs. Meigs, at the front windows,
saw only Richard's rattle-trap where lately had stood a
line of cars that might have been parked before a Chris-
tian Science church. Richard looked over her shoulder,
and remarked, "Kane must have had a key to Cecile's
car. Good thing to get it out of the way."
"Dick, I can't believe that they're all gone!"
"Now don't you wish you'd sent for me earlier?"
teased Richard.
"But suppose they come back when you're not here?"
"Remind Mom what a mess she made of Cecile's
affairs. And don't be too easy with her."
"Dick, I was almost sorry for her this morning. After
all, Millicent always does her duty as she sees it."
"H'm, h'm! Only she sees it cock-eyed." Richard
stretched, and grinned down at Mrs. Meigs. "Well, it's
time I was getting along. How about coming out to
Madison with me, just for the ride? It's a beautiful
day. We always get a few lovely warm days in Novem-
ber, after everything's frozen. Isn't this what they call
'Indian Summer'?"
"So it is. Indian summer.' Do you know, Dick, I
feel as if I were passing into an Indian summer myself?
The leaves are down, and there's been a heavy frost;
but it never seems to me as if any sunshine is quite so
bright as autumn sunshine.""I hope it lasts till Christmas," said Richard. "Well,
get your bonnet on. You can come back by train; or
maybe you can bum a ride back in somebody else's car,
if any one I know is driving in to-day."
"Oh, Dick, I'd love to! But I have so many things
I want to see to to-day."
"What things?" demanded Richard.
Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "Well, I think I'll give a party."
"You can do that any time. You do," teased Richard.
"But you see this is sort of a special party. I'm really
going to stay here in my own home. So I can't very
well do less than give a housewarming."
"You gave a housewarming less than three weeks ago.
Don't you remember, the day I came here to change?"
"I know. But since then I've learned a new informa-
tive double of a two bid. I tried to teach it to my
friends the other day at Mrs. Bishop's. But her daughter
thought it was too exciting for old ladies. If you think
your mother is a nuisance sometimes, you ought to see
Mrs. Bishop's daughter."
Richard grinned from ear to ear. "You look like a
million dollars this morning, Gram. You're an awful
fraud, though. In this very room, last night, you almost
scared me with your 'one foot in the grave' talk."
"Strictly speaking, I suppose I have one foot in the
grave," Mrs. Meigs chuckled. "But so long as I'm able,
I hope to keep the other foot under a card-table."
She walked to the door with him. As he turned to bid
her good-by, Mrs. Meigs stooped and felt for the lump
in her stocking. "You're a dear boy to have taken so
much of your time for me," she said. "Here, buy your-
self a new top or a pair of roller-skates."
"A hundred!" exclaimed Richard. "Sure you didn'tgive me this by mistake for a one? Gee, this will stake
me to a new old car. But Gram, is it safe to carry so
much money that way?"
"Not a bit," said Mrs. Meigs cheerfully. "Money's
never safe until it's spent."
He kissed her on the tip of the nose, and went off
with a wave of his hand. Grandchildren were wonder-
ful. You felt so close to them, closer really than you
did to your own children.
Mrs. Meigs went to the telephone. She called Mrs.
Cronkhite, and Miss Lamb, and Mrs. Bishop. Mrs.
Bishop's voice sounded as if she had been crying. "A
pretty kettle of fish it would be," thought Mrs. Meigs,
"if I'd let myself be moved out of here! Mrs. Bishop
wouldn't have had any place to come."
They could all come that afternoon. Poor things, they
had so few engagements, and her parties were such an
event for them. Mrs. Meigs was the only one of the
four who had a home of her own, and the only one
who had grandchildren.
When that was settled, Mrs. Meigs went out to the
kitchen. "Anna, we're having company this afternoon,"
she said. "The dining-room is in a mess, but it's just my
bridgers, and we always eat in the front room anyhow."
"Your old ladies ain't a mite of trouble," said Anna.
"But we'll be lucky if that Mrs. O'Neil finishes her
breakfast by the time the party gets here. She ain't
appeared yet."
"I suppose Mrs. O'Neil will be looking for a job as
soon as her baby gets big enough," said Mrs. Meigs. "If
she went housekeeping for somebody, she could take the
baby with her. But I'm not at all sure that she's a good
housekeeper, Anna. I was wondering if you could showher a few things. Let her think she's helping you, and
just give her a hint or two as she goes along. You know
all about housekeeping. It would be easy enough for
you."
"Oh, I'll show her fast enough," said Anna. "She's a
nice little thing. Too pretty to have much sense,
though."
"She's had a hard time, we must remember. It will be
fun, showing that baby off to my bridgers. Anna, what
do you think I ought to have for a prize?"
"You gave a big box of candy last time. Let's see.
How about a real nice bottle of perfume?"
"That's a splendid idea," said Mrs. Meigs. "I could
get it at the drug-store, I suppose. But it's such a lovely
morning, I'd be glad of the walk downtown."
"You got plenty of time," agreed Anna. "And you
may as well get out of the house while I clean up."
"Don't touch the best dishes," warned Mrs. Meigs.
"Mrs. Graham is coming to-morrow to pack them."
"Is she? Well," said Anna philosophically, "to-mor-
row's my afternoon off."
Mrs. Meigs put on her bonnet, and looked in her
handbag to be sure she had money enough. As she
stepped into the brilliant morning, she thought sud-
denly of Cal. It had been such a dark day when he
died; it hardly seemed fair to him that she should go
on like this in the sunshine.
She put the thought resolutely away from her. Never
look back! Anyhow she hadn't any time to look back.
A party to-day. She must be sure to see Natalie to-
morrow. Cecile too; maybe she'd ask both Natalie and
Cecile to lunch with her downtown. Funny to think
that Cecile might be included now in married women'sparties! Would either of them have any good ideas on
the subject of Molly?
A friendly stranger had opened the heavy door of
the building for her. From the sidewalk a small boy on a
velocipede waved a greeting. The policeman on the beat
touched his cap. Even the conductor of a passing car
waited with his hand on the bell until she shook her
head at him. You had to be an old lady in a widow's
bonnet to find out how kind people really are.
It was a brilliant day. In summer you expect summer
weather; but Indian summer is a present. Mrs. Meigs
had always loved presents. Holding her small body
erect, and feeling the familiar tug as her widow's veil
blew out behind her in the breeze, she stepped off down
the street.
As she crossed to the sunny side she chuckled to
herself, "I hope by this time Millicent is having a nice
nap!"EEK BOOK
